First Cohomology Groups of Minimal Flows This work was supported by the Slovak Research and Development Agency under the contract APVV-15-0439 and by VEGA 1/0786/15.
Matúš Dirbák

Abstract
Our interest in this work is in group extensions of minimal flows with compact abelian groups in the fibres. We study their structure from categorical and algebraic points of view, and describe relations of their dynamics to the one-dimensional algebraic-topological invariants. We determine the first cohomology groups of flows with simply connected acting groups and those of topologically free flows possessing a free cycle. As an application we show that minimal extensions of these flows not only do exist, but they have a rich algebraic structure.



keywords
Minimal flow, group extension, first cohomology group.

First Cohomology Groups of Minimal Flows

Chapter 1
Introduction

1.1
Minimal flows

The theory of dynamical systems is nowadays a deeply developed field of mathematics, which interacts with many mathematical disciplines. As the field possesses many branches, one encounters several opinions on what the subject of the theory of dynamical systems is (or should be). One point of view is that the theory of dynamical systems is the study of the global properties of groups of transformations. The subject has its roots in celestial and statistical mechanics, where the group is
\mathbb{R}
. In the abstract part of the theory “larger” groups are considered, such as Lie groups or, more generally, locally compact groups and Polish groups.

In this work we are interested in the topological aspect of the theory of dynamical systems, where the main object of study is a flow (or a semiflow). By a
flow
or a
transformation group
or a
(dynamical) system

\mathcal{F}
we mean a representation of a topological (usually locally compact) group
\Gamma
as a group of homeomorphisms on a topological (usually compact or locally compact) space
X
, which is continuous as a mapping
\Gamma\times X\to X
; we write
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
.

One of the most important classes of dynamical systems in this setting is formed by the minimal ones, which were defined for the first time by Birkhoff in
[
4
]
. Recall that a flow
\mathcal{F}
is
minimal
if all its orbits are dense. The importance of minimal flows in topological dynamics is highlighted by the following facts. First, by a Birkhoff’s theorem, every flow on a compact space contains a (closed) minimal subflow. Second, minimal systems are the irreducible dynamical systems in the sense that they are the ones that do not possess any proper (closed) subsystem. Third, minimal systems are often viewed as topological analogues of ergodic systems from ergodic theory, the latter ones being probably the most important systems in the theory of dynamical systems as a whole.

In this work we will be interested mainly in minimal flows with
connected
acting groups, a special attention will be paid to actions of connected Lie groups. (Notice that a minimal flow with a connected acting group has automatically a connected phase space.) In fact, our main results will concern first cohomology groups of minimal flows
\mathcal{F}
of the following two types.

•

The acting group
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{F}
is a connected Lie group and the phase space
X
of
\mathcal{F}
is a compact (connected) manifold.

•

The acting group
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{F}
is a simply connected Lie group (more generally, a simply connected topological group) and the phase space
X
of
\mathcal{F}
is a (not necessarily locally connected) continuum.

In the first of these two situations we shall be particularly interested in flows which are topologically free and/or possess a free cycle (see Subsection
2.5.2
for definitions of these two concepts). In the second situation we shall pay a special attention to flows whose phase spaces
X
have a non-trivial first cohomotopy group
\pi^{1}(X)
. As a matter of fact, the algebraic properties of the one dimensional invariants of the space
X
(the first weak homology group in the first case and the first cohomotopy group in the second case) will occur frequently in our discussions and results on the first cohomology groups of the flow
\mathcal{F}
.

1.2
The structure of minimal flows

It is often the case in mathematics that in order to understand a general object, one tries to express it in terms of special objects, special morphisms and concrete operations. Such is the situation also in the theory of minimal flows. Let us briefly recall several classical results in this direction (for a detailed exposition with some interesting applications, we refer to
[
27
]
). Throughout this whole section we shall assume that phase spaces of the considered flows are compact.

The first result in this direction was obtained by Furstenberg (see
[
23
, Theorem 2.4]
), who showed that every metrizable minimal distal flow can be obtained from the trivial flow by using isometric extensions and inverse limits along segments of countable ordinals. As important corollaries of this structure theorem he proved that every distal flow possesses an invariant probability measure (
[
23
, Theorem 12.3]
) and that no simply connected space supports a minimal distal flow with an abelian locally compact acting group (
[
23
, Theorem 11.1]
). One of the essential tools in Furstenberg’s proof was the enveloping semigroup of a flow, defined by Ellis in
[
11
]
(see also
[
12
]
), which turns out to be a group if the flow under consideration is distal. (For many interesting results on Ellis enveloping semigroups or Ellis actions we refer to
[
15
]
and
[
1
]
.) A few years later after Furstenberg published his structure theorem, a different proof of a strengthening of his result to the class of quasi-separable minimal distal flows was presented by Ellis (see
[
15
, Chapters 13, 15]
), the main ingredient in his new proof being his own discovery of a Galois theory of distal extensions. (For yet another proof of the Furstenberg’s result we refer to
[
10
, Chapter 20]
, where minimal flows are treated as invariant closed equivalence relations on the universal minimal flow.)

Soon after the discovery of the structure of minimal distal flows, an analogous structure theorem was obtained also for metrizable point-distal minimal flows. Under the assumption of residuality of the set of distal points, Veech proved that such systems possess almost automorphic extensions, which can be obtained from the trivial flow by using (proper) isometric extensions, almost automorphic extensions and inverse limits (see
[
54
, Theorem 7.2]
). As was conjectured by Veech and later proved by Ellis, the assumption of residuality of the set of distal points is redundant (see
[
16
]
, in which the Veech structure theorem was extended to the class of quasi-separable point-distal flows). Later Bronšteĭn discovered a structure theorem for minimal prodal flows. He showed that such flows lift across proximal epimorphisms to flows, which can be constructed from the trivial flow by using isometric and proximal extensions and inverse limits (see
[
6
]
).

A structure theorem for general minimal systems was proved by Ellis, Glasner, Shapiro, McMahon and Veech (see
[
17
]
,
[
44
]
,
[
55
]
and
[
26
]
). It involves the notion of PI-towers, relatively incontractible extensions, isometric extensions, proximal extensions, weakly mixing extensions and inverse limits. The picture is more complex than in the structure theorems mentioned above (and we shall not go into details), but it should be noted that some of the earlier structure theorems follow from this general one (this is the case, for instance, with the structure of point-distal flows (see
[
27
, pp. 197–198]
) and the structure of prodal flows (see
[
27
, pp. 198–199]
)).

A structure theorem for minimal normal flows has been proved by Glasner, Mentzen and Siemaszko (see
[
33
]
). They showed that every minimal normal flow lifts across a proximal epimorphism to a minimal normal flow, which descends via the composition of a virtually weakly mixing extension and a group extension to a (minimal) proximal flow. The situation simplifies if the acting group of the flow under consideration is abelian, in which case the normal minimal flow descends onto its maximal Kronecker factor via a virtually weakly mixing extension map.

We conclude our short discussion on the structure of minimal flows by mentioning several results on the structure of minimal tame flows. In
[
29
]
Glasner proved that minimal tame flows with abelian acting groups are PI and have zero topological entropy. Later, Huang, Kerr, Li and Glasner showed that such flows are almost automorphic and uniquely ergodic (see
[
39
]
,
[
42
]
,
[
30
]
). Recently, a structure theorem for minimal tame flows with an
arbitrary
acting group was proved by Glasner (see
[
31
, Theorem 5.3]
). As a corollary of this very general theorem, Glasner proved that the earlier results on minimal tame flows remain true for flows with amenable acting groups (see
[
31
, Corollary 5.4]
). More concretely, every minimal tame flow with an amenable acting group is almost automorphic and uniquely ergodic.

1.3
Cochain complexes associated to a minimal flow

With every (minimal) flow and every abelian topological group one can associate in a natural way a cochain complex and the corresponding cohomology groups. Before describing this construction, let us recall a more general concept, namely the cohomology of (right)
\Gamma
-modules for a given group
\Gamma
. (For further information on the results of this section we refer to
[
51
, Chapter 7]
and
[
52
, Section 1.21]
.)

Let
\Gamma
be a group and
\Lambda
be a right
\Gamma
-module; that is,
\Lambda
is an abelian group, on which
\Gamma
acts from the right by automorphisms. For
n\geq 1
let
F^{n}(\Gamma,\Lambda)
consist of all functions
\Gamma^{n}\to\Lambda
. With operations defined point-wise,
F^{n}(\Gamma,\Lambda)
is an abelian group. Further, let
F^{0}(\Gamma,\Lambda)=\Lambda
and
F^{n}(\Gamma,\Lambda)=0
for
n\leq-1
. Then there is a cochain complex of abelian groups

\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta_{n-2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}F^{n-1}(\Gamma,\Lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta{n-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}F^{n}(\Gamma,\Lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta_{n}}}{{\longrightarrow}}F^{n+1}(\Gamma,\Lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta_{n+1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\dots,

(1.1)

where the morphisms
\delta_{n}
are defined as follows. First, if
n\geq 1
then

\begin{split}(\delta_{n}\varphi)(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n+1})&=\varphi(\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n+1})\gamma_{1}\\
&+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i}\varphi(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{i-1},\gamma_{i+1}\gamma_{i},\gamma_{i+2},\dots,\gamma_{n+1})\\
&+(-1)^{n+1}\varphi(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\end{split}

for all
\varphi\in F^{n}(\Gamma,\Lambda)
and
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n+1}\in\Gamma
. Further, if
n=0
then

(\delta_{0}\lambda)(\gamma)=\lambda\gamma-\lambda

for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Finally, if
n\leq-1
then, of course,
\delta_{n}=0
. The identity
\delta_{n+1}\delta_{n}=0
holds true and (
1.1
) is thus a cochain complex of abelian groups. The cohomology groups of this complex are then defined in the usual way.

Now let
\Gamma
be a topological group,
X
be a topological space and
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow with acting homeomorphisms
T_{\gamma}
(
\gamma\in\Gamma
). Fix an abelian topological group
G
. With operations defined point-wise, the set
\Lambda=C(X,G)
of all continuous maps
\lambda\colon X\to G
is an abelian group, on which the group
\Gamma
acts from the right via
\Lambda\times\Gamma\ni(\lambda,\gamma)\mapsto\lambda T_{\gamma}\in\Lambda
. The corresponding acting transformations are clearly automorphisms of
\Lambda
and so
\Lambda
is a right
\Gamma
-module. The cochain complex (
1.1
) and the morphisms
\delta_{n}
are then defined as above.

Since we are working in the topological category, we want to restrict ourselves to continuous maps. Hence, for
n\geq 1
we consider only those elements
\varphi\in F^{n}(\Gamma,\Lambda)
, for which the map
\Gamma^{n}\times X\ni(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n},x)\mapsto(\varphi(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}))(x)\in G
is continuous. Such maps
\varphi
form a subgroup of
F^{n}(\Gamma,\Lambda)
, which is naturally isomorphic to the group
C^{n}(\mathcal{F},G)
of all continuous maps
\Gamma^{n}\times X\to G
. This identification leads to a cochain complex obtained by restricting the one in (
1.1
)

\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta_{n-2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C^{n-1}(\mathcal{F},G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta_{n-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C^{n}(\mathcal{F},G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta_{n}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C^{n+1}(\mathcal{F},G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta_{n+1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\dots,

(1.2)

where
C^{0}(\mathcal{F},G)=C(X,G)
,
C^{n}(\mathcal{F},G)=0
for
n\leq-1
and the morphisms
\delta_{n}
are defined as follows. First, if
n\geq 1
then

\begin{split}(\delta_{n}f)(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n+1},x)&=f(\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n+1},T_{\gamma_{1}}x)\\
&+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i}f(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{i-1},\gamma_{i+1}\gamma_{i},\gamma_{i+2},\dots,\gamma_{n+1},x)\\
&+(-1)^{n+1}f(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n},x)\end{split}

for all
f\in C^{n}(\mathcal{F},G)
,
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n+1}\in\Gamma
and
x\in X
. Further, if
n=0
then

(\delta_{0}f)(\gamma,x)=f(T_{\gamma}x)-f(x)

for all
f\in C^{0}(\mathcal{F},G)
,
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
x\in X
. Finally, if
n\leq-1
then, of course,
\delta_{n}=0
.

1.4
Cohomology groups of a minimal flow

Given the cochain complex (
1.2
) and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, one can consider the group of
n
-cocycles

Z^{n}(\mathcal{F},G)=\ker(\delta_{n})
, the group of
n
-coboundaries

B^{n}(\mathcal{F},G)=\mathop{\rm im}(\delta_{n-1})
and the corresponding
n^{\text{th}}
cohomology group

H^{n}(\mathcal{F},G)=Z^{n}(\mathcal{F},G)/B^{n}(\mathcal{F},G).

A map
f\in C^{1}(\mathcal{F},G)
is a
1
-cocycle if and only if it satisfies the identity

f(\alpha,T_{\beta}x)-f(\alpha\beta,x)+f(\beta,x)=0.

(1.3)

Moreover, the
1
-coboundaries are the maps
f\in C^{1}(\mathcal{F},G)
expressible in the form

f(\gamma,x)=g(T_{\gamma}x)-g(x)

(1.4)

for an appropriate
g\in C^{0}(\mathcal{F},G)
, the map
g
being called a
transfer function
of
f
. (Let us mention that a transfer function of a given
1
-coboundary
f
is not unique in general, but if the flow
\mathcal{F}
is minimal then a transfer function of
f
is unique up to an additive constant.)

We have, obviously,
H^{n}(\mathcal{F},G)=0
for every
n\leq-1
. The
0^{\text{th}}
cohomology group
H^{0}(\mathcal{F},G)
is also determined easily. Indeed, by minimality of
\mathcal{F}
, the kernel
\ker(\delta_{0})
of
\delta_{0}
consists of the constant maps
X\to G
and hence is isomorphic to
G
. Thus, since
\mathop{\rm im}(\delta_{-1})=0
,

H^{0}(\mathcal{F},G)=\ker(\delta_{0})/\mathop{\rm im}(\delta_{-1})\cong\ker(\delta_{0})\cong G.

Naturally, it is much more difficult to determine the higher-dimensional cohomology groups
H^{n}(\mathcal{F},G)
(
n\geq 1
), already the case
n=1
being highly non-trivial. In fact, it is one of the main aims of our work to determine the first cohomology group
H^{1}(\mathcal{F},G)
in the case when
G
is a
compact
(connected) abelian group. One motivation for the study of this “compact case” comes from the theory of topological groups (
[
38
, p. 369]
):

“Compact abelian groups form the most important class of abelian topological

groups,as is evidenced by the considerable literature on them and their applications.”

As a matter of fact, compact abelian groups have their honored place in the category of all compact groups (
[
38
, p. 447]
):

“So it emerges that compact abelian groups are not simply examples of compact groups but basic ingredients of the structure of all compact groups.”

To illustrate this statement, recall the following results from the structure theory of compact groups.

•

Every compact connected group is a (topological) semi-direct product
G\ltimes H
of an abelian compact connected group
G
and a semisimple compact connected group
H
(see
[
38
, Theorem 9.39(i), p. 478]
).

•

Every compact connected group is a union of its closed connected abelian subgroups (see
[
38
, Theorem 9.32(ii), p. 473]
).

•

The first Betti number
\beta_{1}(K)
of a compact connected Lie group
K
coincides with the topological dimension
\dim(K/K^{\prime})
of the quotient group of
K
modulo the commutator subgroup
K^{\prime}
of
K
and this quotient group is a torus; this follows from the second structure theorem for connected compact Lie groups (see
[
38
, Theorem 6.41(i), p. 221]
) and from the Weyl’s theorem (see
[
5
, Corollary 4, p. 285]
).

Since we shall deal only with the one-dimensional cohomology in this work, let us adopt the following notation for the corresponding groups:

\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)=Z^{1}(\mathcal{F},G),\hskip 8.53581pt\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)=B^{1}(\mathcal{F},G),\hskip 8.53581pt\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)=H^{1}(\mathcal{F},G).

Finally, to simplify our notation even more, we shall write

\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}=\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{1}),\hskip 8.53581pt\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}=\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{1}),\hskip 8.53581pt\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}=\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{1}),

where
\mathbb{T}^{1}
stands for the circle group.

1.5
Dynamical interpretation of the first cohomology

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow with acting homeomorphisms
T_{\gamma}
(
\gamma\in\Gamma
),
G
be an abelian topological group and
\mathcal{C}\colon\Gamma\times X\to G
be a continuous map. Given
\gamma\in\Gamma
, consider the map

\widetilde{T}_{\gamma}\colon X\times G\ni(x,g)\mapsto(T_{\gamma}x,\mathcal{C}(\gamma,x)+g)\in X\times G.

Clearly, all
\widetilde{T}_{\gamma}
are homeomorphisms on
X\times G
. Moreover, they are acting homeomorphisms of a flow
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X\times G
if and only if

\mathcal{C}(\alpha,T_{\beta}x)+\mathcal{C}(\beta,x)=\mathcal{C}(\alpha\beta,x)

for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma
and
x\in X
, that is, if and only if
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)=Z^{1}(\mathcal{F},G)
is a
1
-cocycle of the cochain complex (
1.2
). If this is the case then we call the flow
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
(or the
1
-cocycle
\mathcal{C}
) a
group extension of
\mathcal{F}
.

Further, if
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)=B^{1}(\mathcal{F},G)
is a
1
-coboundary with a transfer function
\xi\colon X\to G
then the orbit closures of the flow
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
are the vertical translations of the graph of
\xi
in
X\times G
and hence they form a decomposition of
X\times G
into closed
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
-invariant sets, on each of which the corresponding restriction of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{F}
. Therefore, we may think of the flows
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
with
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
as trivial group extensions of
\mathcal{F}
and we are led, also from the dynamical point of view, to focus our attention on the first cohomology group

\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)=\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)/\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)=Z^{1}(\mathcal{F},G)/B^{1}(\mathcal{F},G)=H^{1}(\mathcal{F},G).

There is a standard and commonly used approach to the study of group extensions in the topological setting, namely by means of vertical sections of orbit closures; let us recall it briefly (for more details see Section
3.2
). Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow with acting homeomorphisms
T_{\gamma}
(
\gamma\in\Gamma
),
G
be a
compact
abelian group and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
be a group extension of
\mathcal{F}
. Choose a base point
z
for
X
and let

F(\mathcal{C})=\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,0)|_{z}

be the vertical
z
-section of the orbit closure
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,0)
of
(z,0)
in
X\times G
under the action of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
. Then
g\in G
is an element of
F(\mathcal{C})
if and only if there is a net
(\gamma_{i})
in
\Gamma
with
T_{\gamma_{i}}z\to z
in
X
and
\mathcal{C}(\gamma_{i},z)\to g
in
G
. The set
F(\mathcal{C})
is a closed subgroup of
G
and it does not depend on the choice of a base point
z
for
X
. Moreover, the group
F(\mathcal{C})
is a cohomology invariant of
\mathcal{C}
and it can be used to reconstruct the whole orbit closure of
(z,0)
. Indeed, we have

\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,0)=\overline{\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}T_{\gamma}z\times\left(\mathcal{C}(\gamma,z)+F(\mathcal{C})\right)}

and every vertical section of
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,0)
is an element of the quotient group
G/F(\mathcal{C})
. Interestingly,
F
can be viewed as a covariant functor from the category
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
of all group extensions of
\mathcal{F}
with compact abelian fibre groups into the category
\mathsf{CAGp}
of all compact abelian groups. It detects coboundaries as well as minimal extensions and is continuous in the sense that it preserves limits of inverse systems. The functor
F
will be one of the main tools for our study of group extensions in this work.

Despite a formal analogy of minimal systems with the ergodic ones, there are some essential differences between them. One of the most important distinctions lies in the fact that in topological dynamics there is no analogue of the notion of ergodic decomposition from ergodic theory. Though (compact) topological systems from certain classes do admit a decomposition into minimal subsystems (in other words, are point-wise almost periodic or semi-simple), the phase space of a general topological system does not decompose into its minimal subsystems. The class of point-wise almost periodic systems includes, for instance,

•

distal systems (see
[
11
, Theorem 1]
),

•

products of distal and point-wise almost periodic systems (see
[
2
, Corollary 16, p. 90]
),

•

distal extensions of point-wise almost periodic systems (see
[
2
, p. 93]
).

Independently on whether or not the phase space
X
of a minimal flow
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
is compact, group extensions of
\mathcal{F}
always decompose into minimal subsystems. Indeed, if
G
is a compact abelian group and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is a group extension of
\mathcal{F}
then all the orbit closures
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(x,g)
of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
(
x\in X
,
g\in G
) are minimal sets for
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
with full projections onto
X
, they form a decomposition of the space
X\times G
and are permuted by the vertical rotations of
X\times G
. In particular, by our discussion from the preceding paragraph, minimality of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
reduces to the equality
F(\mathcal{C})=G
.

1.6
Minimal group extensions

Following our setting from Section
1.3
, we are interested in group extensions of a minimal flow
\mathcal{F}
with
abelian
fibre groups
G
. In the theory of dynamical systems it is customary (and natural from various viewpoints) to restrict to locally compact groups
G
(although extensions with other types of fibre groups
G
, Polish in particular, are also of an importance). In topological dynamics there is a considerable difference between the compact and the non-compact case—different phenomena occur in these two situations and different techniques are used in their study. One of these phenomena is the (non-)existence of minimal group extensions; to illustrate this statement, let us recall the following results.

•

If
\mathcal{F}
is a minimal
\mathbb{Z}
-flow on a compact metrizable space then the group extension
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
of
\mathcal{F}
is non-minimal for every
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
; this follows basically from
[
3
, Section 1]
(see also
[
45
, Section 2]
).

•

If
\mathcal{F}
is a free minimal
\mathbb{Z}
-flow (or
\mathbb{R}
-flow) on a compact metrizable space and
G
is a compact connected second countable abelian group then there is
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
such that the group extension
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
of
\mathcal{F}
is minimal (see, e.g.,
[
22
]
,
[
14
]
,
[
48
]
,
[
40
]
,
[
43
]
).

The study of minimal group extensions of a given minimal flow
\mathcal{F}
is motivated also by the following problem, which is one of the central problems in the topological theory of dynamical systems.

(
\mathcal{P}
)

Given a locally compact group
\Gamma
, describe the class
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
of all compact (metrizable) spaces
Y
, on which the group
\Gamma
acts in a (free) minimal way.

If our space
Y
under consideration has the form of a direct product
Y=X\times Z
with
X\in\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
then one can try to construct a minimal
\Gamma
-flow on
Y
in the form of a skew product over a minimal
\Gamma
-flow on
X
. This approach turns out to be useful for two reasons. First, because of their special form, skew products on
Y
are easier to handle and understand than general flows on
Y
. Second, the space
Y
often admits a minimal
\Gamma
-flow in the form of a skew product already under mild assumptions on the fibre
Z
; to illustrate this statement, let us recall the following results.

•

If a compact metrizable space
Z
admits a minimal action of a Polish group with a dense arc-wise connected subgroup then every minimal
\mathbb{Z}
-flow
\mathcal{F}
on an infinite compact metrizable space can be extended to a minimal skew product with the fibre
Z
(see
[
34
, Section 1]
).

•

The above mentioned result holds true also for free minimal actions of locally compact non-compact second countable amenable groups (see
[
9
, Section 4]
).

These and similar results can help us gain some insight into the structure of the classes
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
.

•

If
\Gamma
is a locally compact non-compact second countable amenable group then the class
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
is closed with respect to direct products with the following spaces: compact connected manifolds without boundary, compact connected Hilbert cube manifolds, homogeneous spaces of compact connected metrizable groups, compact metrizable spaces admitting a minimal homeomorphism isotopic to the identity, and others (see
[
9
, Section 4]
and references therein).

•

If
\Gamma
is a locally compact non-compact second countable amenable group possessing a dense arc-wise connected subgroup (say, an amenable connected non-compact Lie group) then the class
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
is closed with respect to (at most) countable direct products (see
[
9
, Section 5]
).

Now, if our fibre is a compact abelian group
Z=G
then it is natural to exploit its additional algebraic structure and to try to construct a minimal skew product on
Y=X\times G
in the form of a group extension of
\mathcal{F}
. For flows with amenable acting groups
\Gamma
, the existence of minimal group extensions follows from
[
9
, Theorem 8]
. As the amenability assumption is used heavily in the proof of the mentioned result, it is unlikely that the techniques used therein can be modified in such a way that they apply also to flows with non-amenable acting groups. Therefore, in the present work we aim at finding techniques that would yield minimal group extensions of
\mathcal{F}
regardless of whether or not the acting group
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{F}
is amenable.

1.7
Minimal flows and one-dimensional invariants

Let
X
be a (Hausdorff) topological space and
\Gamma
be an abelian topological group. If
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
is a minimal flow with acting homeomorphisms
T_{\gamma}
(
\gamma\in\Gamma
) and
T_{\gamma}(x)=x
for some
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
x\in X
then
T_{\gamma}
is the identity on
X
, because the set of fixed points of
T_{\gamma}
is closed and, by commutativity of
\Gamma
, also
\mathcal{F}
-invariant; in other words, if
\Gamma
acts on
X
in an effective and minimal way then the action is free (see, e.g.,
[
2
, p. 23]
). Thus, an elementary obstruction to the existence of a minimal
\Gamma
-flow on
X
is a fixed point property of
X
. Since the fixed point property of a space is often detected by algebraic-topological methods, one can try to employ techniques of algebraic topology to the study of minimal flows, particularly to their (non-)existence on given spaces. A famous sufficient condition for the existence of a fixed point is the Lefschetz theorem (see, e.g.,
[
36
, Theorem 2C.3, p. 179–181]
), which states that a continuous map on a finite polyhedron
X
with non-zero Euler characteristic
\chi(X)
possesses a fixed point, provided it is homotopic to the identity. This theorem has the following (well known) immediate implication on minimality: if an abelian arc-wise connected group
\Gamma
acts in a minimal way on a (non-trivial) finite polyhedron
X
then
\chi(X)=0
.

It was believed for some time (see, e.g.,
[
8
]
,
[
35
]
,
[
53
, Problems, p. 515]
) that the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X)
of a compact connected manifold
X
can carry some information about its (non-)minimality. Specifically, it was conjectured that a simply connected compact manifold
X
can not support a minimal continuous flow, that is, a minimal action
\mathbb{R}\curvearrowright X
. This conjecture was partially supported by a theorem of Furstenberg (see
[
23
, Theorem 11.1]
), who showed that a simply connected compact metric space can not admit a
distal
minimal action of an abelian locally compact group. However, the conjecture turned out to be false. Indeed, for every smooth compact connected manifold without boundary
Y
, the product
X=\mathbb{S}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}\times Y
supports a (smooth) minimal flow
\mathbb{R}\curvearrowright X
; this follows from a result of Fathi and Herman (see
[
18
, Théorème 2]
). Now, since a smooth compact connected manifold without boundary
Y
may have an arbitrary finitely generated group as its fundamental group
\pi_{1}(Y)
already in dimension
4
, the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X)
of a compact manifold
X
admitting a minimal flow
\mathbb{R}\curvearrowright X
can be an arbitrary finitely generated group (in particular, it can be trivial) for instance in dimension
10
.

The situation does not improve if we take general continua
X
and their first cohomotopy (i.e., first Čech cohomology) groups
\pi^{1}(X)
into consideration: given an arbitrary torsion-free abelian group
A
with
\mathop{\rm rank}(A)\leq\mathfrak{c}
, there is a continuum
X
having
A
as its first cohomotopy group,
\pi^{1}(X)\cong A
, and supporting a minimal flow
\mathbb{R}\curvearrowright X
. Indeed, it is sufficient to take
X=A^{*}
, the Pontryagin dual of the discrete group
A
. Clearly, the group
X
has a dense one-parameter subgroup
q\colon\mathbb{R}\to X
, which gives rise to a minimal flow
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathbb{R}\times X\ni(t,x)\mapsto q(t)x\in X
. Moreover, there are isomorphisms of groups
\pi^{1}(X)\cong X^{*}\cong A^{**}\cong A
(see
[
38
, Theorem 8.57, p. 420]
or our Subsection
2.4.10
).

Now let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow,
G
be a compact abelian group and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
be a group extension of
\mathcal{F}
. By commutativity of the group
G
, all its irreducible unitary representations are of dimension one and so the one-dimensional representations of
G
(that is, the characters
\chi\in G^{*}
) carry all the information about
G
. Since the flow
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
induced by
\mathcal{C}
on
X\times G
respects the group structure of
G
to a large extent, it seems reasonable to expect that there might exist deeper links between the dynamics of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
on one side and the structure of the
one-dimensional
algebraic-topological invariants of the space
X
on the other side. We are looking for such links in the present work, concentrating on flows with the following two types of phase spaces
X
.

•

The space
X
is a compact connected manifold. In this case we would like to find a relation of the dynamics of group extensions of
\mathcal{F}
to the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X)
(or the first homology group
H_{1}(X)
) of
X
.

•

The space
X
is an arbitrary (not necessarily locally connected) compact connected space. In this case we are searching for links between the dynamics of group extensions of
\mathcal{F}
and the first cohomotopy (that is, the first Čech cohomology) group
\pi^{1}(X)
of
X
.

Let us describe one situation in support of our conjecture from the previous paragraph. Let
\Gamma
be a connected (Lie) group,
X
be a compact manifold and
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow. Fix a compact abelian group
G
and a
finite
subgroup
H
of
G
. Given the existence of a group extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
of
\mathcal{F}
with
F(\mathcal{C})=H
, can we find a relation between
H
and
\pi_{1}(X)
? To this end, choose a base point
z
for
X
and write
M=\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,0)
for the orbit closure of
(z,0)
in
X\times G
under the action of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
. By our discussion from Section
1.5
,
M
is a minimal set for
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
, hence it is connected by connectedness of
\Gamma
and compact by compactness of
X
and
G
. Further, the group
H
acts freely on
M
by vertical rotations and the corresponding homogeneous space
M/H
is homeomorphic to
X
. Thus, it follows that
H
is a quotient group of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X)
of
X
and, by commutativity of
H
, also of the first homology group
H_{1}(X)
of
X
(see, e.g.,
[
47
, Chapter 13, § 81]
). In particular, if the group
H_{1}(X)
is torsion-free then the number of elementary divisors of
H
does not exceed the Betti number (that is, the rank) of
H_{1}(X)
.

1.8
Free group extensions

Let
\mathsf{Cat}
be a category and
K
be an object of
\mathsf{Cat}
. We shall call
K
a
free object
for
\mathsf{Cat}
if the set
\mathop{\rm Hom}(K,L)
of morphisms
K\to L
is a singleton for every object
L
of
\mathsf{Cat}
. Clearly, if a free object of
\mathsf{Cat}
exists then it is unique up to an isomorphism.

Now let
\mathsf{Cat}
be a category,
\mathsf{Tp}
be a subcategory of the category
\mathsf{Top}
of topological spaces and
\phi\colon\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Tp}
be a covariant functor. Given a topological space
X
from
\mathsf{Tp}
, consider the category
\mathsf{Map}_{\phi}(X,\mathsf{Cat})
defined as follows:

\centerdot

the objects of
\mathsf{Map}_{\phi}(X,\mathsf{Cat})
are the pairs
(B,\psi)
, where
B
is an object of
\mathsf{Cat}
and
\psi\colon X\to\phi(B)
is a morphism in
\mathsf{Tp}
,

\centerdot

given objects
(B,\psi)
,
(C,\chi)
in
\mathsf{Map}_{\phi}(X,\mathsf{Cat})
, a morphism
k\colon(B,\psi)\to(C,\chi)
is a morphism
k\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(B,C)
in
\mathsf{Cat}
with
\phi(k)\psi=\chi
,

\centerdot

the composition of two morphisms in
\mathsf{Map}_{\phi}(X,\mathsf{Cat})
is defined as their composition in
\mathsf{Cat}
and the identity on
(B,\psi)
is the identity on
B
in
\mathsf{Cat}
.

In this way
\mathsf{Map}_{\phi}(X,\mathsf{Cat})
becomes a category in its own right. A free object for the category
\mathsf{Map}_{\phi}(X,\mathsf{Cat})
consists of an object
A
of
\mathsf{Cat}
and a morphism
\varphi\colon X\to\phi(A)
in
\mathsf{Tp}
such that for every object
B
of
\mathsf{Cat}
and every morphism
\psi\colon X\to\phi(B)
in
\mathsf{Tp}
there is a unique morphism
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
in
\mathsf{Cat}
with
\phi(h)\varphi=\psi
; see Figure
1.1
. The same construction works if
\mathsf{Tp}
is a subcategory of the category
\mathsf{PtTop}
of pointed topological spaces and base point preserving maps; in this situation we assume that every topological group carries its identity as the base point.

\begin{CD}X@>{\varphi}>{}>\phi(A)\hskip 28.45274ptA\\
\Big\|@V{}V{\phi(h)}V\hskip 28.45274pt@V{}V{h}V\\
X@>{}>{\psi}>\phi(B)\hskip 28.45274ptB\end{CD}

Figure 1.1:
Free object
(A,\varphi)
for the category
\mathsf{Map}_{\phi}(X,\mathsf{Cat})

Let us recall some well-known special cases of the construction described above.

•

The Stone-Čech compactification of a space
X
.
In this case
\mathsf{Cat}=\mathsf{CpTop}
is the category of compact topological spaces,
\mathsf{Tp}=\mathsf{Top}
is the category of topological spaces,
\phi
is the inclusion functor
\mathsf{CpTop}\to\mathsf{Top}
and
X
is a Tychonov space (see, e.g.,
[
41
, pp. 152–154]
).

•

The Bohr compactification of a group
X
.
In this case
\mathsf{Cat}=\mathsf{CAGp}
is the category of compact abelian groups,
\mathsf{Tp}=\mathsf{LCAGp}
is the category of locally compact abelian groups,
\phi
is the inclusion functor
\mathsf{CAGp}\to\mathsf{LCAGp}
and
X
is a locally compact abelian group (see, e.g.,
[
19
, Section 4.7]
). (Some properties of the Bohr compactification are summarized also in our Subsection
2.4.6
.)

•

The free compact abelian group over a space
X
.
In this case
\mathsf{Cat}=\mathsf{CAGp}
is the category of compact abelian groups,
\mathsf{Tp}=\mathsf{PtTop}
is the category of pointed topological spaces,
\phi
is the functor
\mathsf{CAGp}\to\mathsf{PtTop}
forgetting the algebraic structure and
X
is a pointed topological space (see
[
38
, Chapter 8, p. 415]
). (Some properties of free compact abelian groups are summarized also in our Subsection
2.4.11
.)

•

The free compact group over a space
X
.
In this case
\mathsf{Cat}=\mathsf{CGp}
is the category of compact groups,
\mathsf{Tp}=\mathsf{PtTop}
is the category of pointed topological spaces,
\phi
is the functor
\mathsf{CGp}\to\mathsf{PtTop}
forgetting the algebraic structure and
X
is a pointed topological space (see
[
38
, Chapter 11, pp. 577–578]
).

Now let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow,
\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}}
be a compact abelian group and
\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}})
be a group extension of
\mathcal{F}
. Following the ideas from the preceding paragraphs, we call
(\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}},\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}})
a
free group extension of
\mathcal{F}
if for every compact abelian group
G
and every group extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
of
\mathcal{F}
there is a unique topological morphism
h_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}}\to G
with
h_{\mathcal{C}}\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}}=\mathcal{C}
; see Figure
1.2
.

\begin{CD}\Gamma\times X@>{\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}}}>{}>\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}}\\
\Big\|@V{}V{h_{\mathcal{C}}}V\hskip 28.45274pt@V{}V{h_{\mathcal{C}}}V\\
\Gamma\times X@>{}>{\mathcal{C}}>G\hskip 28.45274ptG\end{CD}

Figure 1.2:
Free group extension
(\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}},\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}})
of
\mathcal{F}

One of our aims in this work is to construct a free group extension of an arbitrary minimal flow
\mathcal{F}
. Intuitively, a free group extension of
\mathcal{F}
should contain in itself encoded information about all group extensions of
\mathcal{F}
and we therefore expect to be able to express the most important properties of group extensions of
\mathcal{F}
in terms of the free group extension. In particular, we wish to find an explicit relation between
\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}}
and
F(\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}})
on one side and the groups
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
,
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
on the other side, and express the group
\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}}
in terms of familiar compact abelian groups.

Important categories arising in topological dynamics fail to possess free objects, but turn out to possess objects universal in a weaker sense. To give an example, let
\Gamma
be a topological group and
\mathsf{MinFlow}_{\Gamma}
be the category of all minimal
\Gamma
-flows with compact phase spaces; notice that by minimality and compactness assumptions, every morphism in
\mathsf{MinFlow}_{\Gamma}
is an epimorphism. By a theorem of Ellis from
[
13
]
(see also
[
15
, Proposition 7.13, p. 62]
), the category
\mathsf{MinFlow}_{\Gamma}
possesses an object
\mathcal{F}_{u}
such that the set of morphisms
\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{F}_{u},\mathcal{F})
is non-empty for every object
\mathcal{F}
in
\mathsf{MinFlow}_{\Gamma}
. Traditionally,
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is referred to as a
universal minimal
\Gamma
-flow
; it is unique up to an isomorphism (see
[
15
, Corollary 7.16, p. 62]
). (For many other examples of categories in topological dynamics that possess a universal object, we refer to
[
25
]
and
[
15
, Chapter 7]
.)

A universal minimal
\Gamma
-flow
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is not a free object for the category
\mathsf{MinFlow}_{\Gamma}
as defined at the beginning of this section. Indeed, the set of morphisms
\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{F}_{u},\mathcal{F}_{u})
, which coincides with the group
\text{Aut}(\mathcal{F}_{u})
of automorphisms of
\mathcal{F}_{u}
by coalescence of
\mathcal{F}_{u}
(see
[
2
, pp. 115–116]
), is far from being a singleton. On the contrary, if
(x,y)
is an arbitrary element of a minimal set for the product
\Gamma
-flow
\mathcal{F}_{u}\times\mathcal{F}_{u}
then there is
h\in\text{Aut}(\mathcal{F}_{u})
with
h(x)=y
; the converse statement is also true: if
h\in\text{Aut}(\mathcal{F}_{u})
then
(x,h(x))
is necessarily an element of a minimal set for
\mathcal{F}_{u}\times\mathcal{F}_{u}
for every
x
from the base space of
\mathcal{F}_{u}
(indeed, since the map
z\mapsto(z,h(z))
is an (injective) morphism of flows
\mathcal{F}_{u}\to\mathcal{F}_{u}\times\mathcal{F}_{u}
, its image, that is, the graph of
h
, is a minimal set of
\mathcal{F}_{u}\times\mathcal{F}_{u}
). Thus, since
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is the only possible candidate for a free object of
\mathsf{MinFlow}_{\Gamma}
, it follows that
\mathsf{MinFlow}_{\Gamma}
fails to possess a free object in general.

Now, given a minimal flow
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
, we are interested in the existence of a universal object for the category of all
minimal
group extensions of
\mathcal{F}
. The universality is now understood in the sense that every minimal group extension of
\mathcal{F}
is an epimorphic image of the universal one. To be more precise, let
\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}^{m}}
be a compact abelian group and
\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}^{m}}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}^{m}})
be a minimal group extension of
\mathcal{F}
. We call
(\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}^{m}},\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}^{m}})
a
universal minimal group extension of
\mathcal{F}
if for every compact abelian group
G
and every minimal group extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
of
\mathcal{F}
there is a topological morphism
q\colon\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}^{m}}\to G
with
q\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}^{m}}=\mathcal{C}
; see Figure
1.3
.

\begin{CD}\Gamma\times X@>{\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}^{m}}}>{}>\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}^{m}}\hskip 28.45274pt\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}^{m}}\\
\Big\|@V{}V{q}V\hskip 28.45274pt@V{}V{q}V\\
\Gamma\times X@>{}>{\mathcal{C}}>G\hskip 28.45274ptG\end{CD}

Figure 1.3:
Universal minimal group extension
(\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}^{m}},\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}^{m}})
of
\mathcal{F}

Notice that, by minimality and compactness assumptions, such a morphism
q
is necessarily an epimorphism of groups. Even more importantly, such a morphism
q
is unique (this follows, for instance, from Lemma
4.18
(i) and Remark
4.19
in Section
4.4
); consequently, if a universal minimal group extension
(\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}^{m}},\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}^{m}})
of
\mathcal{F}
does exist then it is automatically a
free
object for the category of all minimal group extensions of
\mathcal{F}
. One of our aims in this work is to find out whether or not this universal/free minimal group extension of
\mathcal{F}
exists. Contrary to the situation of all group extensions of
\mathcal{F}
, this turns out not to be the case.

Finally, we wish to mention that a notion of a universal minimal group extension of a minimal flow
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
has been defined and studied by Glasner (see
[
25
, Section 8.1]
). This concept is, however, not to be confused with the one used in this work. In fact, while a universal minimal group extension in the sense of Glasner exists for every minimal flow
\mathcal{F}
(and coincides with the universal minimal almost periodic extension of
\mathcal{F}
), a universal minimal group extension in our sense (which has been defined in analogy to the notion of a free group extension of
\mathcal{F}
) typically does not exist. On the other hand, if
\mathcal{M}
is an arbitrary minimal subflow of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}}}
(that is, if
\mathcal{M}
is a restriction of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}}}
onto one of its orbit closures) then every minimal group extension
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
of
\mathcal{F}
is an epimorphic image of
\mathcal{M}
; indeed, if
h_{\mathcal{C}}
is the unique morphism
\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}}\to\mathcal{C}
then
\mathop{\rm id}_{X}\times h_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
is an epimorphism of flows. (Let us mention that
\mathcal{M}
is not a group extension of
\mathcal{F}
in the sense used in this work, though it is a group extension of
\mathcal{F}
in the sense used in
[
25
]
.)

1.9
Torsions in cohomology groups

Let
A
be an abelian group and
\mathop{\rm tor}(A)
be the set of all elements of
A
of a finite order. Then
\mathop{\rm tor}(A)
forms a pure subgroup of
A
, traditionally called the
torsion subgroup of
A
, with a torsion-free quotient group
C=A/\mathop{\rm tor}(A)
. The torsion subgroup
\mathop{\rm tor}(A)
of
A
is one of the most important objects associated to
A
, since (
[
21
, p. 35]
)

“
… the structure theory of abelian groups splits into the theories of torsion and torsion-free groups, and investigations of how these are glued together to form mixed groups.
”

To illustrate this, recall that there is an isomorphism
A\cong C\times_{\varphi}\mathop{\rm tor}(A)
, where
\varphi\in Z^{2}(C,\mathop{\rm tor}(A))
is an appropriate symmetric
2
-cocycle (see
[
21
, Chapter 9]
or our Subsections
2.2.7
and
2.2.8
). The simplest situation occurs when
\varphi
is a
2
-coboundary,
\varphi\in B^{2}(C,\mathop{\rm tor}(A))
, in which case
\mathop{\rm tor}(A)
is a direct summand in
A
, that is,
A=\mathop{\rm tor}(A)\oplus C
. This is the case, for instance, in the following situations.

•

The group
A
is divisible. In this case
\mathop{\rm tor}(A)
is also a divisible group and so it is a direct summand in
A
by a theorem of Baer (see
[
21
, Theorem 21.2, p. 100]
).

•

The group
A
is finitely generated. In this case
\mathop{\rm tor}(A)
is a direct sum of finitely many finite cyclic groups and it is a direct summand in
A
by the fundamental theorem on finitely generated abelian groups (see, e.g.,
[
21
, Theorem 15.5, p. 79]
).

•

The torsion subgroup
\mathop{\rm tor}(A)
of
A
is of a bounded order. In this case
\mathop{\rm tor}(A)
is a direct summand in
A
by a theorem of Kulikov, which asserts that a pure subgroup of a bounded order is necessarily a direct summand (see
[
21
, Theorem 27.5, p. 118]
).

When abelian groups are assigned to other mathematical objects, the torsion subgroups of these groups are usually of more than merely a group-theoretical interest. Moreover, the torsion elements of these groups often correspond to elements in the studied objects with some special features. Let us give some examples of this phenomenon.

•

If
G
is a compact abelian group then the torsion subgroup
\mathop{\rm tor}(G^{*})
of the Pontryagin dual
G^{*}
of
G
coincides with the annihilator
(G_{0})^{\perp}
of the identity component
G_{0}
of
G
in
G^{*}
; in particular, the group
G
is connected (respectively, totally disconnected) if and only if the group
G^{*}
is torsion-free (respectively, a torsion group) (see, e.g.,
[
38
, Theorem 8.4, p. 371]
).

•

A compact connected Lie group
G
is semi-simple if and only if its fundamental group
\pi_{1}(G)
(that is, its first homology group
H_{1}(G)
) is a torsion group (see
[
5
, Corollary 4, p. 285]
). Moreover,
\mathop{\rm tor}(\pi_{1}(G))
is isomorphic to a finite central subgroup of the universal cover
\widetilde{G}
of
G
.

•

A closed connected surface
X
is orientable if and only if the torsion subgroup
\mathop{\rm tor}(H_{1}(X))
of the first homology group
H_{1}(X)
of
X
vanishes (see, e.g.,
[
47
, Chapter 12]
).

In this context we also wish to mention a recent work of Glasner and Host (see
[
32
]
) in which a remarkable link was found between torsions and group extensions in the theory of dimension groups of minimal
\mathbb{Z}
-flows on the Cantor space. (For necessary definitions and notation we refer the reader to
[
32
]
. We also mention that the notion of a group extension in
[
32
]
is understood in the sense of
[
25
]
; see our discussion towards the end of Section
1.8
.)

•

Let
\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2}
be minimal
\mathbb{Z}
-flows on the Cantor space and let
p\colon\mathcal{F}_{1}\to\mathcal{F}_{2}
be an epimorphism. Denote by
p^{*}\colon K_{0}(\mathcal{F}_{2})\to K_{0}(\mathcal{F}_{1})
the induced morphism between the associated dimension groups of
\mathcal{F}_{2}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}
, respectively. Then, as was shown in
[
32
]
, there is a one-to-one correspondence between elements of the quotient group
K_{0}(\mathcal{F}_{1})/p^{*}K_{0}(\mathcal{F}_{2})
and pairs of epimorphisms
\mathcal{F}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{F}_{2}
such that
p=q\circ r
and
q
is a group extension by a finite abelian group. Moreover, if
H=\mathop{\rm tor}(K_{0}(\mathcal{F}_{1})/p^{*}K_{0}(\mathcal{F}_{2}))
is the (discrete) torsion group of
K_{0}(\mathcal{F}_{1})/p^{*}K_{0}(\mathcal{F}_{2})
and
K=H^{*}
is the Pontryagin dual of
H
then there is a pair of epimorphisms
\mathcal{F}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r_{u}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{F}_{u}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q_{u}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{F}_{2}
such that
p=q_{u}\circ r_{u}
and
q_{u}
is a group extension by
K
. For further properties of
q_{u}
and
\mathcal{F}_{u}
and for other related results, we refer to
[
32
, Theorems 2.1, 2.3]
.

Now let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow. In the present work we are searching for a dynamical interpretation of the torsion elements in the cohomology group
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
of
\mathcal{F}
, as well as for a relation between the structure of the torsion subgroup
\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})
of
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
on one side and the intrinsic properties of
\mathcal{F}
on the other side. We are also interested in sufficient conditions, under which the group
\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})
is a direct summand in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. Since this is true, for instance, if the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is divisible, we are naturally led also to the study of divisibility of
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. Recall that every quotient group of a divisible group is again divisible and so it suffices to find sufficient conditions for divisibility of the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
.

1.10
The main aims of the present work

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow. Being motivated by our discussion from the preceding sections, we set ourselves the following aims in the present work.

(1)

Given a compact abelian group
G
, express the inclusion
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\subseteq\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
in terms of familiar (or elementary) abelian groups and compute the corresponding first cohomology group
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
.

We accomplish this task in the following two situations.

•

The acting group
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{F}
is a simply connected Lie group and the phase space
X
of
\mathcal{F}
is a compact second countable space with a non-trivial first cohomotopy group
\pi^{1}(X)
; see Theorem
6.12
in Section
6.2
and Theorem
6.23
in Section
6.3
(other results on
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
in this situation are obtained in Theorems
4.36
and
4.39
in Section
4.5
).

•

The acting group
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{F}
is a connected Lie group, the phase space
X
of
\mathcal{F}
is a compact manifold, the flow
\mathcal{F}
is topologically free and possesses a free cycle (the last two properties are defined in Subsection
2.5.2
); see Theorem
6.14
in Section
6.2
and Theorem
6.19
in Section
6.3
(other results on
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
in this situation are obtained in Theorems
6.25
and
6.28
in Section
6.4
).

In the first of these two situations, the cohomology group
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
of
\mathcal{F}
can be expressed in terms of elementary abelian groups, the Pontryagin dual
G^{*}
of
G
and the first cohomotopy group
\pi^{1}(X)
of
X
. In the second situation we express the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
in terms of elementary abelian groups, the Pontryagin dual
G^{*}
of
G
, the first weak homology group
H_{1}^{w}(X)
of
X
and the group
H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})
(the latter group is defined in Subsection
2.5.2
).

(2)

Given a compact abelian group
G
, find sufficient conditions for the existence of minimal extensions in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. More generally, determine the existence of large abelian subgroups within the groupoid
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
.
(The groupoid
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is formed by the minimal extensions from
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
together with the identity of
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, see Subsection
2.5.9
.)

In solving this problem we concentrate on the following three situations.

•

The group
\Gamma
is locally compact, non-compact, second countable, amenable, the space
X
is compact second countable and the flow
\mathcal{F}
possesses a free point; see Theorem
6.3
in Section
6.1
.

•

The group
\Gamma
is simply connected and the space
X
is compact second countable with
\pi^{1}(X)\neq 0
; see Theorem
6.5
in Section
6.1
.

•

The group
\Gamma
is a connected Lie group, the space
X
is a compact manifold and the flow
\mathcal{F}
possesses a free cycle; see Theorem
6.6
in Section
6.1
.

In all these situations we show that for every (non-trivial) compact connected abelian group
G
with weight at most
\mathfrak{c}=2^{\aleph_{0}}
, the groupoid
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
contains an isomorphic copy of the additive group of real numbers
\mathbb{R}
(and hence also an isomorphic copy of every torsion-free abelian group with rank at most
\mathfrak{c}
). Moreover, under some mild additional assumptions in the last two situations, we give necessary and sufficient conditions for a torsion-free abelian group
R
to have an isomorphic copy within the groupoid
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
that would form a dense subset of
\mathop{\rm Div}(\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G))
or
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
; see Theorem
6.32
in Section
6.5
.

(3)

Find relations of dynamical properties of group extensions of
\mathcal{F}
to one-dimensional algebraic-topological invariants (the fundamental group
\pi_{1}
and the first cohomotopy group
\pi^{1}
in particular) and to Pontryagin duality.

In our study of this problem we focus our attention on the following two situations.

•

The group
\Gamma
is connected and the space
X
is a (compact) manifold; see Theorem
5.14
and Corollary
5.16
in Section
5.2
.

•

The group
\Gamma
is connected and the space
X
is compact; see Theorems
5.32
and
5.40
in Section
5.5
.

In the first of these two situations we show that if the section
F(\mathcal{C})
of a group extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
of
\mathcal{F}
is totally disconnected (in particular, if it is finite) then it can be computed by using the monodromy action of the fundamental group of an appropriate quotient group of
G
. In the second situation it is shown that a totally disconnected section
F(\mathcal{C})
can be computed by using the first cohomotopy functor
\pi^{1}
and the Pontryagin duality. These results enable us to determine, whether or not a given closed subgroup
K
of a compact abelian group
G
takes the form of a section
K=F(\mathcal{C})
of some group extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
of
\mathcal{F}
; in the first situation we find necessary and sufficient conditions in Sections
5.3
and
5.4
, in the second situation we accomplish the same task in Section
5.6
.

(4)

Construct a free object for the category
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
of all group extensions of
\mathcal{F}
with compact abelian fibre groups, gain some information about its dynamical properties and express its range in terms of familiar compact abelian groups.

Basic properties of a free object for
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
are collected in Section
4.2
. In Theorem
4.5
a free object for
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is constructed - it consists of a compact abelian group
\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}}
and an extension
\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}})
; the freeness of
\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}}
lies in the fact that for every object
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
there is a unique morphism
h_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}}\to\mathcal{C}
. In the rest of Section
4.2
we

\centerdot

compute the section
F(\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}})
of
\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}}
,

\centerdot

find a relation between
\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}}
and
F(\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}})
on one side and a free compact abelian group over the base space
X
of
\mathcal{F}
on the other side,

\centerdot

express selected properties of group extensions
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{F}
in terms of concepts involving
\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}}
and
h_{\mathcal{C}}
.

In Section
4.3
we relate topological and algebraic properties of the group
\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}}
to algebraic properties of the groups
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. In Section
4.4
we show that a free object for the category of all
minimal
group extensions of
\mathcal{F}
does not exist. Finally, since the inclusion
F(\mathop{\rm\daleth_{\mathcal{F}}})\subseteq\mathop{\rm\gimel_{\mathcal{F}}}
is intimately related to the inclusion
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}\subseteq\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
, we may use our results on the problem (1) and express the first inclusion in terms of familiar compact abelian groups. This is done in the following two situations.

•

The group
\Gamma
is a simply connected Lie group and the space
X
is compact second countable with
\pi^{1}(X)\neq 0
.

•

The group
\Gamma
is a connected Lie group, the space
X
is a compact manifold, the flow
\mathcal{F}
is topologically free and possesses a free cycle.

These results are obtained in Section
6.2
; see Theorems
6.12
and
6.14
, respectively.

(5)

Find a dynamical interpretation of torsions in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. Express the torsion subgroup
\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})
of
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
in terms of familiar (or elementary) abelian groups. Find sufficient conditions for
\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})
to be a direct summand in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and determine its complementary summand
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}/\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})
.

In Corollary
3.9
in Section
3.2
we show that the torsion subgroup
\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})
of
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
coincides with the group of cohomology classes of non-minimal extensions from
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
; consequently, if
\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})
is a direct summand in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
then each of its complementary summands in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is a subgroup of the groupoid
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinH}_{\mathcal{F}}}
of the cohomology classes of extensions from
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
(see Subsection
2.5.9
). Further, we express the group
\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})
in terms of familiar abelian groups in the following situations.

•

The group
\Gamma
is simply connected and the space
X
is compact; see Theorem
4.41
in Section
4.6
.

•

The group
\Gamma
is a connected Lie group and the space
X
is a compact manifold; see Theorem
4.44
in Section
4.6
.

Moreover, in the first of these two situations, the group
\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})
is a direct summand in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
; see Theorem
3.43
in Section
3.5
. In the second situation this is also the case, provided the flow
\mathcal{F}
is topologically free; see Theorem
4.47
in Section
4.7
. Finally, it follows from our results on the structure of the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
in Section
6.2
that, under further mild assumptions on
\mathcal{F}
or
X
, the complementary summand to
\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is isomorphic to the additive group of real numbers
\mathbb{R}
in both these situations. Indeed, in the first situation, this is true if the space
X
is second countable with
\pi^{1}(X)\neq 0
(see Theorem
6.12
in Section
6.2
) and, in the second situation, this is true if the flow
\mathcal{F}
possesses a free cycle (see Theorem
6.14
in Section
6.2
).

Problems (1)–(5) formulated above are studied in Chapters
4
,
5
and
6
. Chapter
3
is, in a sense, auxiliary - it is devoted to the study of tools and techniques, which are used to solve our five main problems. There are basically five main ideas that occur in Chapter
3
.

(i)

For every compact abelian group
G
, there is a (topological) isomorphism between the group of extensions
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and the group of morphisms
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
, where
G^{*}
stands for the Pontryagin dual of
G
; see Theorem
3.1
and its corollaries in Section
3.1
.

(ii)

There is a functorial approach to sections of orbit closures
F(\mathcal{C})
of group extensions
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{F}
; see Theorem
3.7
and its corollaries in Section
3.2
. This approach, in which
F
is viewed as a covariant functor from the category
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
of all group extensions of
\mathcal{F}
to the category
\mathsf{CAGp}
of all compact abelian groups, is very convenient and makes many proofs throughout the whole present work more transparent.

(iii)

Given a compact abelian group
G
, the functor
F
gives rise to a map
F\colon\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\to 2^{G}
with values in the hypersemigroup
2^{G}
over
G
(see Subsection
2.4.16
for the definition of
2^{G}
). In Section
3.3
it is shown that the map
F
respects infinite sums in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and
2^{G}
in a certain, rather feeble, way; see Theorem
3.23
, Proposition
3.28
and Theorem
3.29
. Although this property of
F
from Theorem
3.23
seems fairly weak at a first glance, we shall find it extremely useful on many occasions.

(iv)

Since the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
of coboundaries is rarely closed in the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
with respect to the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets, the quotient cohomology group
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
does not carry a Hausdorff topology. In Section
3.4
we introduce on
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
a new topology, which we call the
ext-topology
. This is a Hausdorff group topology induced by a
2^{G}
-valued translation-invariant metric, which makes the map
F\colon\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\to 2^{G}
continuous; see Theorem
3.34
. This topology turns out to be more than a mere abstract construction; as a matter of fact, in Chapter
6
we will be able to express the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
with the ext-topology in terms of familiar abelian topological groups for large classes of minimal flows
\mathcal{F}
.

(v)

A special form of group extensions
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
as maps
\mathcal{C}\colon\Gamma\times X\to G
allows us to lift them to group extensions across covering morphisms also in situations when the phase space
X
of our flow
\mathcal{F}
is not locally connected. More concretely, this is always possible (even for non-compact abelian groups
G
) if the acting group
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{F}
is simply connected; see Theorem
3.38
in Section
3.5
. An important corollary of this result is Theorem
3.43
from Section
3.5
, which binds the groups
\pi^{1}(X)
,
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
into a short exact sequence; this theorem will turn out to be an important tool in our study of group extensions of minimal flows with simply connected acting groups
\Gamma
.

Chapter
2
of this work contains preliminary material from topology, theory of (compact) abelian groups and topological dynamics. The first four sections from this chapter are devoted mainly to recalling those concepts and results, which are used frequently in this work. However, in Section
2.5
we also introduce some new concepts and study their basic properties. It is of course not necessary to read the whole Chapter
2
before turning to the core of this work beginning with Chapter
3
. In fact, the reader will find it sufficient to leaf through Subsections
2.1.1
,
2.1.2
,
2.2.1
,
2.3.1
and
2.4.2
for basic notation and conventions, and then continue with Subsections
2.5.1
,
2.5.6
,
2.5.8
,
2.5.9
,
2.5.12
and
2.5.13
. Afterwards, the reader may return to selected parts of Chapter
2
, whenever he/she finds it useful to recall a relevant concept or result. We hope that this first chapter will make the reading of our work more comfortable.

Chapter 2
Preliminaries

2.1
Categories

2.1.1
Sets

We denote by
\mathsf{Sets}
the category of sets. The cardinality of a set
A
is denoted by
\mathop{\rm card}(A)
and a singleton set
\{x\}
by a shorter symbol
x
. For the composition of maps
g
and
f
we write
gf
instead of
g\circ f
. If
(X_{j})_{j\in J}
is a collection of sets and
i\in J
then
\mathop{\rm pr}_{i}
stands for the projection
\prod_{j\in J}X_{j}\to X_{i}
. We denote the set of all integral, rational, real and complex numbers by
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{C}
, respectively, and the symbol
\mathbb{N}
is used to denote the set of all positive integers. Finally, we write
\mathfrak{c}=2^{\aleph_{0}}
for the cardinality of the continuum.

2.1.2
Categories of groups

If
\mathsf{Cat}
is a category and
K
is an object of
\mathsf{Cat}
then we write
K\in\mathsf{Cat}
. We will be working with the following categories of groups:

•

\mathsf{AbGp}
–the category of abelian groups,

•

\mathsf{AbTpGp}
–the category of abelian topological groups,

•

\mathsf{DAGp}
–the category of discrete abelian groups,

•

\mathsf{CAGp}
–the category of compact abelian groups,

•

\mathsf{LCGp}
–the category of locally compact groups,

•

\mathsf{LCAGp}
–the category of locally compact abelian groups,

•

\mathsf{LieGp}
[symbol]
\mathsf{LieGp}
–the cateogry of Lie groups.

The categories
\mathsf{AbGp}
and
\mathsf{DAGp}
are isomorphic in a natural way and we shall therefore identify them when convenient. If
G,H\in\mathsf{AbTpGp}
then
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H)
denotes the set of all topological (that is, continuous) morphisms
G\to H
.

Every group
G\in\mathsf{AbTpGp}
is a uniform space, the uniformity being derived in a usual way from the topological group structure on
G
. The group
G
is called complete if every Cauchy net in
G
is convergent in
G
. Recall that each
G\in\mathsf{LCAGp}
is a complete group.

2.1.3
Category
\mathsf{Hom}(K,\mathsf{Cat})

Given a category
\mathsf{Cat}
and an object
K\in\mathsf{Cat}
, we denote by
\mathsf{Hom}(K,\mathsf{Cat})
the category defined as follows. The objects of
\mathsf{Hom}(K,\mathsf{Cat})
are the morphisms
l\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K,L)
with
L\in\mathsf{Cat}
. Further, if
l_{1}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K,L_{1})
and
l_{2}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K,L_{2})
then
\mathop{\rm Hom}(l_{1},l_{2})
consists of the morphisms
k\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(L_{1},L_{2})
with
kl_{1}=l_{2}
. The composition of morphisms in
\mathsf{Hom}(K,\mathsf{Cat})
is simply their composition in
\mathsf{Cat}
. Finally, if
L\in\mathsf{Cat}
and
\mathop{\rm id}_{L}
denotes the unit
\mathop{\rm id}_{L}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(L,L)
then
\mathop{\rm id}_{L}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(l,l)
for every
l\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K,L)
.

2.1.4
Category
\mathsf{Hom}(\mathsf{Cat},L)

Given a category
\mathsf{Cat}
and an object
L\in\mathsf{Cat}
, we denote by
\mathsf{Hom}(\mathsf{Cat},L)
the category defined as follows. The objects of
\mathsf{Hom}(\mathsf{Cat},L)
are the morphisms
l\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K,L)
with
K\in\mathsf{Cat}
. Further, if
l_{1}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K_{1},L)
and
l_{2}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K_{2},L)
then
\mathop{\rm Hom}(l_{1},l_{2})
consists of the morphisms
k\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K_{1},K_{2})
with
l_{2}k=l_{1}
. The composition of morphisms in
\mathsf{Hom}(\mathsf{Cat},L)
is their composition in
\mathsf{Cat}
. Finally, if
K\in\mathsf{Cat}
and
\mathop{\rm id}_{K}
denotes the unit
\mathop{\rm id}_{K}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K,K)
then
\mathop{\rm id}_{K}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(l,l)
for every
l\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K,L)
.

2.1.5
Free objects and universal elements

A
free object
for a category
\mathsf{Cat}
is an object
K\in\mathsf{Cat}
such that the set
\mathop{\rm Hom}(K,L)
is a singleton for every object
L\in\mathsf{Cat}
. Any two free objects for a given category
\mathsf{Cat}
are isomorphic within
\mathsf{Cat}
; we shall therefore often speak of
the
free object
K
for
\mathsf{Cat}
.

Let
\mathsf{Cat}
be a category and
F\colon\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Sets}
be a covariant (respectively, contravariant) functor. A
universal element
for
F
consists of an object
K\in\mathsf{Cat}
and an element
k\in F(K)
such that for every
L\in\mathsf{Cat}
and every
l\in F(L)
there is a unique
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K,L)
(respectively,
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(L,K)
) with
F(h)\colon k\mapsto l
. A universal element for a given functor
F
is essentially unique: if
K^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Cat}
and
k^{\prime}\in F(K^{\prime})
also constitute a universal element for
F
then there is an isomorphism
h\colon K\to K^{\prime}
(respectively,
h\colon K^{\prime}\to K
) with
F(h)\colon k\mapsto k^{\prime}
. We shall therefore often speak of
the
universal element for
F
.

2.1.6
Direct systems and their limits

A
direct system
in a category
\mathsf{Cat}
consists of a directed set
J
, a family of objects
K_{j}\in\mathsf{Cat}
(
j\in J
) and a family of morphisms
q_{ik}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K_{i},K_{k})
(
i\leq k
), such that
q_{jj}=\mathop{\rm id}_{K_{j}}
for every
j\in J
and
q_{kl}q_{ik}=q_{il}
for all
i\leq k\leq l
. A
direct limit
of this system consists of an object
K\in\mathsf{Cat}
and a family of morphisms
q_{j}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K_{j},K)
(
j\in J
) with
q_{k}q_{ik}=q_{i}
for
i\leq k
, such that the following condition holds:

•

given an object
L\in\mathsf{Cat}
and a family of morphisms
s_{j}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K_{j},L)
(
j\in J
) with
s_{k}q_{ik}=s_{i}
for
i\leq k
, there is a unique morphism
r\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K,L)
with
rq_{j}=s_{j}
for every
j\in J
.

Being a universal element for an appropriate covariant functor
\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Sets}
, a direct limit of a given direct system is unique up to isomorphism. We call a category
\mathsf{Cat}

complete
if every direct system in
\mathsf{Cat}
has a limit.

2.1.7
Inverse systems and their limits

An
inverse system
in a category
\mathsf{Cat}
consists of a directed set
J
, a family of objects
K_{j}\in\mathsf{Cat}
(
j\in J
) and a family of morphisms
p_{ik}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K_{k},K_{i})
(
i\leq k
), such that
p_{jj}=\mathop{\rm id}_{K_{j}}
for every
j\in J
and
p_{ik}p_{kl}=p_{il}
for all
i\leq k\leq l
. An
inverse limit
of this system consists of an object
K\in\mathsf{Cat}
and a family of morphisms
p_{j}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K,K_{j})
(
j\in J
) with
p_{ik}p_{k}=p_{i}
for
i\leq k
, such that the following condition holds:

•

given an object
L\in\mathsf{Cat}
and a family of morphisms
s_{j}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(L,K_{j})
(
j\in J
) with
p_{ik}s_{k}=s_{i}
for
i\leq k
, there is a unique morphism
r\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(L,K)
with
p_{j}r=s_{j}
for every
j\in J
.

Being a universal element for an appropriate contravariant functor
\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Sets}
, an inverse limit of a given inverse system is unique up to isomorphism. We call a category
\mathsf{Cat}

co-complete
if every inverse system in
\mathsf{Cat}
has a limit.

2.2
Abelian groups

Our references in this section are monographs
[
21
]
and
[
38
]
.

2.2.1
Basic facts

Let
A
be an abelian group. Depending on what is more convenient, we shall write the operations in
A
either additively or multiplicatively; in the first case,
0
stands for the neutral element of
A
, in the second case, the neutral element of
A
is denoted by
e
.

Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
k
-endomorphism of
A
is the morphism
\kappa_{k}\colon A\to A
, acting by the rule
\kappa_{k}(a)=ka
for every
a\in A
. The group
A
is called

•

k
-divisible, if
\mathop{\rm im}(\kappa_{k})=A
,

•

divisible, if it is
k
-divisible for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

a torsion group, if
A=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\ker(\kappa_{k})
,

•

torsion-free, if
\ker(\kappa_{k})=0
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

The subgroup of
A
generated by a set
S\subseteq A
is denoted by
\langle S\rangle
; if
S=\{a\}
is a singleton then we write
\langle a\rangle
instead of
\langle\{a\}\rangle
. The notation
B\mathrel{\lhd}A
is used to express that
B
is a subgroup of
A
; if the group
A
is topological then the symbol
B\mathrel{\lhd}A
means that
B
is a closed subgroup of
A
.

Let
A
be an abelian group,
B
be a subgroup of
A
and
d
be a positive integer. We say that
B
is a
d
-pure subgroup
of
A
(or that
B
is
d
-pure in
A
) if
B\cap\kappa_{d}(A)=\kappa_{d}(B)
; if
A
is torsion-free then this is equivalent to
\kappa_{d}^{-1}(B)\subseteq B
. The group
B
is
pure
in
A
if it is
d
-pure in
A
for every
d\in\mathbb{N}
.

The direct sum and the direct product of a family of abelian groups
A_{i}
(
i\in I
) is denoted by
\bigoplus_{i\in I}A_{i}
and
\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}
, respectively. Finally, given a cardinal
\mathfrak{k}
, the direct sum and the direct product of
\mathfrak{k}
copies of a group
A\in\mathsf{AbGp}
is denoted by
A^{(\mathfrak{k})}
and
A^{\mathfrak{k}}
, respectively.

2.2.2
Groups of morphisms

Let
A,B\in\mathsf{AbGp}
. We denote by
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
the set of all morphisms
A\to B
. With the point-wise defined operations,
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
is an abelian group. Further, if
C\mathrel{\lhd}A
and
D\mathrel{\lhd}B
, we denote by
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,C;B,D)
the set of all morphisms
h\colon A\to B
with
h(C)\subseteq D
. Clearly,
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,C;B,D)
is a subgroup of
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
. Finally, given abelian groups
A
,
B
,
A_{i}
,
B_{i}
(
i\in I
), there are isomorphisms

\mathop{\rm Hom}\left(\bigoplus_{i\in I}A_{i},B\right)\ni h\mapsto(h\sigma_{i})_{i\in I}\in\prod_{i\in I}\mathop{\rm Hom}(A_{i},B),

where
\sigma_{j}
is the inclusion morphism
A_{j}\to\bigoplus_{i\in I}A_{i}
for every
j\in I
, and

\mathop{\rm Hom}\left(A,\prod_{i\in I}B_{i}\right)\ni h\mapsto(\text{pr}_{i}h)_{i\in I}\in\prod_{i\in I}\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B_{i}),

where
\mathop{\rm pr}_{j}
is the projection morphism
\prod_{i\in I}B_{i}\to B_{j}
for every
j\in I
.

2.2.3
Groupoids of monomorphisms

Given
A,B\in\mathsf{AbGp}
, we use the symbol
\mathop{\rm Mon}(A,B)
to denote the set of all monomorphisms
A\to B
together with the trivial morphism
0
. Algebraically,
\mathop{\rm Mon}(A,B)
is a groupoid; that is, the product in
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
restricts to a partial operation on
\mathop{\rm Mon}(A,B)
, which is associative and commutative, and for which
0
is a neutral element. Clearly, every element
h\in\mathop{\rm Mon}(A,B)
has an inverse
-h\in\mathop{\rm Mon}(A,B)
. We shall refer to
\mathop{\rm Mon}(A,B)
as the
groupoid of monomorphisms

A\to B
.

2.2.4
Elementary divisors

Let
F
be a free abelian group of a finite rank
r
and let
E
be a subgroup of
F
. Then there exist a basis
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}
for
F
, an integer
0\leq n\leq r
and positive integers
d_{1},\dots,d_{n}
, such that
d_{1}f_{1},\dots,d_{n}f_{n}
is a basis for
E
and
d_{i}
divides
d_{i+1}
for
i=1,\dots,n-1
. Such numbers
d_{1},\dots,d_{n}
are unique and are called the
elementary divisors of
E
in
F
.

2.2.5
Torsions and divisibility

Let
A\in\mathsf{AbGp}
. The union of all divisible subgroups of
A
is the largest divisible subgroup of
A
, we denote it by
\mathop{\rm Div}(A)
. If
A
is either torsion-free or admits a topology of a compact group then
\mathop{\rm Div}(A)=\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\kappa_{k}(A)
. The group
\mathop{\rm Div}(A)
is a direct summand in
A
; each of its complementary summands in
A
is isomorphic to
A/\mathop{\rm Div}(A)
and is a reduced group (that is, it contains no non-trivial divisible subgroups).

Given a positive integer
k
, we denote by
\mathop{\rm tor}_{k}(A)
the group of the
k
-torsions of
A
, that is, the group of all
a\in A
with
ka=\kappa_{k}(a)=0
. The union
\mathop{\rm tor}(A)=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathop{\rm tor}_{k}(A)
is called the torsion subgroup of
A
. If
a\in\mathop{\rm tor}(A)
then the order (or the period) of
a
in
A
, denoted by
\mathop{\rm ord}(a)
, is the smallest of the positive integers
k
with
ka=0
. For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\mathop{\rm per}_{k}(A)
the set of all elements
a\in A
with order
\mathop{\rm ord}(a)=k
. If
a\in A\setminus\mathop{\rm tor}(A)
then we say that
a
is of an infinite order in
A
and write
\mathop{\rm ord}(a)=\infty
.

We shall view the group
\mathbb{T}^{1}
as a subset of
\mathbb{C}
with the complex multiplication. Then for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathop{\rm tor}_{k}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
consists of the
k^{\text{th}}
roots of unity. Since there is an obvious isomorphism between
\mathop{\rm tor}_{k}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
and
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
, we shall often identify these two groups.

Given co-prime integers
q,r\geq 2
, there is an isomorphism

\text{tor}_{qr}(A)=\text{tor}_{q}(A)\oplus\text{tor}_{r}(A)\cong\text{tor}_{q}(A)\times\text{tor}_{r}(A),

under which the set
\mathop{\rm per}_{qr}(A)\subseteq\mathop{\rm tor}_{qr}(A)
corresponds to
\mathop{\rm per}_{q}(A)\times\mathop{\rm per}_{r}(A)
. Consequently, if
k=p_{1}^{m_{1}}\dots p_{n}^{m_{n}}
is a prime decomposition of an integer
k\geq 2
then there is an isomorphism

\text{tor}_{k}(A)=\text{tor}_{p_{1}^{m_{1}}}(A)\oplus\dots\oplus\text{tor}_{p_{n}^{m_{n}}}(A)\cong\text{tor}_{p_{1}^{m_{1}}}(A)\times\dots\times\text{tor}_{p_{n}^{m_{n}}}(A),

under which the set
\mathop{\rm per}_{k}(A)\subseteq\mathop{\rm tor}_{k}(A)
corresponds to
\mathop{\rm per}_{p_{1}^{m_{1}}}(A)\times\dots\times\mathop{\rm per}_{p_{n}^{m_{n}}}(A)
.

If the group
A
is divisible then so is its torsion subgroup
\mathop{\rm tor}(A)
; in such a situation,
\mathop{\rm tor}(A)
is a direct summand in
A
, each of its complementary summands in
A
is isomorphic to
A/\mathop{\rm tor}(A)
and is a torsion-free group. Finally, for every
A\in\mathsf{AbGp}
,

\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm Div}(A))=\mathop{\rm Div}(\mathop{\rm tor}(A))=\mathop{\rm tor}(A)\cap\mathop{\rm Div}(A).

2.2.6
Betti number

Let
A
be a finitely generated abelian group. Then the torsion subgroup
\mathop{\rm tor}(A)
of
A
is a direct summand in
A
and its complementary summand
F
in
A
