is a free abelian group of a finite rank. We denote the rank of
F
by
\beta(A)
and call it the Betti number of
A
.

2.2.7
2-cocycles and 2-coboundaries

Let
A,C\in\mathsf{AbGp}
. A
(symmetric)
2
-cocycle over
C
with values in
A
is a map
\varphi\colon C\times C\to A
, satisfying the following identities:

(
C_{1}
)

\varphi(c_{1},c_{2})+\varphi(c_{1}+c_{2},c_{3})=\varphi(c_{1},c_{2}+c_{3})+\varphi(c_{2},c_{3})
,

(
C_{2}
)

\varphi(0,c)=\varphi(c,0)=0
,

(
C_{3}
)

\varphi(c_{1},c_{2})=\varphi(c_{2},c_{1})
.

Such maps
\varphi
form a group
Z^{2}(C,A)
with the point-wise operations. Every map
f\colon C\to A
with
f(0)=0
gives rise to a
2
-cocycle
\varphi\in Z^{2}(C,A)
, given by

\varphi(c_{1},c_{2})=f(c_{1})+f(c_{2})-f(c_{1}+c_{2});

in this situation we say that
\varphi
is a
2
-coboundary
with a
transfer function

f
. The
2
-coboundaries form a subgroup
B^{2}(C,A)
of
Z^{2}(C,A)
; we shall denote the corresponding quotient cohomology group
Z^{2}(C,A)/B^{2}(C,A)
by
\mathop{\rm Ext}(C,A)
.

Given a map
\varphi\colon C\times C\to A
, we denote by
C\times_{\varphi}A
the product
C\times A
equipped with the binary operation

(c_{1},a_{1})+(c_{2},a_{2})=(c_{1}+c_{2},a_{1}+a_{2}+\varphi(c_{1},c_{2})).

In order that
C\times_{\varphi}A
be a group, it is necessary and sufficient that conditions (
C_{1}
) and (
C_{2}
) hold true. Moreover, the group
C\times_{\varphi}A
is then commutative if and only if
\varphi
satisfies condition (
C_{3}
). Consequently,
C\times_{\varphi}A\in\mathsf{AbGp}
if and only if
\varphi\in Z^{2}(C,A)
.

2.2.8
Extensions of abelian groups

A short exact sequence of abelian groups

E\colon 0\longrightarrow A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu}}{{\longrightarrow}}B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu}}{{\longrightarrow}}C\longrightarrow 0

(2.1)

is called an
extension of
C
by
A
. Two such extensions

E_{1}\colon 0\longrightarrow A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}B_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C\longrightarrow 0\hskip 11.38109pt\text{and}\hskip 11.38109ptE_{2}\colon 0\longrightarrow A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}B_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C\longrightarrow 0

are called
equivalent
if there is a morphism of groups
h\colon B_{1}\to B_{2}
with
h\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}
and
\nu_{2}h=\nu_{1}
; by the five-lemma, such a morphism
h
is automatically an isomorphism. We write
E_{1}\cong E_{2}
to express that
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are equivalent extensions. The extension
E
in (
2.1
) is said to
split
if it is equivalent to the extension

E_{0}(C,A)=E_{0}\colon 0\longrightarrow A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C\oplus A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C\longrightarrow 0,

where
\mu_{0}(a)=(0,a)
and
\nu_{0}(c,a)=c
for all
a\in A
and
c\in C
; this occurs if and only if one of the following conditions holds:

•

there is
k\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(C,B)
with
\nu k=\mathop{\rm Id}_{C}
,

•

there is
l\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(B,A)
with
l\mu=\mathop{\rm Id}_{A}
.

Every
2
-cocycle
\varphi\in Z^{2}(C,A)
gives rise to an extension

E(\varphi)=E_{\varphi}\colon 0\longrightarrow A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\varphi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C\times_{\varphi}A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu_{\varphi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C\longrightarrow 0,

(2.2)

where
\mu_{\varphi}(a)=(0,a)
and
\nu_{\varphi}(c,a)=c
for all
a\in A
and
c\in C
. Recall the following facts:

•

every extension
E
of the form (
2.1
) is equivalent to an extension
E_{\varphi}
from (
2.2
) for some
\varphi\in Z^{2}(C,A)
,

•

given
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in Z^{2}(C,A)
, the extensions
E_{\varphi_{1}}
,
E_{\varphi_{2}}
are equivalent if and only if the
2
-cocycles
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
are cohomologous (that is, if and only if
\varphi_{1}-\varphi_{2}\in B^{2}(C,A)
).

It follows from these two statements that the set of equivalence classes of the extensions of
C
by
A
can be identified with the cohomology group
\mathop{\rm Ext}(C,A)
.

Recall the following properties of
\mathop{\rm Ext}
:

•

given abelian groups
A,C,A_{i},C_{i}
(
i\in I
), there are isomorphisms

\mathop{\rm Ext}\left(\bigoplus_{i\in I}C_{i},A\right)\cong\prod_{i\in I}\mathop{\rm Ext}(C_{i},A)

and

\mathop{\rm Ext}\left(C,\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}\right)\cong\prod_{i\in I}\mathop{\rm Ext}(C,A_{i}),

•

if
C\in\mathsf{AbGp}
then the equality
\mathop{\rm Ext}(C,A)=0
holds for every
A\in\mathsf{AbGp}
if and only if the group
C
is free abelian (in particular, if
B\in\mathsf{AbGp}
,
A\mathrel{\lhd}B
and the group
B/A
is free abelian, then
A
is a direct summand in
B
),

•

if
A\in\mathsf{AbGp}
then the equality
\mathop{\rm Ext}(C,A)=0
holds for every
C\in\mathsf{AbGp}
if and only if the group
A
is divisible (in particular, if
A
is a divisible subgroup of an abelian group
B
then
A
is a direct summand in
B
),

•

\mathop{\rm Ext}(\mathbb{Z}_{k},A)\cong A/kA
for all
A\in\mathsf{AbGp}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

2.2.9
Some concrete extensions

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Consider the short exact sequence in
\mathsf{AbGp}

E(\mathbb{Z}_{k})\colon 0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\kappa_{k}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{k}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{k}\longrightarrow 0,

(2.3)

where
\kappa_{k}(m)=km
and
p_{k}(m)=m
(mod
k
) for every
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. This sequence is equivalent to the sequence
E_{\varphi_{k}}
, where
\varphi_{k}\in Z^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{k},\mathbb{Z})
acts by the rule

\varphi_{k}(r,s)=\begin{cases}0;&\text{if $r+s<k$},\\
1;&\text{if $r+s\geq k$},\end{cases}

(2.4)

for all
0\leq r,s\leq k-1
. Indeed, the map

h\colon\mathbb{Z}\ni m\mapsto\left(p_{k}(m),\left[\frac{m}{k}\right]\right)\in\mathbb{Z}_{k}\times_{\varphi_{k}}\mathbb{Z}

is an isomorphism of groups with
h\kappa_{k}=\mu_{\varphi_{k}}
and
\nu_{\varphi_{k}}h=p_{k}
, where
[m/k]
stands for the integer part of
m/k
.

Now consider the short exact sequence in
\mathsf{AbGp}

E(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})\colon 0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow 0,

(2.5)

where
j
is the inclusion morphism and
p
is the quotient morphism. This sequence is equivalent to the sequence
E_{\psi}
, where
\psi\in Z^{2}(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
acts by the rule

\psi(r,s)=\begin{cases}0;&\text{if $r+s<1$},\\
1;&\text{if $r+s\geq 1$},\end{cases}

(2.6)

for all rationals
0\leq r,s<1
. Indeed, the map

h\colon\mathbb{Q}\ni q\mapsto(p(q),[q])\in(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})\times_{\psi}\mathbb{Z}

is an isomorphism of groups with
hj=\mu_{\psi}
and
\nu_{\psi}h=p
, where
[q]
stands for the integer part of
q
.

2.2.10
Direct sum of sequences and
2
-cocycles

Given short exact sequences of abelian groups

E_{i}\colon 0\longrightarrow A_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}B_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C_{i}\longrightarrow 0,

i=1,\dots,n
, there is an exact sequence of abelian groups

E\colon 0\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}C_{i}\longrightarrow 0,

where
\mu=\mu_{1}\times\dots\times\mu_{n}
and
\nu=\nu_{1}\times\dots\times\nu_{n}
. We call
E
the direct sum of the sequences
E_{i}
and write
E=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}E_{i}
.

Write
A=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}
and
C=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}C_{i}
. For
i=1,\dots,n
, let
\pi_{i}
and
\vartheta_{i}
denote the projection morphisms
C\to C_{i}
and
A\to A_{i}
, respectively, and fix
\varphi_{i}\in Z^{2}(C_{i},A_{i})
with
E_{i}\cong E_{\varphi_{i}}
. Define
\varphi\in Z^{2}(C,A)
by the rule
\vartheta_{i}\varphi=\varphi_{i}(\pi_{i}\times\pi_{i})
for
i=1,\dots,n
. We call
\varphi
the direct sum of the
2
-cocycles
\varphi_{i}
and write
\varphi=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}
. There are equivalences

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}E_{i}\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}E(\varphi_{i})\cong E\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}\right).

2.2.11
Exact Hom-Ext sequences

Let
A,B,C\in\mathsf{AbGp}
and
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
. The morphism
Z^{2}(C,A)\ni\varphi\mapsto h\varphi\in Z^{2}(C,B)
maps
2
-coboundaries to
2
-coboundaries and so it gives rise to a morphism
\mathop{\rm Ext}(C,h)\colon\mathop{\rm Ext}(C,A)\to\mathop{\rm Ext}(C,B)
. Clearly, the assignment
h\mapsto\mathop{\rm Ext}(C,h)
is covariant functorial. Similarly, if
A,C,D\in\mathsf{AbGp}
and
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(D,C)
then the morphism
Z^{2}(C,A)\ni\varphi\mapsto\varphi(h\times h)\in Z^{2}(D,A)
induces a morphism
\mathop{\rm Ext}(h,A)\colon\mathop{\rm Ext}(C,A)\to\mathop{\rm Ext}(D,A)
. This time, the assignment
h\mapsto\mathop{\rm Ext}(h,A)
is contravariant functorial.

Now let
E
be a short exact sequence of abelian groups as in (
2.1
) and
D\in\mathsf{AbGp}
. By a theorem due to Cartan and Eilenberg, there is an exact sequence

\begin{split}0&\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Hom}(D,A)\xrightarrow{\mathop{\rm Hom}(D,\mu)}\mathop{\rm Hom}(D,B)\xrightarrow{\mathop{\rm Hom}(D,\nu)}\mathop{\rm Hom}(D,C)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\epsilon}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{\rm Ext}(D,A)\xrightarrow{\mathop{\rm Ext}(D,\mu)}\mathop{\rm Ext}(D,B)\xrightarrow{\mathop{\rm Ext}(D,\nu)}\mathop{\rm Ext}(D,C)\longrightarrow 0,\end{split}

which we shall refer to as the
covariant Hom-Ext sequence
derived from
E
and associated to
D
. Recall that the
connecting morphism

\epsilon
assigns to every morphism
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(D,C)
the cohomology class of
\varphi(h\times h)\in Z^{2}(D,A)
, where
\varphi\in Z^{2}(C,A)
is chosen in such a way that
E\cong E_{\varphi}
.

Further, by a theorem due to Cartan and Eilenberg,
E
and
D
give rise to an exact sequence

\begin{split}0&\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Hom}(C,D)\xrightarrow{\mathop{\rm Hom}(\nu,D)}\mathop{\rm Hom}(B,D)\xrightarrow{\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mu,D)}\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,D)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\epsilon}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{\rm Ext}(C,D)\xrightarrow{\mathop{\rm Ext}(\nu,D)}\mathop{\rm Ext}(B,D)\xrightarrow{\mathop{\rm Ext}(\mu,D)}\mathop{\rm Ext}(A,D)\longrightarrow 0,\end{split}

which we shall refer to as the
contravariant Hom-Ext sequence
derived from
E
and associated to
D
. This time, the
connecting morphism

\epsilon
assigns to every morphism
k\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,D)
the cohomology class of
k\varphi\in Z^{2}(C,D)
, where, as above,
\varphi\in Z^{2}(C,A)
is chosen in such a way that
E\cong E_{\varphi}
.

2.2.12
Whitehead groups

An abelian group
A
is called a
Whitehead group
if it satisfies the equality
\mathop{\rm Ext}(A,\mathbb{Z})=0
or, equivalently, if every morphism
A\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
lifts to a morphism
A\to\mathbb{R}
across the usual quotient morphism
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
. Clearly, every free abelian group is a Whitehead group. Though the converse implication is undecidable in ZFC by the Shelah’s independence theorem, every
countable
Whitehead group is free abelian by the Pontryagin theorem.

2.2.13
Tensor and torsion products

Given groups
A,C\in\mathsf{AbGp}
, we denote their tensor product by
A\otimes C
. Recall that
A\otimes C\in\mathsf{AbGp}
by definition and that the monomial tensors
a\otimes c
(
a\in A
,
c\in C
) form a set of generators of
A\otimes C
. Further, given groups
A,B,C,D\in\mathsf{AbGp}
and morphisms
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
,
k\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(C,D)
, we denote by
h\otimes k
the tensor product of
h
and
k
. By definition,
h\otimes k\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(A\otimes C,B\otimes D)
. Recall that on monomial tensors from
A\otimes C
, the morphism
h\otimes k
acts by the rule
(h\otimes k)(a\otimes c)=h(a)\otimes k(c)
(
a\in A
,
c\in C
).

Recall the following properties of tensor products:

•

there is an isomorphism
A\otimes C\cong C\otimes A
for all
A,C\in\mathsf{AbGp}
,

•

A\otimes C=0
whenever
A\in\mathsf{AbGp}
is a torsion group and
C\in\mathsf{AbGp}
is divisible,

•

there are isomorphisms
\mathbb{Z}\otimes C\cong C
and
\mathbb{Z}_{k}\otimes C\cong C/kC
for all
C\in\mathsf{AbGp}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

given abelian groups
A_{i}
(
i\in I
),
C_{j}
(
j\in J
), there is an isomorphism

\Bigg(\bigoplus_{i\in I}A_{i}\Bigg)\otimes\Bigg(\bigoplus_{j\in J}C_{j}\Bigg)\cong\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in J\end{subarray}}A_{i}\otimes C_{j},

•

given abelian groups
A,C,D
, there is an isomorphism of groups

\varphi\colon\mathop{\rm Hom}(A\otimes C,D)\ni h\mapsto\varphi(h)=\widetilde{h}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,\mathop{\rm Hom}(C,D)),

(2.7)

where
\widetilde{h}(a)\colon C\ni c\mapsto h(a\otimes c)\in D
for every
a\in A
; if the group
D
is topological and all three
\mathop{\rm Hom}
groups above are equipped with the topology of point-wise convergence, then
\varphi
is in fact a topological isomorphism.

Given groups
A,C\in\mathsf{AbGp}
, we denote their torsion product by
\mathop{\rm Tor}(A,C)
. Recall that
\mathop{\rm Tor}(A,C)=\mathop{\rm Tor}(\mathop{\rm tor}(A),\mathop{\rm tor}(C))\in\mathsf{AbGp}
is a torsion group by definition. Further, given groups
A,B,C,D\in\mathsf{AbGp}
and morphisms
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
,
k\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(C,D)
, we denote by
\mathop{\rm Tor}(h,k)
the torsion product of
h
and
k
. By definition,
\mathop{\rm Tor}(h,k)
is an element of
\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathop{\rm Tor}(A,C),\mathop{\rm Tor}(B,D))
.

Recall the following properties of
\mathop{\rm Tor}
:

•

there is an isomorphism
\mathop{\rm Tor}(A,C)\cong\mathop{\rm Tor}(C,A)
for all
A,C\in\mathsf{AbGp}
,

•

there are isomorphisms
\mathop{\rm Tor}(\mathbb{Z}_{k},C)\cong\mathop{\rm tor}_{k}(C)
and
\mathop{\rm Tor}(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z},C)\cong\mathop{\rm tor}(C)
for all
C\in\mathsf{AbGp}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

given abelian groups
A_{i}
(
i\in I
),
C_{j}
(
j\in J
), there is an isomorphism

\mathop{\rm Tor}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{i\in I}A_{i},\bigoplus_{j\in J}C_{j}\Bigg)\cong\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in J\end{subarray}}\mathop{\rm Tor}(A_{i},C_{j}).

Now let
E
be a short exact sequence of abelian groups as in (
2.1
) and
D\in\mathsf{AbGp}
. By a theorem due to Cartan and Eilenberg, there is an exact sequence

\begin{split}0&\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Tor}(A,D)\xrightarrow{\mathop{\rm Tor}(\mu,\mathop{\rm id})}\mathop{\rm Tor}(B,D)\xrightarrow{\mathop{\rm Tor}(\nu,\mathop{\rm id})}\mathop{\rm Tor}(C,D)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\epsilon}}{{\longrightarrow}}A\otimes D\xrightarrow{\mu\otimes\mathop{\rm id}}B\otimes D\xrightarrow{\nu\otimes\mathop{\rm id}}C\otimes D\longrightarrow 0,\end{split}

which we shall refer to as the
exact sequence for tensor and torsion products
derived from
E
and associated to
D
. The morphism
\epsilon
is called the
connecting morphism
.

2.2.14
Divisible hull and injective resolution

Let
A
be a torsion-free abelian group. The tensor product
\mathbb{Q}\otimes A
is a torsion-free abelian group and each of its elements is expressible in the form of a monomial tensor. The mapping
j\colon A\to\mathbb{Q}\otimes A
, given by
j(a)=1\otimes a
, is a monomorphism of groups with the image
\mathop{\rm im}(j)=\mathbb{Z}\otimes A
. In fact,
\mathbb{Q}\otimes A
is a divisible hull of
A
with respect to the inclusion morphism
j
.

Under the identification
A\cong\mathop{\rm im}(j)=\mathbb{Z}\otimes A
, there is an isomorphism

(\mathbb{Q}\otimes A)/A\cong(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})\otimes A.

More precisely, if
q
stands for the quotient morphism
\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
then

p=q\otimes\text{id}_{A}\colon\mathbb{Q}\otimes A\to(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})\otimes A

is a quotient morphism with kernel
\ker(p)=\mathbb{Z}\otimes A
. We will refer to the corresponding short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Q}\otimes A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})\otimes A\longrightarrow 0

(2.8)

as the
injective resolution
of
A
.

Recall that a subset
S
of
A
is called
independent
in
A
if the subgroup
\langle S\rangle
of
A
generated by
S
is free abelian with the basis
S
. The set
S
is independent in
A
if and only if its image
j(S)
is a set of rationally independent elements of
\mathbb{Q}\otimes A
. Every independent set
S\subseteq A
is contained in a maximal independent set. A set
S\subseteq A
is maximal independent if and only if its image
j(S)
is a rational linear basis for
\mathbb{Q}\otimes A
. Consequently, any two maximal independent sets have the same cardinality
\mathop{\rm rank}(A)
called the
rank
of
A
, which coincides with the rational linear dimension
\mathop{\rm dim_{\mathbb{Q}LS}}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes A)
of
\mathbb{Q}\otimes A
. Finally, an independent set
S\subseteq A
is maximal independent if and only if the quotient group
A/\langle S\rangle
is a torsion group.

2.3
Topology

Our references in this section are monographs
[
36
]
and
[
38
, Chapter 8]
.

2.3.1
Some conventions

By a (topological) space we mean a Hausdorff topological space. A space
X
is often, explicitly or implicitly, assumed pointed, its base point then being denoted by
z
. The base point of a topological group is always its identity. If
X
and
Y
are spaces with base points
z
and
v
, respectively, then the product space
X\times Y
is equipped with the base point
(z,v)
. We shall denote the weight of
X
by
\mathop{\rm w}(X)
and the density of
X
by
d(X)
. Finally, the symbol
\mathsf{CLAC}
stands for the category of the connected and locally arc-wise connected topological spaces; clearly, each space
X\in\mathsf{CLAC}
is arc-wise connected.

2.3.2
First weak homology group

Let
X
be a pointed space with the base point
z
. We denote the fundamental group of
X
by
\pi_{1}(X,z)
or, briefly, by
\pi_{1}(X)
. To simplify notation, we shall sometimes identify a loop in
X
based at
z
with its path-homotopy class in
\pi_{1}(X)
. The product of two elements
f,g\in\pi_{1}(X)
in
\pi_{1}(X)
is denoted by
f*g
; the same symbol
f*g
is used to denote the product of two paths
f,g
in
X
with
f(1)=g(0)
. (If
X
is a topological group with the identity
z
then the group
\pi_{1}(X)
is abelian, the product in
\pi_{1}(X)
coincides with the point-wise product in
X
and the inverse of
f\in\pi_{1}(X)
in
\pi_{1}(X)
is its point-wise inverse in
X
.) Further, given
z^{\prime}\in X
and a path
f
in
X
from
z
to
z^{\prime}
, we denote by
\widehat{f}
the isomorphism
\pi_{1}(X,z)\ni g\mapsto\overline{f}*g*f\in\pi_{1}(X,z^{\prime})
, where
\overline{f}
stands for the path reverse to
f
.

The first homology group of
X
is denoted by
H_{1}(X)
and is interpreted exclusively as the abelianisation of
\pi_{1}(X)
. The first weak homology group of
X
is denoted by
H_{1}^{w}(X)
. Recall that
H_{1}^{w}(X)
is defined as the largest torsion-free quotient group of
H_{1}(X)
, that is,
H_{1}^{w}(X)=H_{1}(X)/\mathop{\rm tor}(H_{1}(X))
; we shall denote by
p_{X}
the quotient morphism
\pi_{1}(X)\to H_{1}^{w}(X)
. Should it be necessary to invoke the base point
z
of
X
, we write
H_{1}^{w}(X,z)
and
p_{X,z}
instead of
H_{1}^{w}(X)
and
p_{X}
, respectively. Given
z^{\prime}\in X
and a path
f
in
X
from
z
to
z^{\prime}
, the isomorphism
\widehat{f}\colon\pi_{1}(X,z)\to\pi_{1}(X,z^{\prime})
projects to an isomorphism
\widehat{f}\colon H_{1}^{w}(X,z)\to H_{1}^{w}(X,z^{\prime})
with
p_{X,z^{\prime}}\widehat{f}=\widehat{f}p_{X,z}
.

If
Y
is a space and
h\colon X\to Y
is a continuous map then the same symbol
h^{\sharp,z}
(briefly,
h^{\sharp}
) is used to denote the induced morphisms
\pi_{1}(X,z)\to\pi_{1}(Y,h(z))
and
H_{1}^{w}(X,z)\to H_{1}^{w}(Y,h(z))
; thus,

h^{\sharp,z}p_{X,z}=p_{Y,h(z)}h^{\sharp,z}\colon\pi_{1}(X,z)\to H_{1}^{w}(Y,h(z)).

Finally, given
z^{\prime}\in X
and a path
f
in
X
from
z
to
z^{\prime}
, we have

h^{\sharp,z^{\prime}}\widehat{f}=\widehat{hf}h^{\sharp,z}\colon H_{1}^{w}(X,z)\to H_{1}^{w}(Y,h(z^{\prime})).

2.3.3
Fundamental groups of manifolds and Lie groups

Recall that the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X)
of a connected manifold
X
is countable. If
X
is also compact then the group
\pi_{1}(X)
is finitely generated and hence
H_{1}^{w}(X)
is a free abelian group of a finite rank; the rank of
H_{1}^{w}(X)
is sometimes called the first Betti number of
X
.

We shall make use of the following results on homotopy of Lie groups due to Iwasawa and Mal’cev. Let
\Gamma\in\mathsf{LieGp}
be a connected Lie group. Any two maximal compact subgroups of
\Gamma
are connected and conjugate in
\Gamma
. Moreover, if
K
is a maximal compact subgroup of
\Gamma
then the inclusion morphism
K\to\Gamma
is a homotopy equivalence and so it induces isomorphisms of groups
\pi_{1}(K)\to\pi_{1}(\Gamma)
and
H_{1}^{w}(K)\to H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)
. It follows that the group
\pi_{1}(\Gamma)
is finitely generated and that
H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)
is a free abelian group of a finite rank.

2.3.4
Covering maps

Let
E,B
be topological spaces and
p\colon E\to B
be a continuous surjective map. Recall that
p
is called a covering map if every element of
B
has a neighbourhood
U
such that the set
p^{-1}(U)
is a disjoint union of open sets
V_{i}\subseteq E
(
i\in I
), for which the restrictions
p\colon V_{i}\to U
are homeomorphisms. In such a case we say that
U
is evenly covered by
p
and that
V_{i}
is a
p
-slice over
U
for every
i\in I
. If the set
U
is connected then there is a unique family of
p
-slices over
U
.

2.3.5
Covering morphisms

Let
G,G^{\prime}
be abelian topological groups and let
p\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{\prime},G)
be an epimorphism. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

p
is a covering morphism,

•

p
is a local homeomorphism,

•

p
is open (equivalently, quotient) with a discrete kernel
\ker(p)
.

If, in addition, both
G^{\prime},G
are locally compact and
\sigma
-compact then every epimorphism
G^{\prime}\to G
is open and so the following condition is equivalent to the previous three:

•

p
has a discrete kernel
\ker(p)
.

2.3.6
Lifts across covering maps

Let
X,E,B
be pointed spaces and let
f\colon X\to B
,
p\colon E\to B
be continuous base point preserving maps. If
X\in\mathsf{CLAC}
and
p
is a covering map then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

f
lifts across
p
to a continuous base point preserving map
X\to E
,

•

f^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(X)\subseteq p^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(E)
.

These conditions are fulfilled if
\pi_{1}(X)=0
, in which case we call the space
X
simply connected.

2.3.7
Monodromy action

Let
E,B
be topological spaces and
p\colon E\to B
be a covering map. Fix
b_{0}\in B
and denote by
K
the fibre
p^{-1}(b_{0})
of
p
over
b_{0}
. The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(B,b_{0})
of
B
acts from the right on the set
K
by the rule

K\times\pi_{1}(B,b_{0})\ni(e,f)\mapsto\widetilde{f}_{e}(1)\in K,

where
\widetilde{f}_{e}
stands for the lift of
f
across
p
starting at
e
and
\widetilde{f}_{e}(1)
is the endpoint of
\widetilde{f}_{e}
. This action is known as the
monodromy action
of
\pi_{1}(B,b_{0})
on
K
. For every
e\in K
, the isotropy group of
e
with respect to this action is
p^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(E,e)
. Finally, if the group
\pi_{1}(B,b_{0})
is abelian (which is the case, for instance, if
B
is a topological group and
b_{0}
is the identity of
B
) then we may view the monodromy action as an action of
\pi_{1}(B,b_{0})
on
K
from the left.

2.3.8
Topological linear dimension

If
L
is a linear space over a field
\mathbb{F}
then its linear dimension is denoted by
\dim_{\mathbb{F}\text{LS}}(L)
. If
B
is a real Banach space then we use the symbol
\mathop{\rm dim_{\mathbb{R}BS}}(B)
to denote the topological linear dimension of
B
, that is, the smallest of the linear dimensions
\mathop{\rm dim_{\mathbb{R}LS}}(L)
of the dense linear subspaces
L
of
B
. We recall the Kruse-Schmidt-Stone theorem, which asserts that for every Banach space
B
,
\mathop{\rm card}(B)=d(B)^{\aleph_{0}}
, where
d(B)
stands for the density of
B
. If the space
B
is infinite-dimensional then
d(B)=\mathop{\rm dim_{\mathbb{R}BS}}(B)
and, by the Mackey theorem,
\mathop{\rm dim_{\mathbb{R}LS}}(B)\geq\mathfrak{c}=2^{\aleph_{0}}
.

2.3.9
The space of real functions

Given a pointed compact space
X
with the base point
z
, we denote by
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R})
the set of all continuous maps
f\colon X\to\mathbb{R}
with
f(z)=0
. With operations defined point-wise and with the supremum norm,
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R})
is a Banach space (in fact, a Banach algebra). Recall that if the space
X
is infinite then

\mathop{\rm dim_{\mathbb{R}LS}}(C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R}))=\mathop{\rm card}(C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R}))=\mathop{\rm w}(X)^{\aleph_{0}};

consequently, there is an isomorphism of abelian groups
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}^{\left(\mathop{\rm w}(X)^{\aleph_{0}}\right)}
.

2.4
Compact abelian groups

Our references in this section are monographs
[
37
]
,
[
38
]
,
[
46
]
and
[
50
]
.

2.4.1
Co-completeness of
\mathsf{CAGp}

The category
\mathsf{CAGp}
of compact abelian groups is co-complete; we recall the usual construction of an inverse limit in
\mathsf{CAGp}
. Let
\mathfrak{G}=(G_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}G_{k})
be an inverse system in
\mathsf{CAGp}
, indexed by a directed set
J
. Denote by
G
the set of all
(g_{j})_{j\in J}\in\prod_{j\in J}G_{j}
, such that
p_{ik}(g_{k})=g_{i}
for all
i\leq k
. Then
G
is a closed subgroup of the product
\prod_{j\in J}G_{j}
and hence
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
. Moreover, the restrictions
p_{j}\colon G\to G_{j}
of the projection morphisms
\mathop{\rm pr}_{j}
satisfy
p_{ik}p_{k}=p_{i}
for all
i\leq k
. As is well known, the group
G
is an inverse limit of
\mathfrak{G}
in
\mathsf{CAGp}
and
p_{j}
(
j\in J
) are the limit projections. Notice that the morphisms
p_{j}
separate the points of
G
. Since an inverse limit of
\mathfrak{G}
in
\mathsf{CAGp}
is unique up to isomorphism, it follows that the limit projections of an arbitrary inverse limit of
\mathfrak{G}
in
\mathsf{CAGp}
separate points.

2.4.2
Pontryagin duality

Let
G\in\mathsf{LCAGp}
. The Pontryagin dual of
G
will be denoted by
G^{*}
. Recall that
G^{*}
consists of the topological morphisms
\chi\colon G\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
, called the characters of
G
. With the compact-open topology and point-wise defined operations,
G^{*}
is a locally compact abelian group. If
G
is compact then
G^{*}
is discrete and if
G
is discrete then
G^{*}
is compact. Moreover,
G^{*}
is second countable if and only if
G
is second countable. The groups
G
and
G^{**}
are topologically isomorphic via the isomorphism
\omega_{G}\colon G\to G^{**}
, acting by the rule
\omega_{G}(g)\colon G^{*}\ni\chi\mapsto\chi(g)\in\mathbb{T}^{1}
for every
g\in G
. We call
\omega_{G}
the Pontryagin isomorphism associated to
G
. Recall also that for every
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
,
\dim(G)=\mathop{\rm rank}(G^{*})
and
\mathop{\rm w}(G)=\mathop{\rm card}(G^{*})
.

Given
G\in\mathsf{LCAGp}
and a subset
A
of
G
, the annihilator of
A
in
G^{*}
is denoted by
[A^{\perp},G^{*}]
or, if no misunderstanding can arise, by a shorter symbol
A^{\perp}
. Recall that
A^{\perp}
is formed by the characters
\chi\in G^{*}
with
A\subseteq\ker(\chi)
. The annihilator of
A
coincides with the annihilator of the closed subgroup of
G
generated by
A
. Moreover,
A^{\perp}
is always a closed subgroup of
G^{*}
. If
H\mathrel{\lhd}G
then
\omega_{G}(H)=H^{\perp\perp}
and
\omega_{G^{*}}(H^{\perp})=\omega_{G}(H)^{\perp}
. Given a system of closed subgroups
H_{i}
(
i\in I
) of
G
, there are equalities
\bigcap_{i\in I}H_{i}^{\perp}=\left(\sum_{i\in I}H_{i}\right)^{\perp}
and
\left(\bigcap_{i\in I}H_{i}\right)^{\perp}=\overline{\sum_{i\in I}H_{i}^{\perp}}
. If
G\in\mathsf{LCAGp}
is either compact or discrete,
H,K\mathrel{\lhd}G
and there is a topological direct sum
G=H\oplus K
, then there is also a topological direct sum
G^{*}=H^{\perp}\oplus K^{\perp}
.

Let
G_{1},G_{2}\in\mathsf{LCAGp}
and
h\colon G_{1}\to G_{2}
be a topological morphism. The Pontryagin dual
h^{*}
of
h
is the topological morphism
G_{2}^{*}\to G_{1}^{*}
, acting by the rule
h^{*}(\Upsilon)=\Upsilon h
for every
\Upsilon\in G_{2}^{*}
. One has
\omega_{G_{2}}h=h^{**}\omega_{G_{1}}
,
\mathop{\rm im}(h)^{\perp}=\ker(h^{*})
and
\ker(h)^{\perp}=\overline{\mathop{\rm im}(h^{*})}
. In particular,
h
is a monomorphism if and only if
h^{*}
has a dense image and, conversely,
h
has a dense image if and only if
h^{*}
is a monomorphism. More generally, if
H_{1}\mathrel{\lhd}G_{1}
and
H_{2}\mathrel{\lhd}G_{2}
then
h(H_{1})^{\perp}=(h^{*})^{-1}(H_{1}^{\perp})
and
h^{-1}(H_{2})^{\perp}=\overline{h^{*}(H_{2}^{\perp})}
. Notice also that
(\omega_{G})^{*}=(\omega_{G^{*}})^{-1}
for every
G\in\mathsf{LCAGp}
.

Let
G_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}G_{k}
be an inverse system in
\mathsf{CAGp}
,
G
be its inverse limit and
p_{i}\colon G\to G_{i}
be the limit projections. Then
G_{i}^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G_{k}^{*}
is a direct system in
\mathsf{DAGp}
,
G^{*}
is its direct limit and
p_{i}^{*}\colon G_{i}^{*}\to G^{*}
are the limit morphisms. We also recall that for every family
G_{i}
(
i\in I
) of compact abelian groups, there is an isomorphism of groups
(\prod_{i\in I}G_{i})^{*}\cong\bigoplus_{i\in I}G_{i}^{*}
. Finally, if
G_{1},\dots,G_{n}\in\mathsf{LCAGp}
then there is a topological isomorphism
(G_{1}\times\dots\times G_{n})^{*}\cong G_{1}^{*}\times\dots\times G_{n}^{*}
.

Let
G\in\mathsf{LCAGp}
,
H\mathrel{\lhd}G
and consider the corresponding short exact sequence in
\mathsf{LCAGp}

0\longrightarrow H\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\longrightarrow}}G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}G/H\longrightarrow 0,

(2.9)

where
j
is the inclusion morphism and
p
is the quotient morphism. Then the dual sequence

0\longrightarrow(G/H)^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{*}\longrightarrow 0

(2.10)

is also short exact in
\mathsf{LCAGp}
. Moreover,
p^{*}
restricts to a topological isomorphism

p^{*}\colon(G/H)^{*}\to\mathop{\rm im}(p^{*})=H^{\perp}.

Consequently, there is a topological isomorphism
(G/H)^{*}\cong H^{\perp}
and the sequence (
2.10
) takes the form

0\longrightarrow H^{\perp}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma}}{{\longrightarrow}}G^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{*}\longrightarrow 0,

(2.11)

where
\sigma
is the inclusion morphism and
\pi=j^{*}
. In particular, there is a topological isomorphism
G^{*}/H^{\perp}\cong H^{*}
.

Given
G\in\mathsf{LCAGp}
, we use the symbol
\mathcal{L}(G)
to denote the
Lie algebra
of
G
. Recall that
\mathcal{L}(G)=\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathbb{R},G)
is formed by the one-parameter subgroups of
G
. With the compact-open topology and point-wise operations,
\mathcal{L}(G)
is an abelian topological group. The
exponential function

\exp_{G}
of
G
is a morphism of topological groups
\mathcal{L}(G)\to G
, acting by the rule
\exp_{G}(h)=h(1)
. (If no misunderstanding can arise concerning the group
G
, we write
\exp
instead of
\exp_{G}
.) The kernel
\mathcal{K}(G)
of
\exp_{G}
is topologically isomorphic to
\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathbb{T}^{1},G)
; indeed, if
p\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
is the usual covering morphism then
\mathop{\rm Hom}(p,G)\colon\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathbb{T}^{1},G)\ni k\mapsto kp\in\mathcal{K}(G)
is a topological isomorphism.

2.4.3
Connectedness and identity arc-component

Let
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
. Then for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathop{\rm tor}_{k}(G^{*})=(\kappa_{k}(G))^{\perp}
and
\kappa_{k}(G^{*})=\mathop{\rm tor}_{k}(G)^{\perp}
.
Also,
\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\kappa_{k}(G^{*})=\mathop{\rm tor}(G)^{\perp}
and
\mathop{\rm tor}(G^{*})=\mathop{\rm Div}(G)^{\perp}
. (Here,
\mathop{\rm Div}(G)
is always a closed subgroup of
G
, but
\mathop{\rm tor}(G)
need not be closed in
G
.) Consequently,
G
is divisible, respectively, torsion-free if and only if
G^{*}
is torsion-free, respectively, divisible. Further, there are identities

\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\kappa_{k}(G)=\mathop{\rm Div}(G)=G_{0}=\overline{G_{a}}=\overline{\exp(\mathcal{L}(G))},

where
G_{0}
is the identity component of
G
and
G_{a}
is the identity arc-component of
G
. In fact, there is an exact sequence of abelian groups

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{L}(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exp}}{{\longrightarrow}}G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{a}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G/G_{a}\longrightarrow 0,

(2.12)

where
j
is the inclusion morphism and
p_{a}
is the quotient morphism. In particular, the identity arc-component
G_{a}
of
G
consists precisely of the points lying on one-parameter subgroups of
G
. The group
G
is connected, respectively, totally disconnected if and only if its dual group
G^{*}
is torsion-free, respectively, a torsion group. Further, the sequence (
2.12
) is equivalent to the covariant Hom-Ext sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathbb{T}^{1})\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Ext}(G^{*},\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow 0,

derived from the natural short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}^{1}\longrightarrow 0

and associated to the group
G^{*}
. In particular, there is an isomorphism of groups
G/G_{a}\cong\mathop{\rm Ext}(G^{*},\mathbb{Z})
. Thus,
G
is arc-wise connected if and only if
G^{*}
is a Whitehead group. Finally, recall that every epimorphism
q\colon G\to H
of compact abelian groups induces an epimorphism
q\colon G_{a}\to H_{a}
.

2.4.4
Maximal toral quotients

Let
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
be a connected group and let
r=\dim(G)=\mathop{\rm rank}(G^{*})
. Fix a maximal independent set
S\subseteq G^{*}
and let
F=\langle S\rangle
be the subgroup of
G^{*}
generated by
S
. Clearly,
F
is a free abelian group with the basis
S
,
\mathop{\rm rank}(F)=\mathop{\rm card}(S)=r
and
G^{*}/F
is a torsion group. By dualising the corresponding short exact sequence in
\mathsf{DAGp}

0\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow G^{*}\longrightarrow G^{*}/F\longrightarrow 0,

we obtain a short exact sequence in
\mathsf{CAGp}

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{K}(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\longrightarrow}}G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{T}(G)\longrightarrow 0.

(2.13)

Here,
\mathbb{K}(G)\cong(G^{*}/F)^{*}
is totally disconnected and
\mathbb{T}(G)\cong F^{*}\cong\mathbb{T}^{r}
. Clearly,
r
is the largest of all the cardinals
\mathfrak{k}
, such that
G
factors onto
\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{k}}
. Therefore, we shall refer to (
2.13
) as a
maximal toral quotient sequence
of
G
. Finally, if
G,H\in\mathsf{CAGp}
are connected groups and there exist epimorphisms
G\to H\to\mathbb{T}(G)
, then
\dim(G)=\dim(H)
and hence
\mathbb{T}(G)\cong\mathbb{T}(H)
.

2.4.5
Maximal toral subgroup

Let
A\in\mathsf{AbGp}
be a countable torsion-free group. Then
A
decomposes into a direct sum
A=\mathfrak{f}(A)\oplus\mathfrak{t}(A)
, where
\mathfrak{f}(A)
is a free abelian group and the group
\mathfrak{t}(A)
is torsion-less (that is,
\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathfrak{t}(A),\mathbb{Z})=0
or, equivalently,
\mathfrak{t}(A)
contains no non-trivial free abelian group as a direct summand). These ideas translate to the category of compact abelian groups as follows. Let
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
be a connected second countable group. Then there is a topological direct sum
G=\mathfrak{f}(G)\oplus\mathfrak{t}(G)
, where
\mathfrak{f}(G)
is a torus and
\mathfrak{t}(G)
is a torus-free group (that is,
\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathbb{T}^{1},\mathfrak{t}(G))=0
). If, in addition,
X
is a (pointed) topological space and
\xi\colon X\to G
is a continuous (base point preserving) map then
\xi=\mathfrak{f}(\xi)\oplus\mathfrak{t}(\xi)
, where
\mathfrak{f}(\xi)
and
\mathfrak{t}(\xi)
are continuous (base point preserving) maps, obtained as compositions of
\xi
with the projection morphisms from
G
onto
\mathfrak{f}(G)
and
\mathfrak{t}(G)
, respectively.

2.4.6
Bohr compactification

Let
G\in\mathsf{LCAGp}
. The Bohr compactification of
G
consists of a group
bG\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and a topological morphism
p\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,bG)
, such that for every
K\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and every
q\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,K)
there is a unique
r\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(bG,K)
with
rp=q
. The Bohr compactification of
G
can be viewed as a universal element for the covariant functor
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,-)\colon\mathsf{CAGp}\to\mathsf{Sets}
and is therefore unique up to a natural equivalence. We shall use a model of
(bG,p)
defined as follows: let
bG=(G^{*})_{d}^{*}
be the dual group of the discretized dual of
G
and
p=\sigma^{*}\omega_{G}
, where
\omega_{G}
is the Pontryagin isomorphism associated to
G
and
\sigma\colon(G^{*})_{d}\to G^{*}
is the identity morphism. The morphism
p
has a dense image. The group
b\mathbb{Z}
is the universal compact monothetic group and
b\mathbb{R}
is the universal compact solenoidal group. We also have a topological isomorphism
(b\mathbb{R})^{\mathfrak{c}}\cong b\mathbb{R}
.

Further, recall that the initial topology on
G
derived from
bG
and
p
is called the Bohr topology of
G
. It has a basis consisting of the sets of the form
\bigcap_{k=1}^{n}\chi_{k}^{-1}(V_{k})
, where
\chi_{k}\in G^{*}
,
V_{k}\subseteq\mathbb{T}^{1}
are open and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. If the group
G
is compact then
p=\omega_{G}
is a topological isomorphism and so the Bohr topology of
G
coincides with the original topology of
G
. In particular, for every identity neighbourhood
W
in
G
there exist
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\chi_{k}\in G^{*}
and
1\in V_{k}\subseteq\mathbb{T}^{1}
open (
k=1,\dots,n
), such that
\bigcap_{k=1}^{n}\chi_{k}^{-1}(V_{k})\subseteq W
.

2.4.7
Maps into compact abelian groups

Let
X
be a pointed topological space with the base point
z
and
G
be an abelian topological group with the identity
e
. We denote by
C_{z}(X,G)
the set of all continuous maps
f\colon X\to G
with
f(z)=e
. With operations defined point-wise,
C_{z}(X,G)
is an abelian group. If the space
X
is locally compact then we equip the group
C_{z}(X,G)
with the compact-open topology, thus turning it into an abelian topological group. (This topology on
C_{z}(X,G)
is in fact that of a uniform convergence on compact sets.) Every morphism of abelian topological groups
h\colon G\to H
gives rise to a morphism of groups

\widehat{h}\colon C_{z}(X,G)\ni f\mapsto hf\in C_{z}(X,H),

which is topological if the space
X
is locally compact.

For a general pointed space
X
and a compact abelian group
G
, consider the mapping

\varphi\colon C_{z}(X,G)\ni f\mapsto\varphi(f)=f^{*}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})),

(2.14)

where
f^{*}
associates with every
\chi\in G^{*}
the map
\chi f\in C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
. The map
\varphi
is an isomorphism of groups. If the space
X
is locally compact and the group
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1}))
carries the topology of point-wise convergence then
\varphi
is an isomorphism of topological groups.

2.4.8
First cohomotopy group

Let
X
be a pointed locally compact space with the base point
z
and
G
be an abelian topological group. Two maps
f_{1},f_{2}\in C_{z}(X,G)
are homotopic if and only if they are connected by a path in
C_{z}(X,G)
, that is, if and only if
f_{1}-f_{2}
is an element of the identity arc-component
C_{z}(X,G)_{a}
of
C_{z}(X,G)
(we shall call the maps from
C_{z}(X,G)_{a}
null-homotopic). Consequently, the set
[X,G]
of homotopy classes of continuous base point preserving maps
X\to G
coincides with the set of cosets
C_{z}(X,G)/C_{z}(X,G)_{a}
and so it carries the structure of an abelian group. If the space
X
is compact and the group
G
is locally contractible then all maps
f\in C_{z}(X,G)
sufficiently close to the identity
e
are null-homotopic and hence are contained in
C_{z}(X,G)_{a}
. Consequently,
C_{z}(X,G)_{a}
is an open subgroup of
C_{z}(X,G)
and it coincides with the identity component
C_{z}(X,G)_{0}
of
C_{z}(X,G)
. (This applies to the case when
G
is a Lie group and, in particular, when
G=\mathbb{T}^{1}
.)

Now let the space
X
be compact connected. The usual covering morphism
p\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
induces a monomorphism
\widehat{p}\colon C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R})\to C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
. The image
\mathop{\rm im}(\widehat{p})
of
\widehat{p}
coincides with
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})_{a}=C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})_{0}
and it is topologically isomorphic to
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R})
via
\widehat{p}
. Consequently,
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})_{a}
is a divisible group and hence it is a direct summand in
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
. Fix a complementary summand
\pi^{1}(X)
of
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})_{a}
in
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
. Then
\pi^{1}(X)
is isomorphic to the first cohomotopy group
[X,\mathbb{T}^{1}]
of
X
, the isomorphism associating with each map
f\in\pi^{1}(X)
its homotopy class
[f]\in[X,\mathbb{T}^{1}]
. The groups
\pi^{1}(X)
and
[X,\mathbb{T}^{1}]
will often be identified in this work. Recall that
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})_{a}
is an open subgroup of
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
. Therefore, the group
\pi^{1}(X)
is discrete and the splitting
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})=C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})_{a}\oplus\pi^{1}(X)
is topological. Also, since
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
is a torsion-free group, it follows that so is
\pi^{1}(X)
. Finally, if the space
X
is second countable then
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
is a Polish group and so the group
\pi^{1}(X)
is countable.

If
X,Y
are compact connected pointed spaces and
\xi\colon X\to Y
is a continuous base point preserving map then we denote by
\xi^{\flat}
the induced morphism
[Y,\mathbb{T}^{1}]\to[X,\mathbb{T}^{1}]
, acting by the rule
\xi^{\flat}([f])=[f\xi]
. Clearly, the assignment
\xi\mapsto\xi^{\flat}
is contravariant functorial.

2.4.9
Inducing morphisms of the first weak homology groups

Let
X
be a compact connected manifold and
z\in X
. Under the usual identification
H_{1}^{w}(\mathbb{T}^{1})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, there is a morphism of groups

\phi\colon C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})\ni f\mapsto f^{\sharp}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(H_{1}^{w}(X),\mathbb{Z}).

Since
X
has the homotopy type of a CW complex,
\phi
is an epimorphism. The kernel
\ker(\phi)
of
\phi
coincides with the identity arc-component
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})_{a}
of
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
. Consequently,
\phi
restricts to an isomorphism
\phi\colon\pi^{1}(X)\to\mathop{\rm Hom}(H_{1}^{w}(X),\mathbb{Z})
. Since
H_{1}^{w}(X)
is a free abelian group of a finite rank, it follows that

\pi^{1}(X)\cong\mathop{\rm Hom}(H_{1}^{w}(X),\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{1}^{w}(X).

2.4.10
First (co)homotopy of compact abelian groups

Let
X
be a compact connected pointed space with the base point
z
and
G
be a compact abelian group. The topological splitting
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})=C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})_{a}\oplus\pi^{1}(X)
yields a topological splitting

\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1}))=\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})_{a})\oplus\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\pi^{1}(X)).

Since
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})_{a}
is topologically isomorphic to
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R})
and the latter group is the additive topological group of a real Banach space, it follows that
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})_{a})
is an arc-wise connected group. Also, since
\pi^{1}(X)
is a discrete group, it follows that the group
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\pi^{1}(X))
is totally disconnected. From these two observations we obtain that

(1)

the group
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})_{a})
coincides with the identity arc-component of the group
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1}))
.

Further, since the quotient morphism
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})\to[X,\mathbb{T}^{1}]
possesses a cross-section, it follows that

(2)

the induced morphism
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1}))\to\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},[X,\mathbb{T}^{1}])
is an epimorphism.

From statements (1) and (2) it follows that the isomorphism
\varphi
from (
2.14
) gives rise to an isomorphism

\widetilde{\varphi}\colon[X,G]\ni[f]\mapsto\widetilde{\varphi}([f])\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},[X,\mathbb{T}^{1}]),

(2.15)

where
\widetilde{\varphi}([f])
associates with every
\chi\in G^{*}
the homotopy class
[\chi f]\in[X,\mathbb{T}^{1}]
.

Given
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
, there are isomorphisms of groups

\pi_{1}(G)\cong[\mathbb{T}^{1},G]\cong\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathbb{T}^{1},G);

(2.16)

indeed, the first isomorphism is clear, the second one follows from (
2.15
) applied to
X=\mathbb{T}^{1}
and the third one is a consequence of the Pontryagin duality. In fact, every homotopy class from
[\mathbb{T}^{1},G]
contains one and only one morphism from
\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathbb{T}^{1},G)
. It follows, in particular, that the inclusion morphism
\overline{\mathop{\rm tor}(G)}\to G
induces an isomorphism of fundamental groups
\pi_{1}\big(\overline{\mathop{\rm tor}(G)}\big)\to\pi_{1}(G)
. Further, from (
2.16
) we obtain an isomorphism of groups
\mu\colon\pi_{1}(G)\to\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}))
, acting by the rule
\mu(l)\colon\chi\mapsto\chi^{\sharp}(l)
for every
l\in\pi_{1}(G)
; consequently, the morphisms
\chi^{\sharp}\colon\pi_{1}(G)\to\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
(
\chi\in G^{*}
) separate the points of
\pi_{1}(G)
. Finally, let
G,H\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H)
. In Figure
2.4
below we indicate how the identifications from (
2.16
) affect the induced morphism
h^{\sharp}\colon\pi_{1}(G)\to\pi_{1}(H)
.

\begin{CD}\pi_{1}(G)=\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathbb{Z})=\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathbb{T}^{1},G)\\
@V{}V{h^{\sharp}}V@V{}V{\mathop{\rm Hom}(h^{*},\mathbb{Z})}V@V{}V{\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathbb{T}^{1},h)}V\\
\pi_{1}(H)=\mathop{\rm Hom}(H^{*},\mathbb{Z})=\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathbb{T}^{1},H)\end{CD}

Figure 2.4:
Induced morphism of the fundamental groups

If
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
is a connected group then every homotopy class from
[G,\mathbb{T}^{1}]
contains a unique character of
G
. Consequently, there are isomorphisms of groups

\pi^{1}(G)\cong[G,\mathbb{T}^{1}]\cong G^{*}.

Recall the following important corollary of these isomorphisms: two compact connected abelian groups are topologically isomorphic if and only if they are homeomorphic as topological spaces. Finally, if
G,H\in\mathsf{CAGp}
are connected groups and
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H)
then the induced morphism
h^{\flat}\colon\pi^{1}(H)\to\pi^{1}(G)
turns into the Pontryagin dual
h^{*}\colon H^{*}\to G^{*}
of
h
under the identifications
\pi^{1}(G)\cong G^{*}
and
\pi^{1}(H)\cong H^{*}
.

2.4.11
Free compact abelian groups

Let
X
be a pointed topological space with the base point
z
,
G_{X}
be a compact abelian group and
\varphi_{X}\colon X\to G_{X}
be a continuous base point preserving map. We say that
G_{X}
is a
free compact abelian group over
X
with respect to the universal map
\varphi_{X}
, if for every group
H\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and every continuous base point preserving map
\psi\colon X\to H
there is a unique
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G_{X},H)
with
h\varphi_{X}=\psi
. For every
X
, such a pair
(G_{X},\varphi_{X})
is unique up to a natural equivalence. Given a pointed space
X
, let
G_{X}=C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})_{d}^{*}
be the Pontryagin dual to the discretized group
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
and let

\varphi_{X}\colon X\ni x\mapsto\left(\varphi_{X}(x)\colon C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})_{d}\ni f\mapsto f(x)\in\mathbb{T}^{1}\right)\in G_{X};

then
G_{X}
is a free compact abelian group over
X
with respect to the universal map
\varphi_{X}
. Recall that if the space
X
is compact then there is a topological isomorphism

G_{X}\cong\left((\mathbb{Q}_{d})^{*}\right)^{\mathop{\rm w}(X)^{\aleph_{0}}}\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\,\text{prime}\end{subarray}}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{p}\right)^{\mathop{\rm w}(\widetilde{X})-1}\times[X,\mathbb{T}^{1}]^{*},

(2.17)

where
\widetilde{X}
denotes the space of the connected components of
X
.

2.4.12
Projective resolution

A group
P\in\mathsf{CAGp}
is called
projective
if for all
K,H\in\mathsf{CAGp}
, every epimorphism
q\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K,H)
and every
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(P,H)
, there is
k\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(P,K)
with
qk=h
. The projective groups
P\in\mathsf{CAGp}
coincide with the torsion-free elements of
\mathsf{CAGp}
. Given a connected group
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
, consider the injective resolution of
G^{*}
in
\mathsf{DAGp}

0\longrightarrow G^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Q}\otimes G^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})\otimes G^{*}\longrightarrow 0.

By dualising this sequence, we obtain a short exact sequence in
\mathsf{CAGp}

0\longrightarrow\mathfrak{K}(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathfrak{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathfrak{P}(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G\longrightarrow 0.

(2.18)

We shall refer to (
2.18
) as the
projective resolution of
G
. The following conditions hold:

•

\mathfrak{P}(G)
is a projective group and
\mathfrak{p}\colon\mathfrak{P}(G)\to G
is an epimorphism,

•

given a projective group
P\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and an epimorphism
p\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(P,G)
, there is a unique epimorphism
q\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(P,\mathfrak{P}(G))
with
\mathfrak{p}q=p
.

These two conditions are usually expressed by saying that
\mathfrak{p}\colon\mathfrak{P}(G)\to G
is the
projective cover of
G
. Notice that the group
\mathfrak{K}(G)\cong((\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})\otimes G^{*})^{*}
is totally disconnected. Also,

\dim(\mathfrak{P}(G))=\mathop{\rm rank}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes G^{*})=\mathop{\rm rank}(G^{*})=\dim(G).

Finally, if
G,K\in\mathsf{CAGp}
are connected groups and there exist epimorphisms
\mathfrak{P}(G)\to K\to G
, then
\dim(G)=\dim(K)
and hence there is a topological isomorphism
\mathfrak{P}(G)\cong\mathfrak{P}(K)
.

2.4.13
Solenoids

Let
{\bf p}=(p_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of integers greater than
1
and let
p_{0}=1
. We denote by
\mathbb{Z}/{\bf p}=\{k/p_{0}\dots p_{m}:k\in\mathbb{Z},m\geq 0\}
the set of all
\bf{p}
-adic rationals. Clearly,
\mathbb{Z}/{\bf p}
is a non-trivial subgroup of
\mathbb{Q}
and so it has rank
1
. Further, we denote by
S_{{\bf p}}
the
{\bf p}
-adic solenoid

S_{{\bf p}}=\lim_{\longleftarrow}\left(\mathbb{T}^{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\kappa_{p_{n}}}}{{\longleftarrow}}\mathbb{T}^{1}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}.

By definition,
S_{{\bf p}}
is a closed subgroup of
\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{N}}
. For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the symbol
\mathop{\rm pr}_{n}
stands for the projection morphism
S_{{\bf p}}\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
on the
n^{\text{th}}
coordinate. Recall an isomorphism of groups

\lambda\colon\mathbb{Z}/{\bf p}\ni\frac{k}{p_{0}\dots p_{m}}\mapsto\chi_{k,m+1}\in(S_{{\bf p}})^{*},

(2.19)

where
\chi_{k,m+1}
acts by the rule
\chi_{k,m+1}(z)=\mathop{\rm pr}_{m+1}(z)^{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
m\geq 0
and
z\in S_{\bf p}
. Consider the morphism
h\in\mathcal{L}(S_{{\bf p}})
, given by
h(t)=(\exp(i2\pi t/p_{0}\dots p_{n-1}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then every element
g
of
\mathcal{L}(S_{{\bf p}})
is of the form
g(t)=h(st)
for some
s\in\mathbb{R}
and so the identity arc-component
(S_{{\bf p}})_{a}
of
S_{{\bf p}}
equals
(S_{{\bf p}})_{a}=\exp(\mathcal{L}(S_{{\bf p}}))=\mathop{\rm im}(h)
. Consequently,
(S_{\bf p})_{a}\cap\mathop{\rm tor}(S_{\bf p})=e
.

2.4.14
Groups of topological morphisms

Given groups
G,H\in\mathsf{LCAGp}
, denote by
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H)
the set of all topological morphisms
G\to H
. With the point-wise operations and the compact-open topology,
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H)
is an abelian topological group. If
K\mathrel{\lhd}H
then
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,K)\mathrel{\lhd}\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H)
. Given
G,H_{1},\dots,H_{n}\in\mathsf{LCAGp}
, there is a topological isomorphism
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,\prod_{i=1}^{n}H_{i})\cong\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H_{i})
, which associates with every morphism
G\to\prod_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}
the
n
-tuple of its coordinate morphisms
G\to H_{i}
. If, in addition,
K_{i}\mathrel{\lhd}H_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
then this isomorphism gives rise to a topological isomorphism

\mathop{\rm Hom}\left(G,\prod_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}\right)\Big/\mathop{\rm Hom}\left(G,\prod_{i=1}^{n}K_{i}\right)\cong\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H_{i})/\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,K_{i}).

Let
G_{1},G_{2},H\in\mathsf{LCAGp}
and
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G_{1},G_{2})
. Then there is a topological morphism

\mathop{\rm Hom}(h,H)\colon\mathop{\rm Hom}(G_{2},H)\ni\varphi\mapsto\varphi h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G_{1},H).

Clearly, the assignment
h\mapsto\mathop{\rm Hom}(h,H)
is contravariant functorial for every
H\in\mathsf{LCAGp}
. Similarly, given
G,H_{1},H_{2}\in\mathsf{LCAGp}
and
k\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(H_{1},H_{2})
, there is a topological morphism

\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,k)\colon\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H_{1})\ni\varphi\mapsto k\varphi\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H_{2}).

This time, the assignment
k\mapsto\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,k)
is covariant functorial for every
G\in\mathsf{LCAGp}
.

Finally, if
G,H\in\mathsf{LCAGp}
then there is a topological isomorphism
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H)\to\mathop{\rm Hom}(H^{*},G^{*})
, which associates with every
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H)
its Pontryagin dual
h^{*}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(H^{*},G^{*})
.

2.4.15
Fréchet and Banach spaces of morphisms

Our aim in this subsection is to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
A
be a countable abelian group and
B
be a real separable Banach space. Equip the set
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
with the point-wise linear operations and with the topology of point-wise convergence. Then
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
is a real separable Fréchet space. If, in addition, the group
A
is of a finite rank then
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
is a real (separable) Banach space.

Proof.

Since a linear combination of morphisms
A\to B
is again a morphism
A\to B
,
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
is a real linear space. Fix a maximal independent set
S
in
A
. For every
a\in S
, consider the map
\|.\|_{a}\colon\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)\ni h\mapsto\|h(a)\|\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\|.\|
is the norm of
B
. By an elementary argument, all the maps
\|.\|_{a}
are semi-norms on
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
and, for every
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
,
h=0
if and only if
\|h\|_{a}=0
for every
a\in S
. Moreover, by our choice of
S
, for every sequence
(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
and every
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
, the following conditions are equivalent:

•

h_{n}\to h
point-wise on
A
,

•

h_{n}(a)\to h(a)
in
B
for every
a\in S
.

Consequently, the semi-norms
\|.\|_{a}
induce on
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
the topology of point-wise convergence. Finally, the completeness of
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
follows from the completeness of
B
, hence
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
is a real Fréchet space. If, in addition, the group
A
is of a finite rank then the set
S
is finite and so the semi-norms
\|.\|_{a}
provide us with the structure of a real Banach space on
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
.

To finish the proof of the lemma, we must show that
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
is a separable space. Since the group
A
is countable and the Banach space
B
is separable, it follows that the product space
B^{A}
is separable and metrizable. Since
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
is a topological subspace of
B^{A}
by definition, the separability of
\mathop{\rm Hom}(A,B)
now follows.
∎

2.4.16
Hypersemigroups

Let
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and let
2^{G}
be the set of all non-empty closed subsets of
G
. With the operation of the product of sets in
G
,
2^{G}
is an abelian semigroup with an identity
e
. With the Vietoris topology,
2^{G}
is a compact space and, by virtue of Lemma
2.2
and Remark
2.3
below, the semigroup operation on
2^{G}
is continuous. Thus,
2^{G}
is a compact semigroup; we shall call it the
hypersemigroup over

G
. The subset
\mathcal{S}(G)
of
2^{G}
, formed by the closed subgroups of
G
, is a subsemigroup of
2^{G}
. By compactness of
G
,
\mathcal{S}(G)
coincides with the set of idempotents of
2^{G}
; that is
H\in 2^{G}
is an element of
\mathcal{S}(G)
if and only if
HH=H
(this follows from the Weil’s lemma). Consequently,
\mathcal{S}(G)
is a closed subset of
2^{G}
and it is therefore a compact semigroup on its own.

We close this subsection by constructing a basis for the Vietoris topology on
2^{G}
, which involves the group structure of
G
; it will simplify our later considerations. Let us begin by fixing some notation. Given a nonempty open set
U\subseteq G
, we write

\mathcal{N}_{u}(U)=\{K\in 2^{G}:K\subseteq U\}\hskip 8.53581pt\text{and}\hskip 8.53581pt\mathcal{N}_{d}(U)=\{K\in 2^{G}:K\cap U\neq\emptyset\}.

Further, given
K\in 2^{G}
and an identity neighbourhood
V
in
G
, we write

\mathcal{U}_{V}(K)=\{H\in 2^{G}:H\subseteq VK\hskip 5.69054pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69054ptK\subseteq VH\}.

By definition of the Vietoris topology, the sets
\mathcal{N}_{u}(U)
,
\mathcal{N}_{d}(U)
form a subbasis for the topology of
2^{G}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
. Given
K\in 2^{G}
, the sets
\mathcal{U}_{V}(K)
form a local base at
K
in
2^{G}
, when
V
runs through the identity neighbourhoods in
G
.

Remark 2.3
.

It follows at once from the lemma that the semigroup operation on
2^{G}
is continuous. Indeed, given
H,K\in 2^{G}
and an identity neighbourhood
V
in
G
, choose an identity neighbourhood
W
in
G
with
WW\subseteq V
. Then
EF\in\mathcal{U}_{V}(HK)
for all
E\in\mathcal{U}_{W}(H)
and
F\in\mathcal{U}_{W}(K)
.

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

First notice that
K\in\mathcal{U}_{V}(K)
for all
K
and
V
by definition. We verify the following statement:

(
*
)

given
K\in 2^{G}
and a neighbourhood
\mathcal{N}
of
K
in
2^{G}
, there is an identity neighbourhood
V
in
G
with
\mathcal{U}_{V}(K)\subseteq\mathcal{N}
.

Since the inclusion
\mathcal{U}_{V}(K)\cap\mathcal{U}_{W}(K)\supseteq\mathcal{U}_{V\cap W}(K)
holds for all identity neighbourhoods
V,W
in
G
, it suffices to verify (
*
) in the case when
\mathcal{N}
is of the form
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{u}(U)
or
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{d}(U)
for some non-empty open set
U\subseteq G
. Fix such a set
U\subseteq G
. If
K\in\mathcal{N}_{u}(U)
then
VK\subseteq U
for an appropriate identity neighbourhood
V
in
G
; in this case,
\mathcal{U}_{V}(K)\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{u}(U)
. If
K\in\mathcal{N}_{d}(U)
then
gV^{-1}\subseteq U
for an appropriate pair of an identity neighbourhood
V
in
G
and an element
g\in K
; in this case,
\mathcal{U}_{V}(K)\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{d}(U)
.

We shall continue by verifying the following statement:

(
\star
)

given
H,K\in 2^{G}
and an identity neighbourhood
V
in
G
with
H\in\mathcal{U}_{V}(K)
, there is an identity neighbourhood
W
in
G
with
\mathcal{U}_{W}(H)\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{V}(K)
.

By compactness of
H
and
K
, there are identity neighbourhoods
W,N
in
G
with
H\subseteq NK
,
K\subseteq NH
and
WN\subseteq V
. We claim that
W
satisfies the conclusion of (
\star
). So let
C\in\mathcal{U}_{W}(H)
. Then the inclusions
C\subseteq WH
and
H\subseteq NK
yield
C\subseteq WNK\subseteq VK
and, similarly, the inclusions
K\subseteq NH
and
H\subseteq WC
yield
K\subseteq NWC\subseteq VC
. Thus,
C\in\mathcal{U}_{V}(K)
, as was to be shown.

We finish the proof of the lemma by showing that all the sets
\mathcal{U}_{V}(K)
are open in
2^{G}
. In view of (
\star
), it suffices to verify the following statement:

(
\circ
)

given
K\in 2^{G}
and an identity neighbourhood
V
in
G
, there is a neighbourhood
\mathcal{N}
of
K
in
2^{G}
with
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{V}(K)
.

Fix an identity neighbourhood
W
in
G
with
WW\subseteq V
and points
g_{1},\dots,g_{n}\in K
with
K\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}W
. Set
U=VK
and
U_{i}=g_{i}W^{-1}
for
i=1,\dots,n
. An elementary argument shows that

K\in\mathcal{N}_{u}(U)\cap\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{N}_{d}(U_{i})\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{V}(K).

Since the set
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{u}(U)\cap\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{N}_{d}(U_{i})
is open in
2^{G}
, this verifies statement (
\circ
).
∎

2.4.17
Group-disjointness

Let
H_{1},H_{2}\in\mathsf{CAGp}
. For the purpose of this work we call
H_{1},H_{2}

group-disjoint
and write
H_{1}\perp H_{2}
, if
H_{1}\times H_{2}
is the only closed subgroup of
H_{1}\times H_{2}
with full projections onto both
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
. This relation is symmetric and saturated with respect to topological isomorphisms. (Our terminology is influenced by the concept of disjointness in topological dynamics: two minimal flows with a common acting group are called disjoint if their product contains no proper subsystem with full both projections. In terms of universal algebra, the groups
H_{1},H_{2}
are group-disjoint if and only if the set of all their topological subdirect products consists of their direct product alone.) We shall use the following characterization of group-disjointness.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
H_{1},H_{2}\in\mathsf{CAGp}
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

1.

H_{1},H_{2}
are group-disjoint,

2.

H_{1},H_{2}
have no non-trivial common quotient group,

3.

H_{1}^{*},H_{2}^{*}
have no non-trivial common subgroup up to isomorphism,

4.

\mathop{\rm ord}(\chi_{1})\neq\mathop{\rm ord}(\chi_{2})
for all
1\neq\chi_{1}\in H_{1}^{*}
and
1\neq\chi_{2}\in H_{2}^{*}
.

Remark 2.5
.

We wish to mention the following facts.

•

From part (4) of the lemma it follows that every family
\mathcal{A}
, consisting of pair-wise group-disjoint non-trivial compact abelian groups, is at most countable. To see this, fix
\chi_{H}\in H^{*}\setminus 1
for every
H\in\mathcal{A}
and consider the function
\mathcal{A}\ni H\mapsto\mathop{\rm ord}\left(\chi_{H}\right)\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. By part (4) of the lemma, this function is injective. Thus, indeed, the family
\mathcal{A}
is at most countable.

•

By part (2) of the lemma, the following statement holds: if
H_{i},G_{i}\in\mathsf{CAGp}
(
i=1,2
),
H_{1}\perp H_{2}
and there are epimorphisms
q_{i}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(H_{i},G_{i})
(
i=1,2
), then
G_{1}\perp G_{2}
.

•

Recall that if a group
H\in\mathsf{CAGp}
is not totally disconnected then there is
\chi\in H^{*}
with
\mathop{\rm ord}(\chi)=\infty
. Thus, by part (4) of the lemma, if
H_{i}
(
i\in I
) is a family of pair-wise group-disjoint groups from
\mathsf{CAGp}
then there is at most one
i\in I
such that the group
H_{i}
is not totally disconnected.

Proof of Lemma
2.4
.

To verify implication (1)
\Rightarrow
(2), fix a group
K\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and epimorphisms
p_{1}\colon H_{1}\to K
,
p_{2}\colon H_{2}\to K
. Consider the set

H=\left\{(h_{1},h_{2})\in H_{1}\times H_{2}:p_{1}(h_{1})=p_{2}(h_{2})\right\}.

Clearly,
H
is a closed subgroup of
H_{1}\times H_{2}
. Since both
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are epimorphisms,
H
has full projections onto both
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
. It follows by group-disjointness of
H_{1},H_{2}
that
H=H_{1}\times H_{2}
and so the group
K
is trivial.

To verify implication (2)
\Rightarrow
(3), assume that
H_{1},H_{2}
have no common quotient group other than the trivial one and that
D
is a common subgroup of
H_{1}^{*},H_{2}^{*}
up to isomorphism. Fix the corresponding monomorphisms
l_{1}\colon D\to H_{1}^{*}
and
l_{2}\colon D\to H_{2}^{*}
. Their dual morphisms
l_{1}^{*}\colon H_{1}\to D^{*}
,
l_{2}^{*}\colon H_{2}\to D^{*}
are epimorphisms and hence, by virtue of (2),
D^{*}
is trivial. It follows that
D
is also trivial, which verifies (3).

To show that (3)
\Rightarrow
(4), assume that
H_{1}^{*}
,
H_{2}^{*}
have no non-trivial common subgroup up to isomorphism and let
\chi_{1}\in H_{1}^{*}
,
\chi_{2}\in H_{2}^{*}
be non-trivial characters of
H_{1},H_{2}
, respectively. By our assumptions, the cyclic subgroups
\langle\chi_{1}\rangle
and
\langle\chi_{2}\rangle
of
H_{1}^{*}
and
H_{2}^{*}
generated by
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
, respectively, are not isomorphic. This means that
\mathop{\rm ord}(\chi_{1})\neq\mathop{\rm ord}(\chi_{2})
, which verifies (4).

We finish the proof by verifying implication (4)
\Rightarrow
(1). To this end, assume that
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
are not group-disjoint and fix a proper closed subgroup
H
of
H_{1}\times H_{2}
with full projections onto
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
. Choose
\Upsilon\in[H^{\perp},(H_{1}\times H_{2})^{*}]\setminus 1
; clearly,
\Upsilon
is of the form
\Upsilon(h_{1},h_{2})=\chi_{1}(h_{1})\chi_{2}(h_{2})^{-1}
for appropriate characters
\chi_{1}\in H_{1}^{*}
and
\chi_{2}\in H_{2}^{*}
. Since
H
has full projections onto both
H_{1},H_{2}
and
\Upsilon
vanishes on
H
, we have
\mathop{\rm im}(\chi_{1})=\mathop{\rm im}(\chi_{2})
. It follows, finally, that both
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
are non-trivial and
\mathop{\rm ord}(\chi_{1})=\mathop{\rm ord}(\chi_{2})
.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
G_{1},\dots,G_{n},H_{1},\dots,H_{m}\in\mathsf{CAGp}
be pair-wise group-disjoint. Then
G_{1}\times\dots\times G_{n}
and
H_{1}\times\dots\times H_{m}
are also group-disjoint.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.4
(3), we need to show that
G_{1}^{*}\times\dots\times G_{n}^{*}
and
H_{1}^{*}\times\dots\times H_{m}^{*}
have no non-trivial common subgroup up to isomorphism. We shall proceed by contradiction; let
D
be a non-trivial group and let
\mu\colon D\to G_{1}^{*}\times\dots\times G_{n}^{*}
and
\nu\colon D\to H_{1}^{*}\times\dots\times H_{m}^{*}
be monomorphisms. By the third part of Remark
2.5
, at most one of the groups
G_{1},\dots,G_{n},H_{1},\dots,H_{m}
is not totally disconnected. Hence,
D
is a torsion group. Fix
d\in D
and
p\in\mathbb{N}
prime with
\mathop{\rm ord}(d)=p
. Write
\mu(d)=(\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{n})
and
\nu(d)=(\Upsilon_{1},\dots,\Upsilon_{m})
, and choose
i
and
j
so that
\chi_{i}\neq 1
and
\Upsilon_{j}\neq 1
. Since
\chi_{i}\neq 1
,
\chi_{i}^{p}=1
and
p
is prime, we have
\mathop{\rm ord}(\chi_{i})=p
. A similar argument yields
\mathop{\rm ord}(\Upsilon_{j})=p
. By Lemma
2.4
(4), it follows that
G_{i}
and
H_{j}
are not group-disjoint, a contradiction.
∎

2.5
Minimal flows and group extensions

Our references in this section are monographs
[
2
]
,
[
7
]
,
[
15
]
,
[
28
]
and
[
56
]
.

2.5.1
Minimal flows

By a
flow

\mathcal{F}
we mean a representation of a topological group
\Gamma
as a group of homeomorphisms on a topological space
X
. We write
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
and always require that the corresponding action
\Gamma\times X\to X
be jointly continuous. The acting homeomorphisms (also called the transition maps) of
\mathcal{F}
are denoted by
T_{\gamma}
(
\gamma\in\Gamma
). It is often assumed that
X
is a pointed space with the base point
z
; in such a situation we may consider the motion map

\mathcal{F}_{z}=\mathcal{F}(-,z)\colon\Gamma\ni\gamma\mapsto\mathcal{F}(\gamma,z)=T_{\gamma}z\in X.

Since
\mathcal{F}_{z}(1)=z
,
\mathcal{F}_{z}
is a (continuous) base point preserving map.

Given
x\in X
, we write
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}}(x)=\{T_{\gamma}x:\gamma\in\Gamma\}
for the
\mathcal{F}
-orbit of
x
and
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}}(x)
for the closure of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}}(x)
in
X
. The flow
\mathcal{F}
is called
minimal
if all its orbits are dense. A point
x\in X
is
free
for
\mathcal{F}
if it has a trivial isotropy group, that is, if
T_{\gamma}x=x
implies
\gamma=1
. The flow
\mathcal{F}
is called
point-free
, respectively,
free
if the set of its free points is nonempty, respectively, the whole
X
.

2.5.2
Topological freeness and free cycles

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a flow with
\Gamma\in\mathsf{LieGp}
connected and with
X
a compact connected manifold. Fix
z\in X
and consider the morphism
\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}\colon H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)\to H_{1}^{w}(X)
induced by the map
\mathcal{F}_{z}
. The image
\mathop{\rm im}(\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp})
of
\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}
is denoted by
H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})
. We say that the flow
\mathcal{F}

•

is
topologically free
if
\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}\colon H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)\to H_{1}^{w}(X)
is a monomorphism (equivalently, if the restriction
\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}\colon H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)\to H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})
is an isomorphism),

•

possesses a free cycle
if
\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F}))<\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(X))
.

We write
n=\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F}))
and
n+m=\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(X))
. Also,
d_{1},\dots,d_{n}
denote the elementary divisors of
H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})
in
H_{1}^{w}(X)
(written, as usual, in the non-decreasing order). The following lemma describes what happens if the base point
z
of the phase space
X
of
\mathcal{F}
is changed.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a flow with
\Gamma\in\mathsf{LieGp}
connected and with
X
a compact connected manifold. Fix
z,z^{\prime}\in X
and let
f
be a path in
X
from
z
to
z^{\prime}
. Then the diagram in Figure
2.5
below commutes. In particular, we have

\widehat{f}\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}=\mathcal{F}_{z^{\prime}}^{\sharp}\colon H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)\to H_{1}^{w}(X,z^{\prime}).

{{\displaystyle\pi_{1}(\Gamma)}}

{{\displaystyle\pi_{1}(X,z)}}

{{\displaystyle\pi_{1}(X,z^{\prime})}}

{{\displaystyle H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)}}

{{\displaystyle H_{1}^{w}(X,z)}}

{{\displaystyle H_{1}^{w}(X,z^{\prime})}}

{{\displaystyle p_{\Gamma}}}

{{\displaystyle p_{X,z}}}

{{\displaystyle p_{X,z^{\prime}}}}

{{\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}}}

{{\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}}}

{{\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{z^{\prime}}^{\sharp}}}

{{\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{z^{\prime}}^{\sharp}}}

{{\displaystyle\widehat{f}}}

{{\displaystyle\widehat{f}}}

Figure 2.5:
Changing the base point
z
of the phase space
X

Remark 2.8
.

Recall from Subsection
2.3.2
that
\widehat{f}\colon H_{1}^{w}(X,z)\to H_{1}^{w}(X,z^{\prime})
is an isomorphism of groups. It follows, in particular, that the concepts and quantities introduced in the paragraph preceding Lemma
2.7
do not depend on the choice of a base point of
X
.

Proof of Lemma
2.7
.

The commutativity of the three vertical squares in Figure
2.5
follows from our discussion in Subsection
2.3.2
. Thus, since
p_{\Gamma}
is an epimorphism, it suffices now to verify the commutativity of the upper horizontal triangle. To this end, fix
g\in\pi_{1}(\Gamma)
and consider the constant loops
c_{1}
,
c_{z}
,
c_{z^{\prime}}
at
1\in\Gamma
,
z,z^{\prime}\in X
, respectively. Then

\begin{split}\widehat{f}\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}(g)&=\overline{f}*\mathcal{F}_{z}g*f=\mathcal{F}(c_{1},\overline{f})*\mathcal{F}(g,c_{z})*\mathcal{F}(c_{1},f)\\
&=\mathcal{F}^{\sharp}\left((c_{1},\overline{f})*(g,c_{z})*(c_{1},f)\right)=\mathcal{F}^{\sharp}\left((c_{1}*g*c_{1},\overline{f}*c_{z}*f)\right)\\
&=\mathcal{F}^{\sharp}\left((g,c_{z^{\prime}})\right)=\mathcal{F}_{z^{\prime}}^{\sharp}(g),\end{split}

as was to be shown.
∎

2.5.3
Freeness versus topological freeness

We shall often assume in this work that the considered minimal flow
\mathcal{F}
is (point-)free or topologically free. The freeness will be required when applying results from
[
9
]
, while topological freeness will be assumed when using techniques developed in this work. There is a formal analogy between the definitions of these two properties:

•

a flow
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
is free if
\mathcal{F}_{z}\colon\Gamma\to X
is an injective map for every
z\in X
,

•

a flow
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
is topologically free if
\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}\colon H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)\to H_{1}^{w}(X)
is a monomorphism.

Despite this formal analogy, these two properties are independent for minimal flows, as we now show.

To see that topological freeness does not imply freeness, let
\Gamma\in\mathsf{LieGp}
be a simply connected group and let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be a non-trivial proper closed co-compact subgroup of
\Gamma
. Consider the natural action
\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X
of
\Gamma
on
X=\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime}
. Then
X
is a compact connected manifold and
\mathcal{F}
is a minimal
\Gamma
-flow on
X
. Since the group
\Gamma^{\prime}
is non-trivial, the flow
\mathcal{F}
is not free; actually,
\mathcal{F}
possesses no free points whatsoever and so it is not even point-free. On the other hand, since the group
\Gamma
is simply connected, we have
H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)=0
and so the induced morphism
\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}\colon H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)\to H_{1}^{w}(X)
is a monomorphism. Thus, the flow
\mathcal{F}
is topologically free.

Now we want to show that freeness does not imply topological freeness; this is done in Proposition
2.9
below. Let us start with some auxiliary observations. We shall consider the usual transitive action of the second unitary group
\text{U}(2)
on the
3
-sphere
\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Recall that the center
Z(\text{U}(2))
of
\text{U}(2)
consists of the matrices
A_{\zeta}=\zeta\mathbb{I}
, where
\mathbb{I}
is the unit of
\text{U}(2)
and
\zeta\in\mathbb{T}^{1}
. Thus,
AA_{\zeta}=A_{\zeta}A
for all
A\in\text{U}(2)
and
\zeta\in\mathbb{T}^{1}
. Notice also that the action of
\text{U}(2)
on
\mathbb{S}^{3}
is effective and that the corresponding restricted action of
Z(\text{U}(2))
on
\mathbb{S}^{3}
is free. It follows, in particular, that we may regard
\text{U}(2)
as a subgroup of the group of all homeomorphisms on
\mathbb{S}^{3}
.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
X
be a compact connected manifold with dimension at least
2
. If
X
admits a minimal continuous flow
\phi\colon\mathbb{R}\curvearrowright X
then there is a free minimal flow
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}\curvearrowright X\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
with
H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})=0
.

Remark 2.10
.

The flow
\mathcal{F}
from the proposition is not topologically free. Indeed, since
H_{1}^{w}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{1})\cong\mathbb{Z}
and
H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})=0
, the induced morphism

\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}\colon H_{1}^{w}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{1})\to H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})\subseteq H_{1}^{w}(X\times\mathbb{S}^{3})

is not a monomorphism.

Proof of Proposition
2.9
.

We shall proceed in four steps.

1st step.
We construct an auxiliary minimal flow
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.

Let
\varphi_{t}
(
t\in\mathbb{R}
) be the acting homeomorphisms of
\phi
. Since the flow
\phi
is minimal and the dimension of
X
is at least
2
, it follows that
\phi
is a free flow. Further, since
\text{U}(2)
is a compact connected Lie group, it is Polish and arc-wise connected. Finally, since the action of
\text{U}(2)
on
\mathbb{S}^{3}
is transitive and hence minimal, we may use
[
9
, Theorem 8]
to find a minimal skew product
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{R}\curvearrowright X\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
over
\phi
, whose fibre maps lie in the group
\text{U}(2)
. That is, for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the
t
-transition
T_{t}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
has the form
T_{t}^{\prime}(x,y)=(\varphi_{t}x,A_{(t,x)}y)
, where
A_{(t,x)}\in\text{U}(2)
for every
x\in X
.

2nd step.
We construct the desired minimal flow
\mathcal{F}
.

Given
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\zeta\in\mathbb{T}^{1}
, consider the map

T_{(t,\zeta)}\colon X\times\mathbb{S}^{3}\ni(x,y)\mapsto\left(\varphi_{t}x,A_{\zeta}A_{(t,x)}y\right)\in X\times\mathbb{S}^{3}.

Since
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{R}\curvearrowright X\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
is a flow and
A_{\zeta}\in Z(\text{U}(2))
for every
\zeta\in\mathbb{T}^{1}
, it follows that the maps
T_{(t,\zeta)}
constitute an action
\mathcal{F}
of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}
on
X\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Clearly,
\mathcal{F}
is continuous as a map

\mathcal{F}\colon(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{1})\times(X\times\mathbb{S}^{3})\to X\times\mathbb{S}^{3}

and so it is a flow. The minimality of
\mathcal{F}
follows immediately from the minimality of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.

3rd step.
We show that the flow
\mathcal{F}
is free.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\zeta\in\mathbb{T}^{1}
,
x\in X
,
y\in\mathbb{S}^{3}
and assume that
T_{(t,\zeta)}(x,y)=(x,y)
; we show that
t=0
and
\zeta=1
. Since
x=\varphi_{t}x
and the flow
\phi
is free, we get
t=0
and hence
y=A_{\zeta}A_{(t,x)}y=A_{\zeta}y
. Since
Z(\text{U}(2))
acts freely on
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, it follows that
\zeta=1
, as was to be shown.

4th step.
We show that
H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})=0
.

Choose a base point
z=(x_{0},y_{0})
for
X\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Consider the topological morphism
j\colon\mathbb{T}^{1}\ni\zeta\mapsto(0,\zeta)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}
. Since
j
is a homotopy equivalence, the induced morphism
j^{\sharp}\colon H_{1}^{w}(\mathbb{T}^{1})\to H_{1}^{w}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{1})
is an isomorphism of groups. Consequently,

H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}\left(H_{1}^{w}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{1})\right)=\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}j^{\sharp}\left(H_{1}^{w}(\mathbb{T}^{1})\right)=(\mathcal{F}_{z}j)^{\sharp}\left(H_{1}^{w}(\mathbb{T}^{1})\right).

(2.20)

Further, by definition of
\mathcal{F}
, the map
\mathcal{F}_{z}j
takes its values in
x_{0}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Since the latter is a simply connected space, we have
(\mathcal{F}_{z}j)^{\sharp}(H_{1}^{w}(\mathbb{T}^{1}))=0
. In view of (
2.20
), this means that
H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})=0
, as was to be shown.
∎

2.5.4
Remarks on topological freeness and free cycles

Our definition of topological freeness and of the existence of a free cycle for a minimal flow
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
involves the induced morphism
\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}
between the first weak homology groups of
\Gamma
and
X
. In this subsection we link these two properties of
\mathcal{F}
to the first and the second homotopy group of the quotient space
X/K
of
X
by the maximal compact subgroup
K
of
\Gamma
. This is intended as an informal discussion and we shall not use the results of this subsection in the rest of this work.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow with
\Gamma\in\mathsf{LieGp}
connected and with
X
a compact connected manifold. We shall assume that
\mathcal{F}
is a free flow. Fix a maximal compact subgroup
K
of
\Gamma
. Recall that
K
is itself a connected Lie group; consequently, the groups
\pi_{2}(K)
and
\pi_{0}(K)=K/K_{0}
both vanish, while the group
\pi_{1}(K)
is abelian. Fix a base point
z
for
X
and consider the restriction
j=\mathcal{F}_{z}|_{K}\colon K\to X
of
\mathcal{F}_{z}
. Further, let
p\colon X\to X/K
be the canonical projection. We shall work under the assumption that
X
is a locally trivial fibre bundle over
X/K
with the fibre
K
; since
K
acts freely on
X
by our assumptions, this is the case if both
X
and the action of
K
on
X
are smooth. Consequently, by
[
20
, Section 9.8]
, we have the corresponding long exact homotopy sequence of the bundle

0\longrightarrow\pi_{2}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p^{\sharp}_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{2}(X/K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}(K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j^{\sharp}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p^{\sharp}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{1}(X/K)\longrightarrow 0,

(2.21)

where
p^{\sharp}_{2}
,
p^{\sharp}_{1}
and
j^{\sharp}_{1}
stand for the morphisms induced by
p
and
j
, respectively, and
\partial
is the connecting boundary morphism. To simplify the situation, we shall further assume that the space
X
has abelian fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X)
. (Though certainly restrictive, this assumption still leaves us a large class of manifolds
X
at our disposal.) By the exactness of (
2.21
) at
\pi_{1}(X/K)
, this yields also the commutativity of
\pi_{1}(X/K)
.

Since
\pi_{1}(K)
,
\pi_{1}(X)
and
\pi_{1}(X/K)
are finitely generated abelian groups, each of them contains its torsion subgroup as a direct summand and the corresponding complementary summand is the first weak homology group of
K
,
X
and
X/K
, respectively. Consequently, the diagram in Figure
2.6
below commutes and its upper row is an exact sequence by the exactness of (
2.21
).

\begin{CD}H_{1}^{w}(K)\oplus\tau_{K}@>{j^{\sharp}_{1}}>{}>H_{1}^{w}(X)\oplus\tau_{X}@>{p^{\sharp}_{1}}>{}>H_{1}^{w}(X/K)\oplus\tau_{X/K}@>{}>{}>0\\
@V{\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}}V{}V@V{\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}}V{}V@V{\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}}V{}V\\
H_{1}^{w}(K)@>{}>{j^{\sharp}}>H_{1}^{w}(X)@>{}>{p^{\sharp}}>H_{1}^{w}(X/K)\end{CD}

Figure 2.6:
Remarks on topological freeness and free cycles

(For typographical reasons, we write
\tau_{K}
,
\tau_{X}
and
\tau_{X/K}
for the torsion subgroups of
\pi_{1}(K)
,
\pi_{1}(X)
and
\pi_{1}(X/K)
, respectively.) We denote by
c
and
d
the largest torsion coefficients of the groups
\tau_{X}
and
\tau_{X/K}
, respectively. Notice also that
p^{\sharp}
is an epimorphism, since
p_{1}^{\sharp}
is an epimorphism.

Now, the flow
\mathcal{F}
is topologically free, by definition, if and only if the induced morphism
\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}\colon H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)\to H_{1}^{w}(X)
is a monomorphism. Since the inclusion morphism
K\to\Gamma
induces an isomorphism
H_{1}^{w}(K)\to H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)
, this occurs if and only if the induced morphism
j^{\sharp}\colon H_{1}^{w}(K)\to H_{1}^{w}(X)
is a monomorphism. By commutativity of the diagram in Figure
2.6
and by the exactness of (
2.21
) at
\pi_{1}(K)
, we have
c\ker(j^{\sharp})\subseteq\ker(j_{1}^{\sharp})=\mathop{\rm im}(\partial)
. Thus, since
H_{1}^{w}(K)\supseteq\ker(j^{\sharp})
is a torsion-free group,

(
*
)

the flow
\mathcal{F}
