is topologically free, provided
\pi_{2}(X/K)
is a torsion group
.

Since the morphism
p_{2}^{\sharp}
from (
2.21
) is a monomorphism, in order that
\pi_{2}(X/K)
be a torsion group it is necessary that
\pi_{2}(X)
be a torsion group.

Further, recall that the flow
\mathcal{F}
possesses a free cycle, by definition, if and only if
\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F}))<\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(X))
, where
H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)=\mathop{\rm im}(j^{\sharp})
. An elementary argument involving properties of the diagram in Figure
2.6
yields
d\ker(p^{\sharp})\subseteq\mathop{\rm im}(j^{\sharp})
and
c\mathop{\rm im}(j^{\sharp})\subseteq\ker(p^{\sharp})
. Consequently,

\mathop{\rm rank}(\ker(p^{\sharp}))=\mathop{\rm rank}(\mathop{\rm im}(j^{\sharp}))=\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F}))

and so the following conditions are equivalent:

•

\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F}))<\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(X))
,

•

\mathop{\rm rank}(\ker(p^{\sharp}))<\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(X))
,

•

\beta(H_{1}^{w}(X/K))\neq 0
.

Thus, since
\beta(H_{1}^{w}(X/K))=\beta(\pi_{1}(X/K))
,

(
*
)

the flow
\mathcal{F}
possesses a free cycle if and only if
\beta(\pi_{1}(X/K))\neq 0
.

2.5.5
Freeness and topological freeness - an implication

We have seen in Subsection
2.5.3
that freeness and topological freeness of a minimal flow are in general independent properties. Our aim in this subsection is to show that, after all, there does exist a non-trivial relationship between these two properties for a special class of flows; this is done in Proposition
2.12
below. Before formulating the result, let us describe the considered situation.

Let
\Gamma,G\in\mathsf{LieGp}
be connected Lie groups,
G
compact, and let
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\Gamma,G)
be a topological morphism. Consider the equicontinuous
\Gamma
-flow
\mathcal{F}
on
G
induced by
h
:

\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\times G\ni(\gamma,g)\mapsto h(\gamma)g\in G.

(2.22)

The following statements hold obviously:

•

\mathcal{F}
is minimal if and only if the image
\mathop{\rm im}(h)
of
h
is dense in
G
,

•

\mathcal{F}
is free if and only if
h
is a monomorphism.

We choose the identity
z=e
as the base point of
G
; this leads to
\mathcal{F}_{z}=h
.

Remark 2.11
.

Given a connected Lie group
\Gamma
, there may not exist a pair of a compact Lie group
G
and a monomorphism
h\colon\Gamma\to G
with a dense image. Indeed, since every compact Lie group is topologically isomorphic to a subgroup of the
n^{\text{th}}
unitary group
\text{U}(n)
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, a necessary and sufficient condition for the existence of such a pair
G,h
is that
\Gamma
possess a faithful finite-dimensional unitary representation; in fact, the group
\Gamma
must possess a non-trivial one-dimensional unitary representation, that is, a non-trivial character
\Gamma\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
. (This last claim can be proved by using results from the structure theory of compact (Lie) groups, but we may also refer to Remark
5.29
.) This is not the case, for instance, when
\Gamma=\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is the second real special linear group, since all its non-trivial unitary representations are infinite-dimensional.

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
\Gamma
,
G
,
h
and
\mathcal{F}
be as above. Assume that
h
is a monomorphism with a dense image, so that the flow
\mathcal{F}
is free and minimal. Then
\mathcal{F}
is also topologically free. Further, if
\Gamma_{t}
and
G_{t}
are maximal tori in
\Gamma
and
G
, respectively, then

\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(G))-\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F}))=\dim(G_{t})-\dim(\Gamma_{t}).

(2.23)

Finally, if
h
is not an isomorphism onto
G
then the flow
\mathcal{F}
possesses a free cycle.

Remark 2.13
.

We wish to add the following remarks.

•

Interestingly, the freeness of the flow
\mathcal{F}
alone does not secure the topological freeness of
\mathcal{F}
; without the assumption that
\mathcal{F}
is minimal, the proposition is no longer true. To see this, let
\Gamma=\mathbb{T}^{2}
,
G=\text{U}(2)
and consider the monomorphism
h
defined by

h\colon\mathbb{T}^{2}\ni(\zeta,\xi)\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\zeta&0\\
0&\xi\end{array}\right)\in\text{U}(2).

The corresponding flow
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathbb{T}^{2}\curvearrowright\text{U}(2)
is free but not minimal. Moreover, since
H_{1}^{w}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
H_{1}^{w}(\text{U}(2))\cong\mathbb{Z}
, the induced morphism
\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}=h^{\sharp}\colon H_{1}^{w}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\to H_{1}^{w}(\text{U}(2))
is not a monomorphism and so the flow
\mathcal{F}
is not topologically free.

•

The dimensions on the right-hand side of (
2.23
) are the topological (that is, manifold) dimensions. Recall that any two maximal tori in a given Lie group are conjugate. Hence,
\dim(\Gamma_{t})
and
\dim(G_{t})
do not depend on the choice of
\Gamma_{t}
and
G_{t}
, respectively.

Proof of Proposition
2.12
.

Let
h_{1}^{\sharp}\colon\pi_{1}(\Gamma)\to\pi_{1}(G)
and
h^{\sharp}\colon H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)\to H_{1}^{w}(G)
be the morphisms induced by
h
. By virtue of
[
49
, Theorem 1.9]
, the following statements hold:

(a)

h_{1}^{\sharp}
is a monomorphism,

(b)

the quotient group
\pi_{1}(G)/\mathop{\rm im}(h_{1}^{\sharp})
is free abelian with rank

\mathop{\rm rank}\left(\pi_{1}(G)/\mathop{\rm im}(h_{1}^{\sharp})\right)=\dim(G_{t})-\dim(\Gamma_{t}),

(c)

h
is an isomorphism onto
G
if and only if
\dim(G_{t})=\dim(\Gamma_{t})
.

Let
\tau_{\Gamma}
and
\tau_{G}
be the torsion subgroups of
\pi_{1}(\Gamma)
and
\pi_{1}(G)
, respectively. Since both
\pi_{1}(\Gamma)
and
\pi_{1}(G)
are finitely generated abelian groups, they contain
\tau_{\Gamma}
and
\tau_{G}
as direct summands with the complementary summands
H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)
and
H_{1}^{w}(G)
, respectively. Moreover, the diagram in Figure
2.7
commutes.

\begin{CD}H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)\oplus\tau_{\Gamma}@>{h^{\sharp}_{1}}>{}>H_{1}^{w}(G)\oplus\tau_{G}\\
@V{\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}}V{}V@V{}V{\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}}V\\
H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)@>{}>{h^{\sharp}}>H_{1}^{w}(G)\end{CD}

Figure 2.7:
Freeness and topological freeness - an implication

Let
d
stand for the largest torsion coefficient of
\tau_{G}
. An elementary argument involving commutativity of the diagram in Figure
2.7
shows that
d\ker(h^{\sharp})\subseteq\ker(h_{1}^{\sharp})
. Since
\ker(h_{1}^{\sharp})=0
by virtue of (a) and the group
H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)
is torsion-free, it follows that
\ker(h^{\sharp})=0
and so
\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}=h^{\sharp}
is a monomorphism. Thus,
\mathcal{F}
is a topologically free flow.

Now we turn to a proof of (
2.23
). First, we claim that

\mathop{\rm im}(h_{1}^{\sharp})=\mathop{\rm im}(h^{\sharp})\oplus\tau_{G}.

(2.24)

The inclusion “
\subseteq
” follows from the commutativity of the diagram in Figure
2.7
and from the inclusion
h_{1}^{\sharp}(\tau_{\Gamma})\subseteq\tau_{G}
. We verify the inclusion “
\supseteq
”. First, observe that
\tau_{G}\subseteq\mathop{\rm im}(h_{1}^{\sharp})
, for the quotient group
\pi_{1}(G)/\mathop{\rm im}(h_{1}^{\sharp})
is torsion-free by virtue of (b). Further, since also
\mathop{\rm im}(h^{\sharp})\subseteq\mathop{\rm im}(h_{1}^{\sharp})+\tau_{G}
, it follows that
\mathop{\rm im}(h_{1}^{\sharp})=\mathop{\rm im}(h_{1}^{\sharp})+\tau_{G}\supseteq\mathop{\rm im}(h^{\sharp})
. Thus,
\mathop{\rm im}(h^{\sharp})\oplus\tau_{G}\subseteq\mathop{\rm im}(h_{1}^{\sharp})
, which finishes the proof of (
2.24
).

From (
2.24
) we obtain

\begin{split}H_{1}^{w}(G)/H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})&=H_{1}^{w}(G)/\mathop{\rm im}(h^{\sharp})\cong\left(H_{1}^{w}(G)\oplus\tau_{G}\right)/\left(\mathop{\rm im}(h^{\sharp})\oplus\tau_{G}\right)\\
&=\pi_{1}(G)/\mathop{\rm im}(h_{1}^{\sharp}).\end{split}

Consequently, by virtue of (b),

\begin{split}\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(G))-\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F}))&=\beta\left(H_{1}^{w}(G)/H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})\right)\\
&=\beta\left(\pi_{1}(G)/\mathop{\rm im}(h_{1}^{\sharp})\right)\\
&=\dim(G_{t})-\dim(\Gamma_{t}),\end{split}

which verifies (
2.23
). Finally, the last statement of the proposition follows from (
2.23
) and from (c).
∎

The following corollary generalizes Proposition
2.12
to a larger class of minimal equicontinuous flows.

Corollary 2.14
.

Let
\Gamma,G\in\mathsf{LieGp}
be connected,
G
compact,
h\colon\Gamma\to G
be a monomorphism with a dense image and
H
be a closed subgroup of
G
. Consider the minimal equicontinuous flow

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\colon\Gamma\times G/H\ni(\gamma,gH)\mapsto h(\gamma)gH\in G/H.

Assume that the group
H
is connected semi-simple and that
h
is not an isomorphism onto
G
. Then the flow
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is topologically free and possesses a free cycle.

Remark 2.15
.

We wish to add the following remarks.

•

Recall that a compact connected Lie group
H
is semi-simple, by definition, if its commutator subgroup
H^{\prime}
is the whole
H
. By virtue of
[
5
, Corollary 4, p. 285]
, this is equivalent to
H
having a finite fundamental group
\pi_{1}(H)
. In particular, if
H
is simply connected then it is semi-simple.

•

The homogeneous space
G/H
from the corollary need not be a group, that is,
H
may not be a normal subgroup of
G
. To give an example, recall that the second special unitary group
\text{SU}(2)
is simply connected (being homeomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{3}
) and hence semi-simple by the previous remark, and it is contained as a closed but not normal subgroup

\text{SU}(2)\subseteq\text{U}(2)\cong\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\text{U}(2)&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\subseteq\text{U}(3)

of the third unitary group
\text{U}(3)
.

Proof of Corollary
2.14
.

Let
i\colon H\to G
be the inclusion morphism and
i_{1}^{\sharp}\colon\pi_{1}(H)\to\pi_{1}(G)
be the morphism induced by
i
. Similarly, let
q\colon G\to G/H
be the canonical projection and
q_{1}^{\sharp}\colon\pi_{1}(G)\to\pi_{1}(G/H)
,
q^{\sharp}\colon H_{1}^{w}(G)\to H_{1}^{w}(G/H)
be the morphisms induced by
q
. By choosing
z=H
as the base point for
G/H
, we obtain
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{z}=qh
and hence

(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{z})^{\sharp}=q^{\sharp}h^{\sharp}\colon H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)\to H_{1}^{w}(G/H).

Thus, by virtue of Proposition
2.12
, in order to prove the corollary it suffices to show that
q^{\sharp}
is an isomorphism.

Since
G
is a locally trivial fibre bundle over
G/H
with the fibre
H
and since
G
,
G/H
are arc-wise connected, the long exact homotopy sequence of the bundle yields exactness of the upper row of the diagram in Figure
2.8
below (see
[
20
, Section 9.8]
).

\begin{CD}\pi_{1}(H)@>{i_{1}^{\sharp}}>{}>\pi_{1}(G)@>{q_{1}^{\sharp}}>{}>\pi_{1}(G/H)@>{\partial}>{}>H/H_{0}@>{}>{}>0\\
@V{p_{G}}V{}V@V{p_{G/H}}V{}V\\
H_{1}^{w}(G)@>{}>{q^{\sharp}}>H_{1}^{w}(G/H)\end{CD}

Figure 2.8:
The flow
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is topologically free and possesses a free cycle

Moreover, the diagram is clearly commutative.

Now, since the group
H
is connected,
H/H_{0}=0
and so
\mathop{\rm im}(q_{1}^{\sharp})=\ker(\partial)=\pi_{1}(G/H)
. Consequently,
q_{1}^{\sharp}
is an epimorphism and hence so is
q^{\sharp}
. Also, since the group
\pi_{1}(G)
is abelian finitely generated, it follows that so is the group
\pi_{1}(G/H)
. Thus, both
\pi_{1}(G)
and
\pi_{1}(G/H)
contain their torsion subgroups as direct summands with the complementary summands
H_{1}^{w}(G)
and
H_{1}^{w}(G/H)
, respectively. Let
c
and
d
be the largest torsion coefficients of
\pi_{1}(G)
and
\pi_{1}(G/H)
, respectively. By an argument analogous to the one from Proposition
2.12
, which involved the commutativity of the diagram in Figure
2.7
, we obtain
d\ker(q^{\sharp})\subseteq\ker(q_{1}^{\sharp})
.

Further, since the group
\pi_{1}(H)
is finite by the first part of Remark
2.15
, we have
\ker(q_{1}^{\sharp})=\mathop{\rm im}(i_{1}^{\sharp})\subseteq\mathop{\rm tor}(\pi_{1}(G))
and so
c\ker(q_{1}^{\sharp})=0
. Consequently,
cd\ker(q^{\sharp})\subseteq c\ker(q_{1}^{\sharp})=0
, whence
\ker(q^{\sharp})=0
, since the group
H_{1}^{w}(G)
is torsion-free. This shows that
q^{\sharp}
is also a monomorphism, as was to be shown.
∎

2.5.6
Group extensions

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow with acting homeomorphisms
T_{\gamma}
(
\gamma\in\Gamma
) and let
G\in\mathsf{AbTpGp}
. A continuous map
\mathcal{C}\colon\Gamma\times X\to G
is called a (
group
)
extension of
\mathcal{F}
(or a
1
-cocycle over

\mathcal{F}
)
with values in

G
if it satisfies the
cocycle identity

\mathcal{C}(\alpha,T_{\beta}x)\mathcal{C}(\beta,x)=\mathcal{C}(\alpha\beta,x).

(2.25)

A flow
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
and its extension
\mathcal{C}\colon\Gamma\times X\to G
give rise to the induced flow
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X\times G
with acting homeomorphisms

\widetilde{T}_{\gamma}(x,g)=\left(T_{\gamma}x,\mathcal{C}(\gamma,x)g\right),

where
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
x\in X
,
g\in G
. (On occasions we shall attribute to
\mathcal{C}
the dynamical properties of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
. For example, we say that
\mathcal{C}
is minimal when
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
is minimal.) Further, given
g\in G
, consider the vertical translation of
X\times G
by
g
:

R_{g}\colon X\times G\ni(x,h)\mapsto(x,gh)\in X\times G.

Since
G
is an abelian group,
R_{g}
is an automorphism of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
; that is,
R_{g}
is a homeomorphism with
R_{g}\widetilde{T}_{\gamma}=\widetilde{T}_{\gamma}R_{g}
for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
. It follows, in particular, that
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(x,gh)=R_{g}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(x,h))
and
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(x,gh)=R_{g}(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(x,h))
for all
x\in X
and
g,h\in G
.

The set of all extensions of
\mathcal{F}
with values in a given group
G
will be denoted by
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. Clearly,
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is an abelian group with point-wise group operations. If both
\Gamma
and
X
are locally compact then we equip
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
with the compact-open topology, that is, with the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets. Thus
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
becomes an abelian topological group. If a net
(\mathcal{C}_{i})_{i\in J}
converges to
\mathcal{C}
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
then we write
\mathcal{C}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ucs}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{C}
or
\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}
u.c.s. If the group
G
is complete (in particular, if it is locally compact) then the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is also complete; that is, every Cauchy net in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
converges u.c.s. to an element of
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. If both
\Gamma
and
X
are locally compact second countable and
G
is a Polish group then
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is also a Polish group; that is, it is separable and completely metrizable. In such a case we have
\mathop{\rm card}(\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G))\leq\mathfrak{c}
.

If the acting group
\Gamma
of the flow
\mathcal{F}
is connected then the phase space
X
of
\mathcal{F}
is also connected by minimality of
\mathcal{F}
. In such a case every extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
takes its values in the identity component
G_{0}
of
G
. Thus, since our main interest in this work is in connected topological transformation groups, we shall often restrict ourselves to extensions of
\mathcal{F}
with values in connected groups
G
.

2.5.7
Quasi-coboundaries

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow and let
G\in\mathsf{AbTpGp}
. By the cocycle identity (
2.25
), the extensions
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
of
\mathcal{F}
can intuitively be viewed as morphisms of groups
\Gamma\to G
twisted by the flow
\mathcal{F}
. Those extensions
\mathcal{C}
that “untwist” are called quasi-coboundaries. More precisely, let
h\colon\Gamma\to G
be a topological morphism and consider the map

\mathcal{Q}_{h}\colon\Gamma\times X\ni(\gamma,x)\mapsto h(\gamma)\in G.

Then
\mathcal{Q}_{h}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
; we call
\mathcal{Q}_{h}
the (
pure
)
quasi-coboundary
(
over
\mathcal{F}
)
induced by
h
. More generally, an extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is called a
quasi-coboundary
, if it is equivalent to
\mathcal{Q}_{h}
for some topological morphism
h\colon\Gamma\to G
. An elementary argument shows that the pure quasi-coboundaries form a closed subgroup of
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
with the topology of u.c.s. convergence.

2.5.8
Coboundaries and cohomology

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow and
G\in\mathsf{AbTpGp}
. An extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is called a
1
-coboundary
(briefly,
coboundary
)
over

\mathcal{F}
if there is a continuous map
\xi\colon X\to G
with
\mathcal{C}(\gamma,x)=\xi(T_{\gamma}x)\xi(x)^{-1}
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
x\in X
; we write
\mathcal{C}=\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)
or
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\xi}
. Such a map
\xi
, called a
transfer function
of
\mathcal{C}
, is not unique. Nevertheless, by minimality of
\mathcal{F}
, if
X
is a pointed space then every coboundary over
\mathcal{F}
has a unique base point preserving transfer function. The coboundaries over
\mathcal{F}
with values in
G
form a subgroup
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
of
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. The quotient group
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)/\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
will be denoted by
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and the underlying short exact sequence in
\mathsf{AbGp}
will be written as

0\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{G}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{G}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\longrightarrow 0.

(2.26)

The congruence on
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
determined by
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is called the
cohomology relation
(or the
equivalence of extensions
) and we shall denote it by the symbol
\simeq
.

To simplify notation, we shall write
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
,
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
,
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
,
\mu
,
\pi
instead of
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
,
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
,
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
,
\mu_{\mathbb{T}^{1}}
,
\pi_{\mathbb{T}^{1}}
, respectively. Thus, the exact sequence (
2.26
) for
G=\mathbb{T}^{1}
takes the form

0\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}\longrightarrow 0.

(2.27)

2.5.9
Groupoids of minimal extensions

Given a minimal flow
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
and a group
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
, we denote by
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
the set of all minimal extensions from
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
together with the identity
e
of
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. By definition,
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is a sub-groupoid of the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
; we call it the
groupoid of minimal extensions
(of
\mathcal{F}
with values in
G
). Further, we set
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinH}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)=\pi_{G}(\mathop{\rm{\bf MinZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G))
. Clearly,
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinH}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is a sub-groupoid of the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. Similarly to the preceding subsection, we write
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinH}_{\mathcal{F}}}
instead of
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
and
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinH}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
, respectively.

2.5.10
Extensions and fundamental group

Our aim in this subsection is to show how the morphisms induced by group extensions act on fundamental groups and first weak homology groups. This is done in Lemma
2.17
below. Let us start with the following motivating example.

Example 2.16
.

Let
\Gamma,G
be connected Lie groups,
G
compact, and let
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\Gamma,G)
be a morphism with a dense image. Consider the minimal equicontinuous flow
\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright G
induced by
h
(see (
2.22
)). By choosing the identity
z=e
of
G
as the base point for
G
, we get
\mathcal{F}_{z}=h
. Now, given
\xi\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,\mathbb{T}^{1})
, we have
\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)(\gamma,g)=\xi(h(\gamma)g)\xi(g)^{-1}=\xi h(\gamma)
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
g\in G
. Consequently,

\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\Gamma\times G)=\xi^{\sharp}h^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\Gamma)=\xi^{\sharp}\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\Gamma).

(2.28)

Moreover, it follows from Lemma
5.26
and Remark
5.27
in Section
5.4
that every
\xi\in C_{z}(G,\mathbb{T}^{1})
is homotopic to a (unique) morphism from
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,\mathbb{T}^{1})
. Consequently, the identity (
2.28
) is true for every continuous base point preserving map
\xi\colon G\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
. The following lemma shows that it is in fact true over an arbitrary (minimal) flow.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow with
\Gamma\in\mathsf{LieGp}
connected and with
X
a compact connected manifold. Choose a base point
z
for
X
and fix
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
,
\xi\in C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
. Then for all
f\in\pi_{1}(\Gamma)
and
g\in\pi_{1}(X)
,

\mathcal{C}^{\sharp}\left((f,g)\right)=\mathcal{C}_{z}^{\sharp}(f)\hskip 8.53581pt\text{and}\hskip 8.53581pt\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)^{\sharp}\left((f,g)\right)=\xi^{\sharp}\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}(f),

(2.29)

where
\mathcal{C}_{z}=\mathcal{C}(-,z)\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{z}=\mathcal{F}(-,z)\colon\Gamma\to X
. Consequently,

\mathcal{C}^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\Gamma\times X)=\mathcal{C}_{z}^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\Gamma)\hskip 8.53581pt\text{and}\hskip 8.53581pt\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\Gamma\times X)=\xi^{\sharp}\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\Gamma).

(2.30)

Moreover, the diagram in Figure
2.9
below commutes, its first three vertical arrows from the left are epimorphisms and the fourth one is an isomorphism. Finally,

\mathcal{C}^{\sharp}H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma\times X)=\mathcal{C}_{z}^{\sharp}H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)\hskip 8.53581pt\text{and}\hskip 8.53581pt\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)^{\sharp}H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma\times X)=\xi^{\sharp}H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F}).

(2.31)

\begin{CD}\pi_{1}(\Gamma)@>{\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}}>{}>\mathop{\rm im}(\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}){}\subseteq{}\pi_{1}(X)@>{\xi^{\sharp}}>{}>\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}){}\cong{}\mathbb{Z}\\
@V{p_{\Gamma}}V{}V@V{p_{X}}V{}V@V{p_{X}}V{}V@V{p_{\mathbb{T}^{1}}}V{}V\Big\|\\
H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)@>{}>{\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}}>H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F}){}\subseteq{}H_{1}^{w}(X)@>{}>{\xi^{\sharp}}>H_{1}^{w}(\mathbb{T}^{1}){}\cong{}\mathbb{Z}\end{CD}

Figure 2.9:
Extensions and one-dimensional invariants

Proof of Lemma
2.17
.

Before turning to the proof, notice that both
\mathcal{F}_{z}
and
\mathcal{C}_{z}
are (continuous) base point preserving maps. We denote by
c_{1}
,
c_{z}
and
c_{e}
the constant loop based at
1\in\Gamma
,
z\in X
and
e=1\in\mathbb{T}^{1}
, respectively.

Fix
f\in\pi_{1}(\Gamma)
and
g\in\pi_{1}(X)
. Then
\mathcal{C}^{\sharp}((f,c_{z}))=\mathcal{C}_{z}^{\sharp}(f)
by definition of
\mathcal{C}_{z}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\sharp}((c_{1},g))=c_{e}
by the cocycle identity. Thus,

\mathcal{C}^{\sharp}\left((f,g)\right)=\mathcal{C}^{\sharp}\left((f,c_{z})*(c_{1},g)\right)=\mathcal{C}^{\sharp}\left((f,c_{z})\right)*\mathcal{C}^{\sharp}\left((c_{1},g)\right)=\mathcal{C}_{z}^{\sharp}(f)*c_{e}=\mathcal{C}_{z}^{\sharp}(f).

Similarly,

\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)^{\sharp}\left((f,g)\right)=\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)_{z}^{\sharp}(f)=\xi\mathcal{F}_{z}f=\xi^{\sharp}\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}(f),

which verifies (
2.29
). The two equalities from (
2.30
) follow at once from the respective equalities in (
2.29
).

The statement of the lemma concerning the diagram in Figure
2.9
follows from our discussion in Subsection
2.3.2
and from our definition of the group
H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})
. To verify (
2.31
), we use (
2.30
) and the commutativity of the diagram in Figure
2.9
. Thus we obtain

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}^{\sharp}H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma\times X)&=\mathcal{C}^{\sharp}p_{\Gamma\times X}\pi_{1}(\Gamma\times X)=p_{\mathbb{T}^{1}}\mathcal{C}^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\Gamma\times X)=p_{\mathbb{T}^{1}}\mathcal{C}_{z}^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\Gamma)\\
&=\mathcal{C}_{z}^{\sharp}p_{\Gamma}\pi_{1}(\Gamma)=\mathcal{C}_{z}^{\sharp}H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)\end{split}

and

\begin{split}\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)^{\sharp}H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma\times X)&=\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)^{\sharp}p_{\Gamma\times X}\pi_{1}(\Gamma\times X)=p_{\mathbb{T}^{1}}\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\Gamma\times X)\\
&=p_{\mathbb{T}^{1}}\xi^{\sharp}\mathop{\rm im}(\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp})=\xi^{\sharp}p_{X}\mathop{\rm im}(\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp})=\xi^{\sharp}H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F}).\end{split}

∎

2.5.11
Banach spaces of extensions

Our aim in this section is to find the structure of a Banach space in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
; this is done in Lemma
2.18
below. First, let us recall some useful facts. Let
\Gamma\in\mathsf{LCGp}
be a compactly generated group and
K\subseteq\Gamma
be a symmetric compact set containing the identity
1
of
\Gamma
in its interior. Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
K^{n}
the set of all products
\gamma_{1}\dots\gamma_{n}
, where
\gamma_{i}\in K
for every
i=1,\dots,n
; clearly,
K^{n}
is a compact subset of
\Gamma
. Observe that
K
generates the group
\Gamma
if and only if
\Gamma=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}K^{n}
. If this is the case then for every compact set
F\subseteq\Gamma
there is
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
F\subseteq K^{n}
.

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow with
\Gamma\in\mathsf{LCGp}
compactly generated and with
X
compact. Fix a generating symmetric compact set
K\subseteq\Gamma
for
\Gamma
, which contains the identity
1
of
\Gamma
in its interior. Then
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
is a real Banach space with the point-wise linear operations and with the norm

\|\mathcal{C}\|=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{2^{n}}\max\big\{|\mathcal{C}(\gamma,x)|:\gamma\in K^{n},x\in X\big\}.

(2.32)

Moreover, the norm topology on
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
coincides with the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.

Remark 2.19
.

Recall from Subsection
2.5.6
that if both
\Gamma
and
X
are additionally assumed second countable then the Banach space
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
is separable.

Proof of Lemma
2.18
.

First,
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
is clearly closed with respect to point-wise linear combinations and so it is a real linear space. Further, given
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\in K
and
x\in X
, we have

\mathcal{C}(\gamma_{1}\dots\gamma_{n},x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{C}\left(\gamma_{i},T_{\gamma_{i+1}\dots\gamma_{n}}x\right)+\mathcal{C}(\gamma_{n},x)

by the cocycle identity. Consequently, for all
\gamma\in K^{n}
and
x\in X
,

|\mathcal{C}(\gamma,x)|\leq n\max\big\{|\mathcal{C}(\alpha,y)|:\alpha\in K,y\in X\big\}.

It follows that the series in (
2.32
) converges and hence
\|\mathcal{C}\|
is well defined for every
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
. It is a routine procedure to verify that
\|.\|
satisfies all the axioms of a norm on
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
; for instance,
\|\mathcal{C}\|=0
implies
\mathcal{C}=0
, since
K
generates
\Gamma
.

We claim that the norm topology on
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
coincides with the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets. To show this, let
(\mathcal{C}_{i})
be a net in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
and let
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
. Since each compact subset of
\Gamma\times X
is contained in
K^{n}\times X
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have an equivalence of the following statements:

•

\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}
uniformly on compact sets,

•

\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}
uniformly on
K^{n}\times X
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

By definition of the norm
\|.\|
, the second of these two statements is equivalent to the convergence
\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}
in the norm topology. This proves the claim.

Finally, it follows from our discussion in Subsection
2.5.6
that
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
is complete in the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets. Thus, with the norm
\|.\|
defined by (
2.32
),
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
is indeed a Banach space.
∎

2.5.12
The category
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow. The extensions of
\mathcal{F}
with values in compact abelian groups form a category
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
defined as follows. The objects of
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
are extensions
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
with
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
. Further, given
G,H\in\mathsf{CAGp}
,
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and
\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(H)
, the set
\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
of morphisms
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
consists of all (topological) morphisms
q\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H)
with
q\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{D}
. The composition of morphisms in
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is simply their composition in
\mathsf{CAGp}
; this is clearly a correct definition. Finally, for all
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, the identity
\mathop{\rm id}_{G}
on
G
is an element of
\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
. Thus,
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is a category in its own right. The following proposition shows that
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is always co-complete.

Proposition 2.20
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow. Then
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is a co-complete category.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{C}=(\mathcal{C}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}\mathcal{C}_{k})
be an inverse system in
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
, indexed by a directed set
J
; we shall find an inverse limit of
\mathfrak{C}
in
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. First, for
j\in J
, let
G_{j}\in\mathsf{CAGp}
be the group with
\mathcal{C}_{j}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G_{j})
. By definition of morphisms and their composition in
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
, there is an inverse system
\mathfrak{G}=(G_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}G_{k})
in
\mathsf{CAGp}
. Let
G\mathrel{\lhd}\prod_{j\in J}G_{j}
be the usual inverse limit of
\mathfrak{G}
in
\mathsf{CAGp}
with the limit projections
p_{j}\colon G\to G_{j}
(see Subsection
2.4.1
for the definition of
G
and
p_{j}
). Consider the map

\mathcal{C}\colon\Gamma\times X\ni(\gamma,x)\mapsto\left(\mathcal{C}_{j}(\gamma,x)\right)_{j\in J}\in G\subseteq\prod_{j\in J}G_{j};

we show that
\mathcal{C}
is an inverse limit of
\mathfrak{C}
in
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
with
p_{j}
(
j\in J
) as the limit projections.

First, since
p_{ik}\mathcal{C}_{k}=\mathcal{C}_{i}
holds for all
i\leq k
, the map
\mathcal{C}
does indeed take its values in the group
G
. Moreover, the cocycle identity and continuity of
\mathcal{C}
are immediate, hence
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. Further, by our definition of
\mathcal{C}
and of the maps
p_{j}
, we have
p_{j}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{j})
for every
j\in J
and
p_{ik}p_{k}=p_{i}
for all
i\leq k
. Thus, it remains to verify the universality of
\mathcal{C}
and
(p_{j})_{j\in J}
. To this end, let
H\in\mathsf{CAGp}
,
\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(H)
and
q_{j}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C}_{j})
(
j\in J
) be such that
p_{ik}q_{k}=q_{i}
for all
i\leq k
; we need to verify the following claim:

•

there is a unique
r\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C})
with
p_{j}r=q_{j}
for every
j\in J
.

We begin by verifying the existence part of the claim. By our assumptions above, we have
q_{j}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(H,G_{j})
for every
j\in J
and
p_{ik}q_{k}=q_{i}
for all
i\leq k
. Thus, since
G
is an inverse limit of
\mathfrak{G}
in
\mathsf{CAGp}
with the morphisms
p_{j}
as the limit projections, there is a unique
r\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H)
with
p_{j}r=q_{j}
for every
j\in J
. We need to show that
r\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C})
or, equivalently, that
r\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{C}
. Let
j\in J
; then

p_{j}(r\mathcal{D})=(p_{j}r)\mathcal{D}=q_{j}\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{C}_{j}=p_{j}\mathcal{C}.

Since the morphisms
p_{j}
separate the points of
G
, it follows that
r\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{C}
. This verifies the existence part of the claim. The uniqueness part follows at once from the inclusion
\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C})\subseteq\mathop{\rm Hom}(H,G)
.
∎

We shall find the following lemma useful.

Lemma 2.21
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}=(G_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}G_{k})
be an inverse system in
\mathsf{CAGp}
indexed by a directed set
J
,
G
be an inverse limit of
\mathfrak{G}
in
\mathsf{CAGp}
and
p_{j}\colon G\to G_{j}
(
j\in J
) be the limit projections. Given a minimal flow
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
and an extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, set
\mathcal{C}_{j}=p_{j}\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G_{j})
for every
j\in J
. Then there is an inverse system
\mathfrak{C}=(\mathcal{C}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}\mathcal{C}_{k})
in
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
,
\mathcal{C}
is its inverse limit and
p_{j}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}_{j}
(
j\in J
) are the limit projections.

Proof.

Given
j\in J
, we have indeed
\mathcal{C}_{j}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G_{j})
, since
p_{j}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,G_{j})
. Further, given
i\leq k
, we have
p_{ik}p_{k}=p_{i}
by the assumptions, hence
p_{ik}\mathcal{C}_{k}=p_{ik}p_{k}\mathcal{C}=p_{i}\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{i}
and so
p_{ik}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{C}_{k},\mathcal{C}_{i})
. Thus,
\mathfrak{C}
is an inverse system in
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. Also, by definition of the extensions
\mathcal{C}_{j}
, we have
p_{j}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{j})
for every
j\in J
.

To show that
\mathcal{C}
is an inverse limit of
\mathfrak{C}
in
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
with the limit projections
p_{j}
, fix
H\in\mathsf{CAGp}
,
\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(H)
and
q_{j}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C}_{j})
(
j\in J
) with
p_{ik}q_{k}=q_{i}
for
i\leq k
; we need to verify the following claim:

(
*
)

there is a unique
r\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C})
with
p_{j}r=q_{j}
for every
j\in J
.

Since
G
is an inverse limit of
\mathfrak{G}
in
\mathsf{CAGp}
with the limit projections
p_{j}
, there is a unique
r\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(H,G)
with
p_{j}r=q_{j}
for every
j\in J
. As
\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C})\subseteq\mathop{\rm Hom}(H,G)
, this immediately implies the uniqueness part of (
*
). To verify the existence part of (
*
), it remains to show that
r\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C})
. This is done similarly to the proof of Proposition
2.20
. Indeed, given
j\in J
, we have

p_{j}(r\mathcal{D})=(p_{j}r)\mathcal{D}=q_{j}\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{C}_{j}=p_{j}\mathcal{C}.

Since the morphisms
p_{j}
(
j\in J
) separate the points of
G
, it follows that
r\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{C}
. Thus,
r\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C})
, as was to be shown.
∎

2.5.13
Induced morphisms

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow,
G,H\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
q\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H)
. The morphism
q
induces a morphism of groups

\widehat{q}\colon\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\ni\mathcal{C}\mapsto q\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(H);

if both
\Gamma
and
X
are locally compact then
\widehat{q}
is a topological morphism. Clearly, the assignment
q\mapsto\widehat{q}
is covariant functorial. Finally, since
\widehat{q}
maps coboundaries to coboundaries, it gives rise to a morphism of groups
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\to\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(H)
, which we shall denote by the same symbol
\widehat{q}
.

Now let
G_{j}
(
j\in J
) be a family of abelian topological groups. For
i\in J
consider the projection morphism
\mathop{\rm pr}_{i}\colon\prod_{j\in J}G_{j}\to G_{i}
. Let

\varphi\colon\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}\Big(\prod_{j\in J}G_{j}\Big)\ni\mathcal{C}\mapsto\left(\text{pr}_{j}\mathcal{C}\right)_{j\in J}\in\prod_{j\in J}\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G_{j}).

Then
\varphi
is a morphism of groups by the preceding paragraph. In fact, an elementary argument shows that
\varphi
is an isomorphism. If, in addition, both
\Gamma
and
X
are locally compact then
\varphi
is a topological isomorphism. In particular, for every cardinal
\mathfrak{k}
, there is a (topological) isomorphism
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{k}})\cong(\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})^{\mathfrak{k}}
.

Chapter 3
Fundamental Tools

3.1
Extensions as group morphisms

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow. Our aim in this section is to show that the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
of the extensions of
\mathcal{F}
with values in a given group
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
is (topologically) isomorphic to the group of morphisms
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
. We construct this isomorphism in Theorem
3.1
and then derive from it some useful corollaries. In particular, we show that the category
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
can be viewed as an opposite to a full subcategory of the category
\mathsf{Hom}(\mathsf{DAGp},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow and
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
. Define a map

\Phi_{G}\colon\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\to\mathop{\rm Hom}\left(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}\right)

(3.1)

by the rule

\Phi_{G}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{C}^{*}\colon G^{*}\ni\chi\mapsto\chi\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}.

Then
\Phi_{G}
is an isomorphism of groups. If, in addition, both
\Gamma
and
X
are locally compact,
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
carry the topology of u.c.s. convergence and
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
is equipped with the topology of point-wise convergence, then
\Phi_{G}
is also a topological isomorphism.

Remark 3.2
.

We wish to add the following remarks.

•

Let us mention how the correspondence from the theorem affects induced morphisms between groups of extensions. So assume that
G,H\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H)
. Recall that
h
induces a morphism of groups
\widehat{h}\colon\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\to\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(H)
acting by the rule
\widehat{h}\colon\mathcal{C}\mapsto h\mathcal{C}
. One verifies at once that
\Phi_{H}\widehat{h}\Phi_{G}^{-1}(\mathcal{C}^{*})=\mathcal{C}^{*}h^{*}
for every
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, where
h^{*}
stands for the morphism dual to
h
. Thus, under the correspondence from the theorem,
\widehat{h}
corresponds to
\Phi_{H}\widehat{h}\Phi_{G}^{-1}=\mathop{\rm Hom}(h^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
.

•

Because of the isomorphism (
3.1
), we shall refer to
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
as the first cohomology group of
\mathcal{F}
with coefficients in
G^{*}
. In particular, since the dual group
(\mathbb{T}^{1})^{*}
of
\mathbb{T}^{1}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
, the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
will be sometimes called the first cohomology group of
\mathcal{F}
with integer coefficients.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

It is a standard procedure to show that for every
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
,
\Phi_{G}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{C}^{*}
is a morphism of groups
G^{*}\to\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. Similarly, one verifies at once that
\Phi_{G}
is a morphism of groups. It is a monomorphism, since the characters of
G
separate the points of
G
. We verify that
\Phi_{G}
is also an epimorphism. To this end, define a map

\Lambda_{G}\colon\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})\to\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G^{**})

by the rule

\Lambda_{G}(\varphi)\colon\Gamma\times X\ni(\gamma,x)\mapsto\left(G^{*}\ni\chi\mapsto\varphi(\chi)(\gamma,x)\in\mathbb{T}^{1}\right)\in G^{**}

for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
x\in X
. Again, one readily verifies that
\Lambda_{G}
is a (well defined) monomorphism of groups. Now consider the Pontryagin isomorphism
\omega_{G}\colon G\to G^{**}
and the induced isomorphism

\widehat{\omega}_{G}\colon\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\ni\mathcal{C}\mapsto\omega_{G}\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G^{**}).

A simple computation shows that
\Lambda_{G}\Phi_{G}=\widehat{\omega}_{G}
. Since
\Lambda_{G}
is a monomorphism and
\widehat{\omega}_{G}
is an isomorphism, this shows that
\Phi_{G}
is an epimorphism. Thus, indeed,
\Phi_{G}
is an isomorphism of groups.

Now assume that both
\Gamma
and
X
are locally compact; we show that
\Phi_{G}
is a topological isomorphism. To this end, fix a net
(\mathcal{C}_{i})_{i\in J}
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and an extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. We show that
\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}
u.c.s. on
\Gamma\times X
if and only if
\mathcal{C}^{*}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}^{*}
point-wise on
G^{*}
.

Assume, first, that
\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}
u.c.s. on
\Gamma\times X
. Then for every
\chi\in G^{*}
,
\chi\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\chi\mathcal{C}
u.c.s. on
\Gamma\times X
or, equivalently,
\mathcal{C}^{*}_{i}(\chi)\to\mathcal{C}^{*}(\chi)
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. This means that
\mathcal{C}^{*}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}^{*}
point-wise on
G^{*}
.

Assume, conversely, that
\mathcal{C}^{*}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}^{*}
point-wise on
G^{*}
, and fix a compact set
K\subseteq\Gamma\times X
and a neighbourhood
W
of
e
in
G
. By compactness of
G
, there exist
n\in\mathbb{N}
, neighbourhoods
V_{1},\dots,V_{n}
of
1
in
\mathbb{T}^{1}
and
\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{n}\in G^{*}
such that
\bigcap_{k=1}^{n}\chi_{k}^{-1}(V_{k})\subseteq W
. Since
\chi_{k}\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\chi_{k}\mathcal{C}
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
for every
k=1,\dots,n
, there is
i_{0}\in J
with
\chi_{k}\mathcal{C}_{i}(\gamma,x)\left(\chi_{k}\mathcal{C}(\gamma,x)\right)^{-1}\in V_{k}
for all
i\geq i_{0}
,
k=1,\dots,n
and
(\gamma,x)\in K
. That is, for
i\geq i_{0}
and
(\gamma,x)\in K
,
\mathcal{C}_{i}(\gamma,x)\mathcal{C}(\gamma,x)^{-1}\in W
. This shows that
\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}
u.c.s. on
\Gamma\times X
.
∎

Corollary 3.3
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow and
G,H\in\mathsf{CAGp}
. Equip the tensor product
G^{*}\otimes H^{*}
with the discrete topology. Then there is an isomorphism of groups

\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(H))\cong\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}((G^{*}\otimes H^{*})^{*}).

The isomorphism is topological, provided both
\Gamma
and
X
are locally compact,
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(H)
and
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}((G^{*}\otimes H^{*})^{*})
carry the topology of u.c.s. convergence and
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(H))
is equipped with the topology of point-wise convergence.

Proof.

The first statement of the corollary follows from Theorem
3.1
and from basic facts concerning morphisms from tensor products of abelian groups:

\begin{split}\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(H))&\cong\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm Hom}(H^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}))\cong\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*}\otimes H^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})\\
&\cong\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}((G^{*}\otimes H^{*})^{*}).\end{split}

(3.2)

If
\Gamma
and
X
are locally compact then the first and the third isomorphism from (
3.2
) are topological by virtue of Theorem
3.1
. The second isomorphism is also topological, since all the groups
G^{*}
,
H^{*}
and
G^{*}\otimes H^{*}
carry the discrete topology and hence all the
\mathop{\rm Hom}
groups in (
3.2
) carry the topology of point-wise convergence (see (
2.7
) and the discussion following it).
∎

Corollary 3.4
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow,
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
H\mathrel{\lhd}G
. Then there is an exact sequence of abelian groups

\begin{split}0&\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(H)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widehat{j}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widehat{p}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G/H)\\
&\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Ext}(H^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Ext}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Ext}(H^{\perp},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})\longrightarrow 0,\end{split}

(3.3)

where
j
is the inclusion morphism
H\to G
,
p
is the quotient morphism
G\to G/H
and
H^{\perp}
stands for the annihilator of
H
in
G
.

Proof.

There is an exact sequence of abelian groups

0\longrightarrow(G/H)^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{*}\longrightarrow 0.

(3.4)

Consider the contravariant Hom-Ext sequence derived from (
3.4
) and corresponding to
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
:

\begin{split}0&\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Hom}(H^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})\xrightarrow{\mathop{\rm Hom}(j^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})}\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})\xrightarrow{\mathop{\rm Hom}(p^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})}\mathop{\rm Hom}((G/H)^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})\\
&\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Ext}(H^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Ext}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Ext}((G/H)^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})\longrightarrow 0.\end{split}

(3.5)

By virtue of Theorem
3.1
and Remark
3.2
, the diagram in Figure
3.10
below commutes,

\begin{CD}\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(H)@>{\widehat{j}}>{}>\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)@>{\widehat{p}}>{}>\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G/H)\\
@V{\Phi_{H}}V{}V@V{\Phi_{G}}V{}V@V{\Phi_{G/H}}V{}V\\
\mathop{\rm Hom}(H^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})@>{}>{\mathop{\rm Hom}(j^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})}>\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})@>{}>{\mathop{\rm Hom}(p^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})}>\mathop{\rm Hom}((G/H)^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})\end{CD}

Figure 3.10:
Obtaining (
3.3
) from (
3.5
)

where all
\Phi_{H}
,
\Phi_{G}
and
\Phi_{G/H}
are isomorphisms. Since, in addition, the groups
(G/H)^{*}
and
H^{\perp}
are isomorphic, the exact sequence (
3.5
) takes the desired form (
3.3
).
∎

Corollary 3.5
.

Given a minimal flow
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
, there is a contravariant functor

\mathfrak{F}\colon\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}\to\mathsf{Hom}(\mathsf{DAGp},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})

defined as follows.

(i)

If
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
then
\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{C}^{*}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
.

(ii)

If
G,H\in\mathsf{CAGp}
,
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
,
\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(H)
and
q\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
then
\mathfrak{F}(q)=q^{*}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{D}^{*},\mathcal{C}^{*})
.

Remark 3.6
.

Under the action of
\mathfrak{F}
, distinct objects correspond to distinct objects and, likewise, distinct morphisms to distinct morphisms. Consequently,
\mathfrak{F}
represents an isomorphism between
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and the opposite of the subcategory
\mathfrak{F}(\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}})
of
\mathsf{Hom}(\mathsf{DAGp},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
. Notice that the subcategory
\mathfrak{F}(\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}})
of
\mathsf{Hom}(\mathsf{DAGp},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
is full, that is,
\mathfrak{F}(\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}))=\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{D}),\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{C}))
for all
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. To see this, fix
G,H\in\mathsf{CAGp}
,
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
,
\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(H)
and
r\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{D}^{*},\mathcal{C}^{*})
. Then
r\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(H^{*},G^{*})
and hence
r=q^{*}
for some
q\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H)
. Since
(q\mathcal{C})^{*}=\mathcal{C}^{*}q^{*}=\mathcal{C}^{*}r=\mathcal{D}^{*}
, it follows that
q\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{D}
. Thus,
q\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
and
\mathfrak{F}(q)=q^{*}=r
.

Proof of Corollary
3.5
.

By virtue of Theorem
3.1
,
\mathfrak{F}
assigns objects to objects. Also,
\mathfrak{F}
assigns morphisms to morphisms, for in the situation from (ii),
\mathcal{C}^{*}q^{*}=(q\mathcal{C})^{*}=\mathcal{D}^{*}
. To verify the axioms of a contravariant functor for
\mathfrak{F}
, fix groups
G,H,K\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and extensions
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E}\in\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
with their values in
G,H,K
, respectively. The identity
\mathop{\rm id}_{G}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
corresponds via
\mathfrak{F}
to the identity
\mathop{\rm id}_{G^{*}}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{C}^{*},\mathcal{C}^{*})
, for
(\mathop{\rm id}_{G})^{*}=\mathop{\rm id}_{G^{*}}
. Also, if
q\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
and
r\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E})
then
\mathfrak{F}(rq)=\mathfrak{F}(q)\mathfrak{F}(r)
, for
(rq)^{*}=q^{*}r^{*}
.
∎

3.2
Functorial approach to sections

An important tool for our study of the first cohomology groups of a minimal flow
\mathcal{F}
are sections of orbit closures in induced skew products. It turns out that this tool can be introduced formally, in the categorical language, as a unique covariant functor
F\colon\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}\to\mathsf{CAGp}
with three natural properties; this is shown in Theorem
3.7
. This theorem and its reformulation in Theorem
3.18
are the main results of the present section. This abstract approach to sections will turn out to be very useful to us; we will often manage with the defining conditions of this functor (and with its other three properties listed in Theorem
3.7
) and will not have to invoke its explicit construction. This will make many proofs throughout this work much more transparent.

Theorem 3.7
.

Given a minimal flow
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
, there exists a unique covariant functor

F\colon\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}\to\mathsf{CAGp},

which satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

if
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
then
F(\mathcal{C})\mathrel{\lhd}G
,

(2)

for every
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
,
F(\mathcal{C})=e
if and only if
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
,

(3)

for all
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and every
q\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
,
F(\mathcal{D})=qF(\mathcal{C})
and
F(q)\colon F(\mathcal{C})\to F(\mathcal{D})
is the restriction of
q
.

Moreover, the functor
F
satisfies the following conditions:

(4)

F(\mathcal{C})=F(\mathcal{D})
whenever
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
satisfy
\mathcal{C}\simeq\mathcal{D}
,

(5)

F\colon\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}\to\mathsf{CAGp}
is continuous in the sense that it preserves limits of inverse systems,

(6)

for every
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
,
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is minimal if and only if
F(\mathcal{C})=G
.

Before turning to the proof of Theorem
3.7
, let us prove the following two corollaries.

Corollary 3.8
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow. Then for every
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
, the isomorphism
\Phi_{G}
from Theorem
3.1
restricts to an isomorphism

\Phi_{G}\colon\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\to\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}).

Also, the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

the sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}\longrightarrow 0

splits,

(b)

the sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{G}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{G}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\longrightarrow 0

splits for every
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
.

Further, given
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

\mathcal{C}
is minimal,

(ii)

for every
\chi\in G^{*}\setminus 1
,
\chi\mathcal{C}\notin\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
.

Finally, if
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
is connected then the isomorphism
\Phi_{G}
from Theorem
3.1
restricts to an isomorphism of groupoids

\Phi_{G}\colon\mathop{\rm{\bf MinZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\to\mathop{\rm Mon}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf MinZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}).

Proof.

Fix
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
. By Theorem
3.7
(2, 3), the following conditions are equivalent for every
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
:

•

\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
,

•

F(\mathcal{C})=e
,

•

\chi F(\mathcal{C})=1
for every
\chi\in G^{*}
,

•

F(\chi\mathcal{C})=1
for every
\chi\in G^{*}
,

•

\mathcal{C}^{*}(\chi)=\chi\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
for every
\chi\in G^{*}
.

This verifies the first statement of the corollary.

We show that conditions (a) and (b) are equivalent. Implication (b)
\Rightarrow
(a) is clear. To verify the converse, assume that
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is a direct summand in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and fix
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
. Then
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}})
is a direct summand in
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
. Consequently, by the first statement of the corollary and by Theorem
3.1
,
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)=\Phi_{G}^{-1}(\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}))
is a direct summand in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)=\Phi_{G}^{-1}(\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}))
. This verifies condition (b).

Fix
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
; we show that conditions (i) and (ii) are equivalent. By Theorem
3.7
(2, 3, 6), the following conditions are equivalent:

•

\mathcal{C}
is minimal,

•

F(\mathcal{C})=G
,

•

\chi F(\mathcal{C})\neq 1
for every
\chi\in G^{*}\setminus 1
,

•

F(\chi\mathcal{C})\neq 1
for every
\chi\in G^{*}\setminus 1
,

•

\chi\mathcal{C}\notin\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
for every
\chi\in G^{*}\setminus 1
.

This verifies the equivalence of (i) and (ii).

We verify the last statement of the corollary. If
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
is connected then the following conditions are equivalent for every
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
by Theorem
3.7
(3, 6):

•

\mathcal{C}
is minimal, that is,
F(\mathcal{C})=G
,

•

\chi F(\mathcal{C})=\mathbb{T}^{1}
for every
\chi\in G^{*}\setminus 1
,

•

F(\chi\mathcal{C})=\mathbb{T}^{1}
for every
\chi\in G^{*}\setminus 1
,

•

\mathcal{C}^{*}(\chi)=\chi\mathcal{C}
is minimal for every
\chi\in G^{*}\setminus 1
.

Moreover, the last condition is clearly equivalent to

•

\mathcal{C}^{*}
is a monomorphism with values in
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
.

This leads to the equality
\Phi_{G}(\mathop{\rm{\bf MinZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\setminus e)=\mathop{\rm Mon}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf MinZ}_{\mathcal{F}}})\setminus 1
. Consequently,
\Phi_{G}
maps
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
onto
\mathop{\rm Mon}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf MinZ}_{\mathcal{F}}})
, as was to be shown.
∎

Corollary 3.9
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow. Then

\mathop{\rm{\bf MinH}_{\mathcal{F}}}\setminus 1=\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}\setminus\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}).

Proof.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
let
\kappa_{k}
be the
k
-endomorphism of
\mathbb{T}^{1}
. Then for every
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
, the following conditions are equivalent by virtue of Theorem
3.7
:

•

\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})
,

•

\mathcal{C}^{k}=1
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

\kappa_{k}F(\mathcal{C})=F(\mathcal{C}^{k})=1
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

F(\mathcal{C})\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

F(\mathcal{C})\neq\mathbb{T}^{1}
,

•

\mathcal{C}\notin\mathop{\rm{\bf MinH}_{\mathcal{F}}}\setminus 1
.

Consequently,
\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})
and
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinH}_{\mathcal{F}}}\setminus 1
are complementary in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
, as was to be shown.
∎

We begin our proof of Theorem
3.7
by verifying the uniqueness of the functor
F
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow. Then there exists at most one covariant functor
F\colon\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}\to\mathsf{CAGp}
with properties
(1)-(3)
from Theorem
3.7
.

Proof.

Assume that
F_{1},F_{2}
are two such functors and fix
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. Denote by
q
the quotient morphism
G\to G/F_{1}(\mathcal{C})
. By condition (3),
F_{1}(q\mathcal{C})=qF_{1}(\mathcal{C})=e
and hence, by virtue of (2),
q\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G/F_{1}(\mathcal{C}))
. Applying (2) and (3) once more yields
e=F_{2}(q\mathcal{C})=qF_{2}(\mathcal{C})
. Thus,
F_{2}(\mathcal{C})\subseteq F_{1}(\mathcal{C})
. The converse inclusion follows by symmetry and so
F_{1},F_{2}
act in the same way on the objects of
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. The fact that they act in the same way also on morphisms of
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
follows immediately from condition (3).
∎

Now we turn to the construction of the functor
F
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow and let
z\in X
. Given
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, set
F(\mathcal{C})=\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)|_{z}
, the vertical
z
-section of the orbit closure of
(z,e)
under the action of the induced skew product
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
on
X\times G
. Then the following statements hold for every
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and every
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
:

(a)

F(\mathcal{C})
is a closed subgroup of
G
,

(b)

for every
x\in X
,
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)|_{x}\in G/F(\mathcal{C})
.

Remark 3.12
.

Observe that by definition of
F(\mathcal{C})
, a point
g\in G
lies in
F(\mathcal{C})
if and only if for every neighbourhood
U
of
z
in
X
and every neighbourhood
V
of
g
in
G
there is
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
T_{\gamma}z\in U
and
\mathcal{C}(\gamma,z)\in V
.

Proof of Lemma
3.11
.

We verify statement (a). By definition,
F(\mathcal{C})
is a closed subset of
G
containing the identity
e
. Thus, due to compactness of
G
, it remains to show that
F(\mathcal{C})
is a sub-semigroup of
G
. So fix
g,h\in F(\mathcal{C})
, a neighbourhood
U
of
z
in
X
and a neighbourhood
V
of
e
in
G
; we shall find
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
T_{\gamma}z\in U
and
\mathcal{C}(\gamma,z)\in ghV
. Fix an identity neighbourhood
W
in
G
with
W^{2}\subseteq V
. Since
h\in F(\mathcal{C})
, there is
\alpha\in\Gamma
with
T_{\alpha}z\in U
and
\mathcal{C}(\alpha,z)\in hW
. Choose a neighbourhood
U^{\prime}
of
z
in
X
with
T_{\alpha}U^{\prime}\subseteq U
and
\mathcal{C}(\alpha\times U^{\prime})\subseteq hW
. Since
g\in F(\mathcal{C})
, there is
\beta\in\Gamma
with
T_{\beta}z\in U^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}(\beta,z)\in gW
. Then

\begin{split}\widetilde{T}_{\alpha\beta}(z,e)&=(T_{\alpha\beta}z,\mathcal{C}(\alpha\beta,z))=(T_{\alpha}T_{\beta}z,\mathcal{C}(\alpha,T_{\beta}z)\mathcal{C}(\beta,z))\\
&\in T_{\alpha}U^{\prime}\times\mathcal{C}(\alpha\times U^{\prime})gW\subseteq U\times ghW^{2}\subseteq U\times ghV.\end{split}

Thus, for
\gamma=\alpha\beta
,
T_{\gamma}z\in U
and
\mathcal{C}(\gamma,z)\in ghV
, as was to be shown.

Now we turn to the proof of (b). For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
\widetilde{T}_{\gamma}\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)=\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)
. By applying the vertical
T_{\gamma}z
-section to this equality, we obtain
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)|_{T_{\gamma}z}=\mathcal{C}(\gamma,z)F(\mathcal{C})
. Thus, (b) holds for every
x
from the
\mathcal{F}
-orbit of
z
. Now let
x\in X
be arbitrary. By letting elements of the
\mathcal{F}
-orbit of
z
approach
x
and by using compactness of
G
, we find
g_{x}\in G
with
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)|_{x}\supseteq g_{x}\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)|_{z}
. Likewise, by letting elements of the
\mathcal{F}
-orbit of
x
approach
z
, we find
h_{x}
with
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)|_{z}\supseteq h_{x}\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)|_{x}
. These two inclusions yield

F(\mathcal{C})=\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)|_{z}\supseteq h_{x}\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)|_{x}\supseteq h_{x}g_{x}\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)|_{z}=h_{x}g_{x}F(\mathcal{C})

and hence
g_{x}F(\mathcal{C})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)|_{x}\subseteq h_{x}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C})
. Consequently,
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)|_{x}=g_{x}F(\mathcal{C})\in G/F(\mathcal{C})
. This finishes the proof of (b).
∎

Till the end of our proof of Theorem
3.7
, we shall assume that the point
z
from Lemma
3.11
above is fixed (in other words, we shall assume that
X
is a pointed space). Of course, the result of the construction from Lemma
3.11
above does not depend on the choice of
z
, this is a consequence of the uniqueness part of Theorem
3.7
.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow. Then the functor
F
defined in Lemma
3.11
satisfies conditions
(1)-(3)
from Theorem
3.7
.

Proof.

Condition (1) follows at once from Lemma
3.11
(a). To verify condition (2), fix
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. If
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, let
\xi
be the transfer function of
\mathcal{C}
with
\xi(z)=e
. Then
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)
is the graph of
\xi
and so
F(\mathcal{C})=\xi(z)=e
. Conversely, if
F(\mathcal{C})=e
then all the vertical sections of
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)
are singletons by Lemma
3.11
(b). By compactness of
G
,
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)
is the graph of a continuous map
\xi\colon X\to G
. We claim that
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and that
\xi
is a transfer function of
\mathcal{C}
. Indeed, by definition of
\xi
,
\mathcal{C}(\gamma,z)=\xi(T_{\gamma}z)
for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
and hence

\mathcal{C}(\alpha,T_{\beta}z)=\mathcal{C}(\alpha\beta,z)\mathcal{C}(\beta,z)^{-1}=\xi(T_{\alpha}(T_{\beta}z))\xi(T_{\beta}z)^{-1}=\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)(\alpha,T_{\beta}z)

for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma
. Consequently,
\mathcal{C}=\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)
on
\Gamma\times\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}}(z)
. By minimality of
\mathcal{F}
it follows that
\mathcal{C}=\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)
on the whole
\Gamma\times X
.

To verify condition (3), assume that
G,H\in\mathsf{CAGp}
,
q\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,H)
and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. The map
\mathop{\rm Id}_{X}\times q\colon X\times G\to X\times H
is a morphism of flows
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathcal{F}_{q\mathcal{C}}
and, by compactness of
G
, it is a closed map. Consequently,

(\text{Id}_{X}\times q)\left(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)\right)=\overline{(\text{Id}_{X}\times q)\left(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)\right)}=\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{q\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)

and hence

qF(\mathcal{C})=(\text{Id}_{X}\times q)\left(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)\right)|_{z}=\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{q\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)|_{z}=F(q\mathcal{C}).

Thus, the restriction
q\colon F(\mathcal{C})\to F(q\mathcal{C})
is a well defined morphism of topological groups.
∎

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow. Then the functor
F
defined in Lemma
3.11
satisfies condition
(4)
from Theorem
3.7
.

Proof.

Fix
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
with
\mathcal{C}\simeq\mathcal{D}
. By symmetry of the cohomology relation, it is sufficient to show that
F(\mathcal{C})\subseteq F(\mathcal{D})
. Let
q\colon G\to G/F(\mathcal{D})
be the quotient morphism. Then, by Lemma
3.13
,
F(q\mathcal{D})=qF(\mathcal{D})=e
and hence
q\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G/F(\mathcal{D}))
. Consequently, it follows by cohomology of
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
that
q\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G/F(\mathcal{D}))
and so
qF(\mathcal{C})=F(q\mathcal{C})=e
by Lemma
3.13
. This verifies the desired inclusion
F(\mathcal{C})\subseteq F(\mathcal{D})
.
∎

Now we want to verify that the functor
F
defined in Lemma
3.11
satisfies condition
(5)
from Theorem
3.7
. As a matter of fact, in the following lemma we show that
F
satisfies a slightly stronger condition than continuity from Theorem
3.7
.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow,
G_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}G_{k}
be an inverse system in
\mathsf{CAGp}
indexed by a directed set
J
and, for
j\in J
, let
\mathcal{C}_{j}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G_{j})
. Assume that
\mathcal{C}_{i}\simeq p_{ik}\mathcal{C}_{k}
for all
i\leq k
. Consider the extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\prod_{j\in J}G_{j})
given by
\mathcal{C}(\gamma,x)=(\mathcal{C}_{j}(\gamma,x))_{j\in J}
for
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
x\in X
. Then

F(\mathcal{C})=\lim_{\longleftarrow}\left(F(\mathcal{C}_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}F(\mathcal{C}_{k})\right)=\bigcap_{j\in J}\text{\emph{pr}}_{j}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{j}),

(3.6)

where
\mathop{\rm pr}_{j}
(
j\in J
) are the limit projections for the inverse system
G_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}G_{k}
.

Remark 3.16
.

The assumptions of the lemma yield an inverse system in
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
, provided the condition
\mathcal{C}_{i}\simeq p_{ik}\mathcal{C}_{k}
is strengthened to
\mathcal{C}_{i}=p_{ik}\mathcal{C}_{k}
for all
i\leq k
. In this case the inverse system
\mathcal{C}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}\mathcal{C}_{k}
has many models of an inverse limit in
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
, but all of them are isomorphic to (a co-restriction of) the extension
\mathcal{C}
from the lemma. Recall that these isomorphisms hold in a strict sense, that is, they include the limit projections as well. In view of Lemma
3.13
this means that formula (
3.6
) holds for every model of an inverse limit of
\mathcal{C}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}\mathcal{C}_{k}
in
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
.

Proof of Lemma
3.15
.

Before turning to the proof we fix some notation. For
i\in J
let
e_{i}
be the unit of
G_{i}
and
\pi_{i}
be the projection morphism
\prod_{j\in J}G_{j}\to G_{i}
. The inverse limit
\lim_{\leftarrow}(G_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}G_{k})
will be denoted by
G
and will be considered in the usual way as the subgroup of
\prod_{j\in J}G_{j}
consisting of the points
(g_{j})_{j\in J}
with
g_{i}=p_{ik}(g_{k})
for all
i\leq k
. Finally, for
i\leq k
we fix a transfer function
\xi_{ik}\colon X\to G_{i}
for the coboundary
\mathcal{C}_{i}(p_{ik}\mathcal{C}_{k})^{-1}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G_{i})
.

By Lemmas
3.13
and
3.14
,
F(\mathcal{C}_{i})=F(p_{ik}\mathcal{C}_{k})=p_{ik}F(\mathcal{C}_{k})
for all
i\leq k
. It follows that the inverse system
G_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}G_{k}
restricts to an inverse system
F(\mathcal{C}_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}F(\mathcal{C}_{k})
and so the middle term in (
3.6
) makes sense. Also,

\begin{split}\lim_{\longleftarrow}\left(F(\mathcal{C}_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}F(\mathcal{C}_{k})\right)&=\left\{(g_{j})_{j\in J}\in G:g_{j}\in F(\mathcal{C}_{j})\hskip 2.84526pt\text{for every $j\in J$}\,\right\}\\
&=\bigcap_{j\in J}\text{pr}_{j}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{j}),\end{split}

which verifies the second equality in (
3.6
).

We finish the proof by verifying the equality
F(\mathcal{C})=\bigcap_{j\in J}\mathop{\rm pr}_{j}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{j})
from (
3.6
). To verify inclusion “
\subseteq
”, fix
i\leq k
. By Lemma
3.13
,

\left(\pi_{i}.(p_{ik}\pi_{k})^{-1}\right)F(\mathcal{C})=F\left(\left(\pi_{i}.(p_{ik}\pi_{k})^{-1}\right)\mathcal{C}\right)=F\left(\mathcal{C}_{i}.(p_{ik}\mathcal{C}_{k})^{-1}\right)=F(\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{ik}))=e_{i}.

Thus, for all
i\leq k
and every
g\in F(\mathcal{C})
,
\pi_{i}(g)=p_{ik}\pi_{k}(g)
. Consequently,
F(\mathcal{C})\subseteq G
and hence
\mathop{\rm pr}_{j}F(\mathcal{C})=\pi_{j}F(\mathcal{C})=F(\pi_{j}\mathcal{C})=F(\mathcal{C}_{j})
for every
j\in J
by Lemma
3.13
. This shows that
F(\mathcal{C})\subseteq\bigcap_{j\in J}\mathop{\rm pr}_{j}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{j})
.

To verify the converse inclusion, fix a character
\chi\in F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}
from the annihilator of
F(\mathcal{C})
in
\prod_{j\in J}G_{j}
. There exist a finite set
E\subseteq J
and characters
\chi_{j}\in G_{j}^{*}
(
j\in E
) such that
\chi=\prod_{j\in E}\chi_{j}\pi_{j}
. Since
\chi\in F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}
, we have
F(\chi\mathcal{C})=\chi F(\mathcal{C})=1
and hence, by Lemma
3.13
,
\chi\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. Thus,

\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}\ni\chi\mathcal{C}=\prod_{j\in E}\chi_{j}\pi_{j}\mathcal{C}=\prod_{j\in E}\chi_{j}\mathcal{C}_{j}.

(3.7)

Fix
k\in J
with
j\leq k
for every
j\in E
. Then

\chi_{j}\mathcal{C}_{j}=\chi_{j}\left((p_{jk}\mathcal{C}_{k})\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{jk})\right)=\left((\chi_{j}p_{jk})\mathcal{C}_{k}\right)\mathop{\rm co}(\chi_{j}\xi_{jk})

for every
j\in E
and hence, by virtue of (
3.7
),
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}\ni(\prod_{j\in E}\chi_{j}p_{jk})\mathcal{C}_{k}
. Applying Lemma
3.13
once more yields

\Big(\prod_{j\in E}\chi_{j}p_{jk}\Big)F(\mathcal{C}_{k})=F\Big(\Big(\prod_{j\in E}\chi_{j}p_{jk}\Big)\mathcal{C}_{k}\Big)=1,

whence it follows that

\begin{split}\chi\left(\text{pr}_{k}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{k})\right)&=\Big(\prod_{j\in E}\chi_{j}\pi_{j}\Big)\left(\text{pr}_{k}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{k})\right)=\Big(\prod_{j\in E}\chi_{j}\text{pr}_{j}\Big)\left(\text{pr}_{k}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{k})\right)\\
&=\Big(\prod_{j\in E}\chi_{j}p_{jk}\text{pr}_{k}\Big)\left(\text{pr}_{k}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{k})\right)\subseteq\Big(\prod_{j\in E}\chi_{j}p_{jk}\Big)F(\mathcal{C}_{k})=1.\end{split}

This leads to

\chi\in\left(\text{pr}_{k}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{k})\right)^{\perp}\subseteq\sum_{j\in J}\left(\text{pr}_{j}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{j})\right)^{\perp}=\Big(\bigcap_{j\in J}\text{pr}_{j}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{j})\Big)^{\perp},

where all the annihilators are taken in
\prod_{j\in J}G_{j}
. Thus,
F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}\subseteq(\bigcap_{j\in J}\mathop{\rm pr}_{j}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{j}))^{\perp}
and so, finally,
F(\mathcal{C})\supseteq\bigcap_{j\in J}\mathop{\rm pr}_{j}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{j})
.
∎

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow. Then the functor
F
defined in Lemma
3.11
satisfies condition
(6)
from Theorem
3.7
.

Proof.

Fix
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. If
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
is minimal then
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)=X\times G
and so
F(\mathcal{C})=\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)|_{z}=G
. Conversely, assume that
F(\mathcal{C})=G
; we show that every orbit of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
is dense in
X\times G
. First, we have
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)=X\times G
by Lemma
3.11
(b). Further, given
g\in G
, the right vertical rotation
R_{g}
of
X\times G
by
g
is an automorphism of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
and hence

\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,g)=\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(R_{g}(z,e))=R_{g}\left(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,e)\right)=R_{g}(X\times G)=X\times G.

Finally, if
x\in X
and
h\in G
then
(z,g)\in\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(x,h)
for some
g\in G
by compactness of
G
and minimality of
\mathcal{F}
. Thus,
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(x,h)\supseteq\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(z,g)=X\times G
. Since
x\in X
and
h\in G
were arbitrary, the minimality of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
follows.
∎

Theorem 3.18
.

Given a minimal flow
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
, there exists a unique covariant functor

F^{*}\colon\mathsf{Hom}(\mathsf{DAGp},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})\to\mathsf{DAGp},

which satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

if
h\in\mathsf{Hom}(\mathsf{DAGp},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
has domain
D
then
F^{*}(h)\mathrel{\lhd}D
,

(2)

for every
h\in\mathsf{Hom}(\mathsf{DAGp},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
with domain
D
,
F^{*}(h)=D
if and only if
\mathop{\rm im}(h)\subseteq\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
,

(3)

if
h,k\in\mathsf{Hom}(\mathsf{DAGp},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
and
r\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(h,k)
, then
F^{*}(h)=r^{-1}(F^{*}(k))
and
F^{*}(r)\colon F^{*}(h)\to F^{*}(k)
is the restriction of
r
.

Moreover, the functor
F^{*}
satisfies the following conditions:

(4)

if
h,k\in\mathsf{Hom}(\mathsf{DAGp},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
have a common domain
D
and they differ by an element of
\mathop{\rm Hom}(D,\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}})
then
F^{*}(h)=F^{*}(k)
,

(5)

for every
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and every
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
,
F^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{*})
equals the annihilator
F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}
of
F(\mathcal{C})
in
G^{*}
.

Proof.

We start by showing that conditions (1)-(3) are satisfied by at most one functor
F^{*}
. More concretely, we show that
F^{*}(h)=h^{-1}(\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}})
is the only possible choice for
F^{*}
. So fix
D\in\mathsf{DAGp}
and
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(D,\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
. Denote by
i
the inclusion morphism
h^{-1}(\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}})\to D
. Then
\mathop{\rm im}(hi)\subseteq\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and hence, by virtue of (2),
F^{*}(hi)=h^{-1}(\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}})
. Thus, by virtue of (3),

h^{-1}(\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}})=F^{*}(hi)=i^{-1}(F^{*}(h))\subseteq F^{*}(h).

Now let
j
be the inclusion morphism
F^{*}(h)\to D
. Then condition (3) yields
F^{*}(hj)=j^{-1}(F^{*}(h))=F^{*}(h)
and hence, by condition (2),
h(F^{*}(h))=\mathop{\rm im}(hj)\subseteq\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. Thus,
F^{*}(h)\subseteq h^{-1}(\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}})
. It follows that

F^{*}(h)=h^{-1}(\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}})

(3.8)

is indeed the only possible choice for
F^{*}
.

We verify that
F^{*}
defined by (
3.8
) satisfies conditions (1)-(4). First, if
D\in\mathsf{DAGp}
and
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(D,\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
then
F^{*}(h)
is a subgroup of
D
by definition and condition (1) thus holds. Condition (2) is satisfied obviously. To verify condition (3), fix
h,k\in\mathsf{Hom}(\mathsf{DAGp},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
and
r\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(h,k)
. The equality
F^{*}(h)=r^{-1}(F^{*}(k))
follows directly from the equality
kr=h
and from the definition of
F^{*}
, and this allows to define
F^{*}(r)
as in (3). It is now easy to see that
F^{*}
is a covariant functor
\mathsf{Hom}(\mathsf{DAGp},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})\to\mathsf{DAGp}
. Finally, to verify (4), let
D\in\mathsf{DAGp}
,
h,k\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(D,\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
and
l\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(D,\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}})
be such that
h=l.k
. Since
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is a subgroup of
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
,

F^{*}(h)=F^{*}(l.k)=(l.k)^{-1}(\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}})=k^{-1}(\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}})=F^{*}(k).

This verifies condition (4).

We finish the proof by verifying condition (5). To this end, fix
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. Given
\chi\in G^{*}
, the following conditions are equivalent due to Theorem
3.7
:

•

\chi\in F^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{*})=(\mathcal{C}^{*})^{-1}(\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}})
,

•

\chi\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{*}(\chi)\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
,

•

\chi F(\mathcal{C})=F(\chi\mathcal{C})=1
,

•

\chi\in F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}
.

This verifies condition (5).
∎

3.3
Summation properties

In Theorem
3.7
we saw that the functor
F\colon\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}\to\mathsf{CAGp}
introduced in Section
3.2
is continuous in the sense that it preserves limits of inverse systems. In this section we investigate another continuity property of
F
, namely its relation to infinite sums in the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and the hypersemigroup
2^{G}
. The main question studied here is the following. Assume that a series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{n}
converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. Does it follow that
F(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{n})=\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}F(\mathcal{C}_{n})}
? If not, does at least one of the inclusions “
\supseteq
” or “
\subseteq
” hold?

By considering first the case of a finite sum, we find out in Proposition
3.19
that an appropriate additional assumption in this problem is a mutual group-disjointness of the groups
F(\mathcal{C}_{n})
. In Theorem
3.23
we then show that, under this group-disjointness assumption, the inclusion “
\supseteq
” can be secured by passing to a subsequence
(\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
(\mathcal{C}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Moreover, in Proposition
3.28
we demonstrate that in order to secure this inclusion, passing to a subsequence is also a necessary procedure. Finally, we close this section by showing that the inclusion “
\subseteq
” fails to hold even when we allow passing to a subsequence of
(\mathcal{C}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X
be a minimal flow and let
G_{n}\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
\mathcal{C}_{n}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G_{n})
for
n=1,\dots,m
. If
F(\mathcal{C}_{1}),\dots,F(\mathcal{C}_{m})
are pair-wise group-disjoint then

F(\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{m})=F(\mathcal{C}_{1})\times\dots\times F(\mathcal{C}_{m}).

(3.9)

If, in addition, all the groups
G_{n}=G
are the same then

F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}_{n}\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{m}F(\mathcal{C}_{n}).

(3.10)

Proof.

First we verify (
3.9
