), proceeding by induction on
m
. So let
m=2
; we show that
F(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2})=F(\mathcal{C}_{1})\times F(\mathcal{C}_{2})
. For
i=1,2
denote by
\mathop{\rm pr}_{i}
the projection morphism
G_{1}\times G_{2}\to G_{i}
. Then
\mathop{\rm pr}_{i}F(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2})=F(\mathop{\rm pr}_{i}(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}))=F(\mathcal{C}_{i})
for both
i=1,2
by Theorem
3.7
(3). Consequently,
F(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2})
is a closed subgroup of
F(\mathcal{C}_{1})\times F(\mathcal{C}_{2})
with full projections. Since
F(\mathcal{C}_{1})
and
F(\mathcal{C}_{2})
are group-disjoint by the assumptions, it follows that
F(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2})=F(\mathcal{C}_{1})\times F(\mathcal{C}_{2})
.

Now fix
k\geq 2
; we show that (
3.9
) holds for
m=k+1
, provided it holds for every
m\leq k
. To this end, fix extensions
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{k},\mathcal{C}_{k+1}
with
F(\mathcal{C}_{1}),\dots,F(\mathcal{C}_{k}),F(\mathcal{C}_{k+1})
mutually group-disjoint. By the induction hypothesis for
m=k
,
F(\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{k})=F(\mathcal{C}_{1})\times\dots\times F(\mathcal{C}_{k})
. Consequently, by Lemma
2.6
, the groups
F(\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{k})
and
F(\mathcal{C}_{k+1})
are group-disjoint. Our induction hypothesis for
m=2
thus yields

\begin{split}F(\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{k+1})&=F((\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{k}),\mathcal{C}_{k+1})=F(\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{k})\times F(\mathcal{C}_{k+1})\\
&=F(\mathcal{C}_{1})\times\dots\times F(\mathcal{C}_{k})\times F(\mathcal{C}_{k+1}),\end{split}

as was to be shown.

Now we verify (
3.10
). So assume that all the groups
G_{n}=G
are the same and consider the map

\varphi\colon G^{m}\ni(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})\mapsto g_{1}+\dots+g_{m}\in G.

By commutativity of
G
,
\varphi
is a morphism of topological groups. Thus, by Theorem
3.7
(3) and by (
3.9
),

\begin{split}F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}_{n}\right)&=F(\varphi(\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{m}))=\varphi F(\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{m})\\
&=\varphi\left(F(\mathcal{C}_{1})\times\dots\times F(\mathcal{C}_{m})\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{m}F(\mathcal{C}_{n}).\end{split}

This verifies (
3.10
).
∎

Corollary 3.20
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow,
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. Assume that
F(\mathcal{C})
is connected and
F(\mathcal{D})
is totally disconnected. Then
F(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D})=F(\mathcal{C})F(\mathcal{D})
.

Remark 3.21
.

Under the assumptions of the corollary, let the group
G
be in addition connected and the extension
\mathcal{C}
be minimal (that is,
F(\mathcal{C})=G
). Then the extension
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}
is also minimal. Indeed, the corollary yields
F(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D})=F(\mathcal{C})F(\mathcal{D})=GF(\mathcal{D})=G
, which verifies minimality of
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}
.

Proof of Corollary
3.20
.

Recall that a connected group from
\mathsf{CAGp}
has only connected quotient groups and a totally disconnected group from
\mathsf{CAGp}
has only totally disconnected quotient groups. Thus, by the assumptions of the corollary, the groups
F(\mathcal{C})
,
F(\mathcal{D})
have no common quotient group other than the trivial one and so they are group-disjoint by Lemma
2.4
. By virtue of (
3.10
) from Proposition
3.19
it follows that, indeed,
F(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D})=F(\mathcal{C})F(\mathcal{D})
.
∎

Theorem 3.22
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X
be a minimal flow. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
G_{n}\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
\mathcal{C}_{n}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G_{n})
. Set
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}\left(\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}G_{n}\right)
. If the groups
F(\mathcal{C}_{n})
are pair-wise group-disjoint then

F(\mathcal{C})=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}F(\mathcal{C}_{n}).

Proof.

If we interpret the product
\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}G_{n}
in the usual way as an inverse limit of the groups
\prod_{n=1}^{m}G_{n}
(
m\in\mathbb{N}
) then the statement of the theorem follows from Proposition
3.19
and from Theorem
3.7
(5).

To be more precise, for
i\leq k
let
p_{ik}\colon\prod_{n=1}^{k}G_{n}\to\prod_{n=1}^{i}G_{n}
be given by
p_{ik}(g_{n})_{n=1}^{k}=(g_{n})_{n=1}^{i}
and for
i\in\mathbb{N}
let
p_{i}\colon\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}G_{n}\to\prod_{n=1}^{i}G_{n}
be given by
p_{i}(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=(g_{n})_{n=1}^{i}
. Then
\prod_{n=1}^{i}G_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}\prod_{n=1}^{k}G_{n}
is an inverse system in
\mathsf{CAGp}
,
\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}G_{n}
is its inverse limit and
p_{i}
(
i\in\mathbb{N}
) are the corresponding limit projections. By virtue of Lemma
2.21
,
p_{i}\mathcal{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}p_{k}\mathcal{C}
is an inverse system in
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
,
\mathcal{C}
is its inverse limit and
p_{i}\colon\mathcal{C}\to p_{i}\mathcal{C}
(
i\in\mathbb{N}
) are the corresponding limit projections. Moreover, by Proposition
3.19
,

F(p_{i}\mathcal{C})=F(\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{i})=F(\mathcal{C}_{1})\times\dots\times F(\mathcal{C}_{i})

for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, the identity (
3.6
) from Lemma
3.15
yields

\begin{split}F(\mathcal{C})&=\lim_{\longleftarrow}\left(F(\mathcal{C}_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ik}}}{{\longleftarrow}}F(\mathcal{C}_{k})\right)=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}p_{n}^{-1}F(p_{n}\mathcal{C})\\
&=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}p_{n}^{-1}\left(F(\mathcal{C}_{1})\times\dots\times F(\mathcal{C}_{n})\right)=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}F(\mathcal{C}_{n}),\end{split}

as was to be shown.
∎

Theorem 3.23
.

Let
\Gamma\in\mathsf{LCGp}
,
X
be a locally compact space and
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
. Assume that all
\Gamma
,
X
and
G
are second countable. Let
\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X
be a minimal flow. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
\mathcal{C}_{n}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and assume that the groups
F(\mathcal{C}_{n})
are pair-wise group-disjoint. If
\mathcal{C}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ucs}}{{\longrightarrow}}e
then there exists an increasing sequence
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of positive integers such that the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}}
converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and

F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}}\right)\supseteq\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}F(\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}})},

(3.11)

where the right-hand side of the inclusion above is defined as the closed subgroup of
G
generated by the groups
F(\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}})
:

\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}F(\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}})}=\overline{\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\sum_{l=1}^{n}F(\mathcal{C}_{k_{l}})\right)}.

(3.12)

Remark 3.24
.

Observe that the right-hand side of (
3.12
) can be expressed as a limit in the hypersemigroup
2^{G}
:

\overline{\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\sum_{l=1}^{n}F(\mathcal{C}_{k_{l}})\right)}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{l=1}^{n}F(\mathcal{C}_{k_{l}}).

Before turning to the proof of Theorem
3.23
, we prove three auxiliary lemmas.

Lemma 3.25
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.23
, there exists an increasing sequence of positive integers
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}}
converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
for every subsequence
(l_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

Let us begin by fixing the following objects:

•

a local base
(W_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
at
e
in
G
with
W_{n+1}+W_{n+1}\subseteq W_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

a sequence
(K_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of compact subsets of
\Gamma
, whose interiors cover
\Gamma
and which satisfy
K_{n+1}\supseteq K_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

a sequence
(C_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of compacts subsets of
X
, whose interiors cover
X
and which satisfy
C_{n+1}\supseteq C_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Since
\mathcal{C}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ucs}}{{\longrightarrow}}e
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, there is an increasing sequence
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of positive integers such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}}(\gamma,x)\in W_{n}
for all
\gamma\in K_{n}
and
x\in C_{n}
. We show that the sequence
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
fulfills the condition from the conclusion of the lemma.

Let
(l_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a subsequence of
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Then
l_{n}=k_{m(n)}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
(m(n))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is an increasing sequence of positive integers. (In particular,
m(n)\geq n
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.) We show that the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}}
converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
; we accomplish this by showing that
(\sum_{n=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. To this end, fix a compact set
F\subseteq\Gamma\times X
and a neighbourhood
W
of
e
in
G
. Choose
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
large enough so that
F\subseteq K_{n_{0}}\times C_{n_{0}}
and
W_{n_{0}}\subseteq W
. Then for all
n\geq n_{0}
,
p\in\mathbb{N}
and
(\gamma,x)\in F
,

\begin{split}\sum_{j=1}^{n+p}\mathcal{C}_{l_{j}}(\gamma,x)-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{C}_{l_{j}}(\gamma,x)&=\sum_{j=n+1}^{n+p}\mathcal{C}_{l_{j}}(\gamma,x)=\sum_{j=n+1}^{n+p}\mathcal{C}_{k_{m(j)}}(\gamma,x)\\
&\in\sum_{j=n+1}^{n+p}W_{m(j)}\subseteq\sum_{j=n+1}^{n+p}W_{j}\subseteq W_{n}\subseteq W_{n_{0}}\subseteq W.\end{split}

Thus,
\left(\sum_{n=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}}\right)_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is indeed a Cauchy sequence in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. Since the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is complete, it follows that the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}}
converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. This proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma 3.26
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow with
\Gamma
and
X
locally compact second countable. Assume that
(\mathcal{C}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
with
\mathcal{C}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ucs}}{{\longrightarrow}}1
. If the groups
F(\mathcal{C}_{n})
are pair-wise group-disjoint and
F(\mathcal{C}_{n})\neq 1
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
then there exists an increasing sequence
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of positive integers such that for every subsequence
(l_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}}
converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and

F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}}\right)=\mathbb{T}^{1}=\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}F(\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}})}.

(3.13)

Proof.

First we indicate how to secure the second equality from (
3.13
). By our assumptions on the groups
F(\mathcal{C}_{n})
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
), we have
F(\mathcal{C}_{i})=\mathbb{T}^{1}
for at most one
i\in\mathbb{N}
. By removing the underlying extension
\mathcal{C}_{i}
from our considerations, we get
1\neq F(\mathcal{C}_{n})\subsetneq\mathbb{T}^{1}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there is an integer
p(n)\geq 2
such that
F(\mathcal{C}_{n})=\mathbb{Z}_{p(n)}
. Since the groups
F(\mathcal{C}_{n})
are pair-wise group-disjoint, it follows that the integers
p(n)
are mutually co-prime. Consequently, if
(l_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is an arbitrary increasing sequence of positive integers then

\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}F(\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}})}=\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{Z}_{p(l_{n})}}=\overline{\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p(l_{j})}}=\overline{\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{Z}_{p(l_{1})\dots p(l_{n})}}=\mathbb{T}^{1}.

This verifies the second equality from (
3.13
).

Now we turn to the first equality from (
3.13
). By Lemma
3.25
, we may assume that the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}}
converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
for every increasing sequence of positive integers
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. We fix the following objects:

•

a base point
z\in X
,

•

a basis
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of neighbourhoods of
z
in
X
with
U_{n+1}\subseteq U_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

a basis
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
for the topology of
\mathbb{T}^{1}
,

•

a sequence
(\widetilde{V}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of nonempty open subsets of
\mathbb{T}^{1}
and a basis
(W_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of neighbourhoods of
1
in
\mathbb{T}^{1}
such that
\widetilde{V}_{n}+W_{n}\subseteq V_{n}
and
W_{n+1}+W_{n+1}\subseteq W_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Now we turn to the next step of the proof. Proceeding by induction on
n
, we shall construct an increasing sequence
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of positive integers and a non-decreasing sequence
(F_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of finite subsets of
\Gamma
. Properties of these sequences will be summarized after the induction step of the construction and their importance will become clear in the final step of the proof.

Since the integers
p(n)\geq 2
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
) are mutually co-prime, we have
p(n)\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
and so there is
k_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
F(\mathcal{C}_{k_{1}})\cap\widetilde{V}_{1}=\mathbb{Z}_{p(k_{1})}\cap\widetilde{V}_{1}\neq\emptyset
. By definition of the functor
F
from Lemma
3.11
, there is
\gamma_{0}\in\Gamma
with
T_{\gamma_{0}}z\in U_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{k_{1}}(\gamma_{0},z)\in\widetilde{V}_{1}
. Set
F_{1}=\{\gamma_{0}\}
. Further, let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and assume that we have at our disposal positive integers
k_{1}<\dots<k_{n}
and finite sets
F_{1}\subseteq\dots\subseteq F_{n}\subseteq\Gamma
such that for every
m=1,\dots,n
, the following conditions hold:

1.

given
1\leq i\leq m
and a (possibly empty) set
B\subseteq\{1,\dots,m-1\}
, there is
\gamma\in F_{m}
with

T_{\gamma}z\in U_{m}\hskip 8.53581pt\text{and}\hskip 8.53581pt\left(\sum_{l\in B}\mathcal{C}_{k_{l}}+\mathcal{C}_{k_{m}}\right)(\gamma,z)\in\widetilde{V}_{i},

2.

if
m\geq 2
then
\mathcal{C}_{k_{m}}(\gamma,z)\in W_{m}
for every
\gamma\in F_{m-1}
.

Now we define
k_{n+1}
and
F_{n+1}
. Since
p(n)\to\infty
and
\mathcal{C}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ucs}}{{\longrightarrow}}1
as
n\to\infty
, there is
k_{n+1}>k_{n}
such that

•

F(\mathcal{C}_{k_{n+1}})\cap\widetilde{V}_{i}=\mathbb{Z}_{p(k_{n+1})}\cap\widetilde{V}_{i}\neq\emptyset
for every
i=1,\dots,n+1
and

•

\mathcal{C}_{k_{n+1}}(\gamma,z)\in W_{n+1}
for every
\gamma\in F_{n}
.

Let
B\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
and
1\leq i\leq n+1
. By Proposition
3.19
,

F\left(\sum_{l\in B}\mathcal{C}_{k_{l}}+\mathcal{C}_{k_{n+1}}\right)=\sum_{l\in B}F(\mathcal{C}_{k_{l}})+F(\mathcal{C}_{k_{n+1}})\supseteq F(\mathcal{C}_{k_{n+1}})\cap\widetilde{V}_{i}\neq\emptyset.

Consequently, there is
\gamma_{B}^{i}\in\Gamma
such that

T_{\gamma_{B}^{i}}z\in U_{n+1}\hskip 8.53581pt\text{and}\hskip 8.53581pt\left(\sum_{l\in B}\mathcal{C}_{k_{l}}+\mathcal{C}_{k_{n+1}}\right)(\gamma_{B}^{i},z)\in\widetilde{V}_{i}.

Let
F_{n+1}
be the union of
F_{n}
with all the elements
\gamma_{B}^{i}
(
B\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
,
1\leq i\leq n+1
). Then the following conditions hold:

•

given
1\leq i\leq n+1
and
B\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
, there is
\gamma\in F_{n+1}
with

T_{\gamma}z\in U_{n+1}\hskip 8.53581pt\text{and}\hskip 8.53581pt\left(\sum_{l\in B}\mathcal{C}_{k_{l}}+\mathcal{C}_{k_{n+1}}\right)(\gamma,z)\in\widetilde{V}_{i},

•

for every
\gamma\in F_{n}
,
\mathcal{C}_{k_{n+1}}(\gamma,z)\in W_{n+1}
.

This finishes the induction step. To summarize, we obtained an increasing sequence
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of positive integers and a non-decreasing sequence
(F_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of finite subsets of
\Gamma
such that conditions (1) and (2) hold for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

We show that the sequence
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies the required condition. To this end, let
(l_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a subsequence of
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and write
l_{n}=k_{t(n)}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The convergence of the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}}
u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is guaranteed by one of our earlier arguments in this proof; denote the sum of this series by
\mathcal{C}
. We show that
F(\mathcal{C})=\mathbb{T}^{1}
. To this end, fix a neighbourhood
U
of
z
in
X
and an arbitrary nonempty open subset
V
of
G
; we need to find
\gamma\in\Gamma
so that
T_{\gamma}z\in U
and
\mathcal{C}(\gamma,z)\in V
. Choose
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
U_{n}\subseteq U
and
\overline{V_{n}}\subseteq V
. Clearly,
t(n)\geq n
. Therefore, by definition of the set
F_{t(n)}
, there is
\gamma\in F_{t(n)}
such that

T_{\gamma}z\in U_{t(n)}\hskip 8.53581pt\text{and}\hskip 8.53581pt\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{C}_{k_{t(i)}}\right)(\gamma,z)\in\widetilde{V}_{n}.

Moreover, since
\gamma\in F_{t(n)}
, we have
\mathcal{C}_{k_{t(i)}}(\gamma,z)\in W_{t(i)}
for every
i>n
. Thus, for every
p\in\mathbb{N}
,

\begin{split}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n+p}\mathcal{C}_{l_{i}}\right)(\gamma,z)&=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n+p}\mathcal{C}_{k_{t(i)}}\right)(\gamma,z)=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{C}_{k_{t(i)}}\right)(\gamma,z)+\sum_{i=n+1}^{n+p}\mathcal{C}_{k_{t(i)}}(\gamma,z)\\
&\in\widetilde{V}_{n}+W_{t(n+1)}+W_{t(n+2)}\dots+W_{t(n+p)}\\
&\subseteq\widetilde{V}_{n}+W_{t(n)+1}+W_{t(n)+2}\dots+W_{t(n)+p}\\
&\subseteq\widetilde{V}_{n}+W_{t(n)}\subseteq\widetilde{V}_{n}+W_{n}\subseteq V_{n}.\end{split}

Consequently,
\mathcal{C}(\gamma,z)=(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}})(\gamma,z)\in\overline{V_{n}}\subseteq V
. Since, in addition,
T_{\gamma}z\in U_{t(n)}\subseteq U_{n}\subseteq U
, the proof is finished.
∎

Lemma 3.27
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow with
\Gamma
and
X
locally compact second countable. Assume that
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is a finite set and for
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
\xi_{n}\colon X\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
be a continuous map. If
\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ucs}}{{\longrightarrow}}1
then there exists an increasing sequence
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of positive integers such that for every subsequence
(l_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{l_{n}})
converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and

F\left(\mathcal{C}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{l_{n}})\right)\supseteq F(\mathcal{C})

(3.14)

for every
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.25
we may assume that the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{k_{n}})
converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
for every increasing sequence of positive integers
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Moreover, it follows from the nature of the statement of the lemma that we may restrict to the case when
\mathcal{A}
is a singleton,
\mathcal{A}=\{\mathcal{C}\}
.

Fix a base point
z\in X
along with the following objects:

•

a sequence
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
containing those and only those elements of a fixed countable basis of
\mathbb{T}^{1}
, which intersect the set
F(\mathcal{C})
,

•

a local base
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
at
z
in
X
with
U_{n+1}\subseteq U_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

a local base
(W_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
at
1
in
\mathbb{T}^{1}
and a sequence
(\widetilde{V}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of open subsets of
\mathbb{T}^{1}
such that
F(\mathcal{C})\cap\widetilde{V}_{n}\neq\emptyset
,
W_{n+1}+W_{n+1}\subseteq W_{n}
and
\widetilde{V}_{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{n}\subseteq V_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proceeding by induction on
n
, we shall construct three sequences:

•

a sequence
(\widetilde{U}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of neighbourhoods of
z
in
X
,

•

a sequence
(\gamma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of elements of
\Gamma
,

•

an increasing sequence
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of positive integers,

such that the following conditions hold for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

1.

\widetilde{U}_{n+1}\subseteq\widetilde{U}_{n}\subseteq U_{n}
,

2.

T_{\gamma_{n}}z\in\widetilde{U}_{n}
and
\mathcal{C}(\gamma_{n},z)\in\widetilde{V}_{n}
,

3.

\xi_{k_{l}}(\widetilde{U}_{n})-\xi_{k_{l}}(\widetilde{U}_{n})\subseteq W_{n}
for
1\leq l\leq n-1
,

4.

\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{k_{n}})(\gamma_{l},z)=\xi_{k_{n}}(T_{\gamma_{l}}z)-\xi_{k_{n}}(z)\in W_{n+1}
for
1\leq l\leq n
.

First we define
\widetilde{U}_{1}
,
\gamma_{1}
and
k_{1}
. Set
\widetilde{U}_{1}=U_{1}
. Since
\widetilde{V}_{1}
intersects
F(\mathcal{C})
, there is
\gamma_{1}\in\Gamma
with
T_{\gamma_{1}}z\in\widetilde{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}(\gamma_{1},z)\in\widetilde{V}_{1}
. Finally, by our assumptions on the coboundaries
\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{i})
, there is
k_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{k_{1}})(\gamma_{1},z)=\xi_{k_{1}}(T_{\gamma_{1}}z)-\xi_{k_{1}}(z)\in W_{2}
.

Now assume that
\widetilde{U}_{l}
,
\gamma_{l}
and
k_{l}
are given for
l=1,\dots,n
; we shall define
\widetilde{U}_{n+1}
,
\gamma_{n+1}
and
k_{n+1}
. First, use continuity of the maps
\xi_{k_{1}},\dots,\xi_{k_{n}}
to find a neighbourhood
\widetilde{U}_{n+1}\subseteq\widetilde{U}_{n}\cap U_{n+1}
of
z
in
X
so that
\xi_{k_{l}}(\widetilde{U}_{n+1})-\xi_{k_{l}}(\widetilde{U}_{n+1})\subseteq W_{n+1}
for every
1\leq l\leq n
. Further, since
F(\mathcal{C})\cap\widetilde{V}_{n+1}\neq\emptyset
, there is
\gamma_{n+1}\in\Gamma
with
T_{\gamma_{n+1}}z\in\widetilde{U}_{n+1}
and
\mathcal{C}(\gamma_{n+1},z)\in\widetilde{V}_{n+1}
. Finally, by our assumptions on the coboundaries
\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{i})
, there is
k_{n+1}>k_{n}
such that
\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{k_{n+1}})(\gamma_{l},z)=\xi_{k_{n+1}}(T_{\gamma_{l}}z)-\xi_{k_{n+1}}(z)\in W_{n+2}
for
1\leq l\leq n+1
. This finishes the induction step of our construction.

We finish the proof by showing that the sequence
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
constructed above satisfies the conclusion of the lemma. First,
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is increasing by definition. Let
(l_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a subsequence of
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and write
l_{n}=k_{t(n)}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{l_{n}})
converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
by our argument from the beginning of the proof; denote the sum of this series by
\mathcal{D}
. To verify (
3.14
), fix a neighbourhood
U
of
z
in
X
and an open set
V\subseteq\mathbb{T}^{1}
intersecting
F(\mathcal{C})
; we need to find
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
T_{\gamma}z\in U
and
(\mathcal{C}+\mathcal{D})(\gamma,z)\in V
. Choose
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
\widetilde{U}_{n}\subseteq U
and
\overline{V_{n}}\subseteq V
. If
p\geq n
then, by virtue of (1)–(4) and by our choice of the sets
V_{i},W_{i},\widetilde{V}_{i}
,

\begin{split}&\left(\mathcal{C}+\sum_{m=1}^{p}\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{l_{m}})\right)(\gamma_{n},z)=\mathcal{C}(\gamma_{n},z)+\sum_{m=1}^{p}\mathop{\rm co}\left(\xi_{k_{t(m)}}\right)(\gamma_{n},z)\\
&=\mathcal{C}(\gamma_{n},z)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq m\leq p\\
t(m)\leq n-1\end{subarray}}\left(\xi_{k_{t(m)}}(T_{\gamma_{n}}z)-\xi_{k_{t(m)}}(z)\right)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq m\leq p\\
n\leq t(m)\end{subarray}}\left(\xi_{k_{t(m)}}(T_{\gamma_{n}}z)-\xi_{k_{t(m)}}(z)\right)\\
&\in\widetilde{V}_{n}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq m\leq p\\
t(m)\leq n-1\end{subarray}}W_{n}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq m\leq p\\
n\leq t(m)\end{subarray}}W_{t(m)+1}\subseteq\widetilde{V}_{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}W_{n}+\sum_{i=n+1}^{t(p)+1}W_{i}\\
&\subseteq\widetilde{V}_{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}W_{n}+W_{n}=\widetilde{V}_{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{n}\subseteq V_{n}.\end{split}

Consequently,

(\mathcal{C}+\mathcal{D})(\gamma_{n},z)=\left(\mathcal{C}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{l_{m}})\right)(\gamma_{n},z)\in\overline{V_{n}}\subseteq V.

Since also
T_{\gamma_{n}}z\in\widetilde{U}_{n}\subseteq U
, the proof of the lemma is finished.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.23
.

Before turning to the proof, notice the following facts. First, since the groups
F(\mathcal{C}_{n})
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
) are pair-wise group-disjoint, it follows from Remark
2.5
that

F(\chi_{1}\mathcal{C}_{n})=\chi_{1}F(\mathcal{C}_{n})\perp\chi_{2}F(\mathcal{C}_{m})=F(\chi_{2}\mathcal{C}_{m})

for all
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\in G^{*}
and
n\neq m
. Further, by virtue of Lemma
2.4
and Remark
2.5
, we may assume that all the groups
F(\mathcal{C}_{n})
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
) are totally disconnected and hence that all the groups
F(\chi\mathcal{C}_{n})=\chi F(\mathcal{C}_{n})
(
\chi\in G^{*}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
) are finite. Finally, by Lemma
3.25
, we may assume that the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}}
converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
(and hence the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\chi\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}}
converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
for every
\chi\in G^{*}
) for every increasing sequence of positive integers
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. We divide the proof into two steps.

1st step.
Since the group
G
is second countable, its dual group
G^{*}
is countable. Write
G^{*}=\{\chi_{m}:m\in\mathbb{N}\}
. We show that there exists a double sequence of positive integers
(k_{m,n})_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that the following conditions hold for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
:

(1)

the sequence
(k_{m,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is increasing and contains
(k_{m+1,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
as its subsequence,

(2)

either all the groups
F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{m,n}})
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
) are trivial or none of them is trivial,

(3)

if
F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{m,n}})\neq 1
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
then

F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}}\right)=\mathbb{T}^{1}=\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}})}

for every subsequence
(l_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of the sequence
(k_{m,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,

(4)

if
F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{m,n}})=1
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
then

F\left(\sum_{l\in B}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{l}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}}\right)\supseteq\sum_{l\in B}F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{l})

for every subsequence
(l_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of the sequence
(k_{m,n})_{n\geq m+1}
and every subset
B
of the set
\{1,2,\dots,k_{m,m}-1\}
.

We shall construct the required double sequence
(k_{m,n})_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}
by induction on
m
. So fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
and assume that
(k_{j,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
(
j=1,\dots,m-1
) are sequences of positive integers satisfying conditions expressed in (1)–(4). In order to construct
(k_{m,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, we distinguish two cases.

First assume that
F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{m-1,n}})\neq 1
for infinitely many indexes
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a subsequence of
(k_{m-1,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{t_{n}})\neq 1
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Since the groups
F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{t_{n}})
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
) are pair-wise group-disjoint, Lemma
3.26
yields a subsequence
(k_{m,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}}\right)=\mathbb{T}^{1}=\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}})}

for every subsequence
(l_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
(k_{m,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. It follows directly from our definition of
(k_{m,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
that conditions (1)–(4) hold true.

Now assume that
F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{m-1,n}})=1
(that is,
\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{m-1,n}}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
) for all but finitely many
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a subsequence of
(k_{m-1,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{t_{n}}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Set
k_{m,i}=t_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,m
and let
\mathcal{A}
be the set of all extensions of the form
\sum_{l\in B}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{l}
, where
B\subseteq\{1,2,\dots,k_{m,m}-1\}
. By virtue of Lemma
3.27
, there is a subsequence
(k_{m,n})_{n\geq m+1}
of
(t_{n})_{n\geq m+1}
such that

F\left(\mathcal{C}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}}\right)\supseteq F(\mathcal{C})

for every subsequence
(l_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
(k_{m,n})_{n\geq m+1}
and for every
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{A}
. It follows directly from our definition of
(k_{m,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
that conditions (1)–(3) hold true. Condition (4) follows from the definition of
(k_{m,n})_{n\geq m+1}
and from the equality
F(\sum_{l\in B}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{l})=\sum_{l\in B}F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{l})
for every subset
B
of
\{1,2,\dots,k_{m,m}-1\}
, the latter being secured by Proposition
3.19
.

2nd step.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}
set
k_{n}=k_{n,n}
. Clearly,
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is an increasing sequence of positive integers. We show that it satisfies the condition expressed in Theorem
3.23
.

The convergence of the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}}
u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
has already been justified at the beginning of the proof. In order to verify (
3.11
), fix a character
\chi
of
G
and choose
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\chi=\chi_{m}
. Then
(k_{n,n})_{n=m}^{\infty}
is a subsequence of
(k_{m,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and hence, by condition (2), there are two possibilities:

(i)

F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}})=1
for every
n\geq m
,

(ii)

F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}})\neq 1
for every
n\geq m
.

We wish to show that in both these cases,

F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}}\right)\supseteq\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}})}.

(3.15)

First consider case (i). Set
B=\{k_{n,n}:1\leq n\leq m-1\}
and
(l_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=(k_{n,n})_{n\geq m+1}
. Clearly,
B
is a subset of
\{1,2,\dots,k_{m,m}-1\}
. Moreover, since
(l_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a subsequence of
(k_{m+1,n})_{n\geq m+1}
by definition and since
(k_{m+1,n})_{n\geq m+1}
is a subsequence of
(k_{m,n})_{n\geq m+1}
, it follows that
(l_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a subsequence of
(k_{m,n})_{n\geq m+1}
. Thus, by applying condition (4) and the inclusion
\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
for every
n\geq m
, we obtain the desired inclusion (
3.15
):

\begin{split}F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}}\right)&=F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}}\right)\\
&=F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{m-1}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}}+\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{m,m}}+\sum_{n=m+1}^{\infty}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}}\right)\\
&=F\left(\sum_{l\in B}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{l}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}}\right)\supseteq\sum_{l\in B}F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{l})\\
&=\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{m-1}F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}})}=\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}})}=\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}})}.\end{split}

Now consider case (ii). Set
(l_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=(k_{n,n})_{n=m}^{\infty}
. Since
(l_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a subsequence of
(k_{m,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, condition (3) yields

F\left(\sum_{n=m}^{\infty}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}}\right)=F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}}\right)=\mathbb{T}^{1}=\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{l_{n}})}=\overline{\sum_{n=m}^{\infty}F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}})}.

Further, since the groups
F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}})
(
1\leq n\leq m-1
) are pair-wise group-disjoint and finite, it follows from Proposition
3.19
that the group

F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{m-1}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}}\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{m-1}F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}})

is also finite. Thus, by virtue of Corollary
3.20
,

\begin{split}F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}}\right)&=F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}}\right)=F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{m-1}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}}+\sum_{n=m}^{\infty}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}}\right)\\
&=F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{m-1}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}}\right)+F\left(\sum_{n=m}^{\infty}\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}}\right)\\
&=\mathbb{T}^{1}\supseteq\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n,n}})}=\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}F(\chi_{m}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}})}.\end{split}

This verifies the desired inclusion (
3.15
) also in case (ii).

To summarize, for every
\chi\in G^{*}
, inclusion (
3.15
) yields

\begin{split}\chi F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}}\right)&=F\left(\chi\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}}\right)=F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\chi\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}}\right)\supseteq\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}F(\chi\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}})}\\
&=\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\chi F(\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}})}=\overline{\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\chi F(\mathcal{C}_{k_{l}})}=\chi\left(\overline{\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{l=1}^{n}F(\mathcal{C}_{k_{l}})}\right)\\
&=\chi\left(\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}F(\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}})}\right).\end{split}

Thus, since
F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{k_{n}}\right)
is a closed subgroup of
G
, inclusion (
3.11
) from the theorem follows at last.
∎

Proposition 3.28
.

Let
\Gamma\in\mathsf{LCGp}\setminus\mathsf{CGp}
be amenable,
X
be a compact space and
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
be connected. Assume that
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
is a minimal flow with a free point. If all
\Gamma
,
X
and
G
are second countable then there exists a sequence
(\mathcal{C}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
such that the groups
F(\mathcal{C}_{n})
are pair-wise group-disjoint, the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{n}
converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and

F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{n}\right)=e\subseteq G=\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}F\left(\mathcal{C}_{n}\right)}.

(3.16)

Proof.

Denote by
\mathop{\rm\overline{\bf{B}}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
the closure of
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. By our assumptions,
G
contains a dense one-parameter subgroup and so it has a dense identity arc-component
G_{a}
. Consequently, by virtue of
[
9
, Theorem 6]
, there is
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm\overline{\bf{B}}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
such that the induced skew product
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
on
X\times G
is topologically transitive (that is, possesses a dense orbit). By our definition of the functor
F
,
F(\mathcal{C})=G
. Now fix a sequence
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of continuous maps
X\to G
such that
\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{n})\to\mathcal{C}
u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. Set
\mathcal{C}_{1}=\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}=-\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{1})=\mathop{\rm co}\left(-\xi_{1}\right)
and
\mathcal{C}_{n}=\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{n-2})-\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{n-1})=\mathop{\rm co}\left(\xi_{n-2}-\xi_{n-1}\right)
for
n\geq 3
. Then
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{C}_{i}=\mathcal{C}-\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{n-1})
for every
n\geq 2
and so the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{n}
converges u.c.s. to the identity
e
of
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. The two equalities from (
3.16
) now hold obviously. Finally, since
F(\mathcal{C}_{1})=G
and
F(\mathcal{C}_{n})=e
for every
n\geq 2
, the groups
F(\mathcal{C}_{n})
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
) are pair-wise group-disjoint.
∎

Theorem 3.29
.

Let
\Gamma\in\mathsf{LCGp}\setminus\mathsf{CGp}
be amenable,
X
be a compact space and
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
be connected. Assume that
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
is a minimal flow with a free point
z
. If all
\Gamma
,
X
and
G
are second countable then there exists a sequence
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of continuous maps
X\to G
such that for every increasing sequence of positive integers
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{k_{n}})
converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and

F\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{k_{n}})\right)=G\supseteq e=\overline{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}F\left(\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{k_{n}})\right)}.

Before turning to the proof of Theorem
3.29
, we prove the following auxiliary lemma.

Lemma 3.30
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.29
, the following statement holds. Given a neighbourhood
U
of
z
in
X
, nonempty open sets
V_{1},\dots,V_{n}\subseteq G
, a compact set
K\subseteq\Gamma
and a neighbourhood
W
of
e
in
G
, there exist a continuous map
\xi\colon X\to G
and points
\gamma^{1},\dots,\gamma^{n}\in\Gamma
such that

(i)

T_{\gamma^{j}}z\in U
and
\xi(T_{\gamma^{j}}z)-\xi(z)\in V_{j}
for every
j=1,\dots,n
and

(ii)

\xi(T_{\gamma}x)-\xi(x)\in W
for all
\gamma\in K
and
x\in X
.

Proof.

Let
\mathop{\rm\overline{\bf{B}}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
be the closure of
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. By our assumptions,
G
has a dense identity arc-component
G_{a}
. Consequently, by virtue of
[
9
, Theorem 6]
, the extensions
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm\overline{\bf{B}}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
with
F(\mathcal{C})=G
form a residual subset of
\mathop{\rm\overline{\bf{B}}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
(see also the proof of Proposition
3.28
). In particular, such an extension
\mathcal{C}
exists within
[K\times X;W]\cap\mathop{\rm\overline{\bf{B}}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. Thus, there are
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm\overline{\bf{B}}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and
\gamma^{1},\dots,\gamma^{n}\in\Gamma
such that

•

T_{\gamma^{j}}z\in U
and
\mathcal{C}(\gamma^{j},z)\in V_{j}
for every
j=1,\dots,n
and

•

\mathcal{C}(\gamma,x)\in W
for all
\gamma\in K
and
x\in X
.

The two conditions above are clearly open with respect to
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm\overline{\bf{B}}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and hence they are satisfied also by an appropriate coboundary. That is, there is a continuous map
\xi\colon X\to G
such that

•

T_{\gamma^{j}}z\in U
and
\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)(\gamma^{j},z)\in V_{j}
for every
j=1,\dots,n
and

•

\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)(\gamma,x)\in W
for all
\gamma\in K
and
x\in X
.

This verifies both conditions (i) and (ii).
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.29
.

We start by fixing the following objects:

•

a local base
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
at
z
in
X
with
U_{n+1}\subseteq U_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

a sequence
(K_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of compact subsets of
\Gamma
, whose interiors cover
\Gamma
and which satisfy
K_{n}\subseteq K_{n+1}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

a basis
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
for the topology of
G
,

•

a local base
(W_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
at
e
in
G
and a sequence
(\widetilde{V}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of nonempty open subsets of
G
with
W_{n+1}+W_{n+1}\subseteq W_{n}
and
\widetilde{V}_{i}+W_{n}+W_{n}\subseteq V_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n<\infty
.

We claim that there exist

•

a sequence
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of continuous maps
X\to G
,

•

a sequence
(\widetilde{U}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of neighbourhoods of
z
in
X
and

•

a family
\gamma_{n}^{j}
(
1\leq j\leq n<\infty
) of elements of
\Gamma
,

such that the following conditions hold:

(a)

\widetilde{U}_{n}\subseteq U_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

(b)

\xi_{i}(x)-\xi_{i}(z)\in W_{2n-1}
for
x\in\widetilde{U}_{n}
and
1\leq i\leq n-1
,

(c)

T_{\gamma_{n}^{j}}z\in\widetilde{U}_{n}
and
\xi_{n}(T_{\gamma_{n}^{j}}z)-\xi_{n}(z)\in\widetilde{V}_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq n<\infty
,

(d)

\xi_{n}(T_{\gamma}x)-\xi_{n}(x)\in W_{n}
for
\gamma\in K_{n}\cup\{\gamma_{k}^{j}:1\leq j\leq k\leq n-1\}
and
x\in X
.

The construction of these objects follows easily by induction on
n
. Indeed, assume that the maps
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n-1}
, the sets
\widetilde{U}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{U}_{n-1}
and the points
\gamma_{k}^{j}
(
1\leq j\leq k\leq n-1
) are given. Fix a neighbourhood
\widetilde{U}_{n}
of
z
in
X
small enough so that
\widetilde{U}_{n}\subseteq U_{n}
and
\xi_{i}(x)-\xi_{i}(z)\in W_{2n-1}
for all
x\in\widetilde{U}_{n}
and
i=1,\dots,n-1
. Finally, use Lemma
3.30
to find a continuous map
\xi_{n}\colon X\to G
and points
\gamma_{n}^{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}^{n}\in\Gamma
such that

•

T_{\gamma_{n}^{j}}z\in\widetilde{U}_{n}
and
\xi_{n}(T_{\gamma_{n}^{j}}z)-\xi_{n}(z)\in\widetilde{V}_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,n
and

•

\xi_{n}(T_{\gamma}x)-\xi_{n}(x)\in W_{n}
for all
\gamma\in K_{n}\cup\{\gamma_{k}^{j}:1\leq j\leq k\leq n-1\}
and
x\in X
.

Then, clearly, all the conditions (a)–(d) hold true.

We show that the sequence
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies the conclusion of the theorem. To this end, fix an increasing sequence of positive integers
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. First, we verify the u.c.s. convergence of
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{k_{n}})
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. So let
K\subseteq\Gamma
be a compact set and
W
be a neighbourhood of
e
in
G
. Choose
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
with
K\subseteq K_{n_{0}}
and
W_{n_{0}}\subseteq W
. Let
\gamma\in K
,
x\in X
,
n\geq n_{0}
and
p\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
K_{k_{l}}\supseteq K_{l}\supseteq K_{n_{0}}\supseteq K
for every
l\geq n_{0}
, it follows from condition (d) above that

\begin{split}\sum_{l=1}^{n+p}\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{k_{l}})(\gamma,x)-\sum_{l=1}^{n}\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{k_{l}})(\gamma,x)&=\sum_{l=n+1}^{n+p}\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{k_{l}})(\gamma,x)\\
&=\sum_{l=n+1}^{n+p}\left(\xi_{k_{l}}(T_{\gamma}x)-\xi_{k_{l}}(x)\right)\\
&\in\sum_{l=n+1}^{n+p}W_{k_{l}}\subseteq\sum_{l=n+1}^{n+p}W_{l}\subseteq W_{n}\subseteq W_{n_{0}}\subseteq W.\end{split}

By completeness of
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{k_{n}})
converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. Denote the sum of this series by
\mathcal{C}
.

We show that
\mathcal{C}(\gamma_{k_{n}}^{n},z)\in\overline{V_{n}}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. So fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
and let
p\in\mathbb{N}
. We shall make use of the following observations.

•

Given
1\leq l\leq n-1
, we have
T_{\gamma_{k_{n}}^{n}}z\in\widetilde{U}_{k_{n}}
by virtue of (c). Hence
\xi_{k_{l}}(T_{\gamma_{k_{n}}^{n}}z)-\xi_{k_{l}}(z)\in W_{2k_{n}-1}\subseteq W_{2n-1}
by virtue of (b).

•

We have
\xi_{k_{n}}(T_{\gamma_{k_{n}}^{n}}z)-\xi_{k_{n}}(z)\in\widetilde{V}_{n}
by virtue of (c).

•

Given
n+1\leq l\leq n+p
, we have
\xi_{k_{l}}(T_{\gamma_{k_{n}}^{n}}z)-\xi_{k_{l}}(z)\in W_{k_{l}}\subseteq W_{l}
by virtue of (d).

These observations yield

\begin{split}\sum_{l=1}^{n+p}\mathop{\rm co}(\xi_{k_{l}})(\gamma_{k_{n}}^{n},z)&=\sum_{l=1}^{n-1}\left(\xi_{k_{l}}(T_{\gamma_{k_{n}}^{n}}z)-\xi_{k_{l}}(z)\right)+\left(\xi_{k_{n}}(T_{\gamma_{k_{n}}^{n}}z)-\xi_{k_{n}}(z)\right)+\\
&+\sum_{l=n+1}^{n+p}\left(\xi_{k_{l}}(T_{\gamma_{k_{n}}^{n}}z)-\xi_{k_{l}}(z)\right)\\
&\in\sum_{l=1}^{n-1}W_{2n-1}+\widetilde{V}_{n}+\sum_{l=n+1}^{n+p}W_{l}\subseteq\sum_{l=1}^{n-1}W_{n+l}+\widetilde{V}_{n}+\sum_{l=n+1}^{n+p}W_{l}\\
&\subseteq W_{n}+\widetilde{V}_{n}+W_{n}\subseteq V_{n}.\end{split}

Since these inclusions hold for every
p\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows that, indeed,
\mathcal{C}(\gamma_{k_{n}}^{n},z)\in\overline{V_{n}}
.

We finish the proof of the theorem by showing that
F(\mathcal{C})=G
. To this end, fix a neighbourhood
U
of
z
in
X
and a nonempty open set
V\subseteq G
; we need to find
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
T_{\gamma}z\in U
and
\mathcal{C}(\gamma,z)\in V
. Choose
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
U_{n}\subseteq U
and
\overline{V_{n}}\subseteq V
, and set
\gamma=\gamma_{k_{n}}^{n}
. Then
T_{\gamma}z\in\widetilde{U}_{k_{n}}\subseteq U_{k_{n}}\subseteq U_{n}\subseteq U
by virtue of (c) and (a), and
\mathcal{C}(\gamma,z)\in\overline{V_{n}}\subseteq V
by our argument above.
∎

3.4
The ext-topology

In the previous section we had an opportunity to recall
[
9
, Theorem 6]
, which states that under mild assumptions on a minimal flow
\mathcal{F}
and a group
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
, a generic extension from the closure of
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is minimal; in particular,
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is not a closed subgroup of
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
in the topology of u.c.s. convergence. Consequently, the quotient topology on
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is not Hausdorff and so
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
with this topology is not a topological group in the sense used in this work.

Our aim in this section is to introduce a natural (Hausdorff) group topology
\tau_{ext}
on
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. We are motivated by our results from the preceding sections, where we discussed two continuity properties of the functor
F
. Being inspired by these considerations, we are searching for a group topology on
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, with respect to which the map
F\colon\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\to 2^{G}
would be continuous. Our main result in this section, namely Theorem
3.34
, shows that the coarsest topology with these properties does exist and is induced by a natural
2^{G}
-valued translation-invariant metric on
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. We call this topology the
ext-topology
and denote it by
\tau_{ext}
.

Before turning to our results recall that for
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
, the hyper-semigroup
2^{G}
over
G
carries the Vietoris topology. Moreover, by Lemma
2.2
, for every
K\in 2^{G}
, the sets

\mathcal{U}_{V}(K)=\left\{H\in 2^{G}:H\subseteq VK\hskip 5.69054pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69054ptK\subseteq VH\right\}

form a local base at
K
in
2^{G}
, when
V
runs through the neighbourhoods of
e
in
G
. Consequently, given a net
(H_{i})_{i\in I}
of closed subgroups of
G
, the following conditions are equivalent:

•

H_{i}\to e
in
2^{G}
,

•

for every neighbourhood
V
of
e
in
G
, there is
i_{0}\in I
such that
H_{i}\subseteq V
for every
i\geq i_{0}
.

Lemma 3.31
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow and
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
. If
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
then the following statements hold:

(a)

F(\mathcal{C})=e
if and only if
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
,

(b)

F(\mathcal{C}^{-1})=F(\mathcal{C})^{-1}=F(\mathcal{C})
,

(c)

F(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D})\subseteq F(\mathcal{C})F(\mathcal{D})
.

Proof.

Statement (a) follows from Theorem
3.7
(2). Since the inversion in
G
is an automorphism of
G
and
F(\mathcal{C})
is a subgroup of
G
, statement (b) follows from Theorem
3.7
(3). To verify statement (c), let
\varphi\colon G\times G\to G
be the product in
G
and for
i=1,2
, let
\mathop{\rm pr}_{i}\colon G\times G\to G
be the projection on the
i
-th coordinate. By Theorem
3.7
(3),
\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}F(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})=F(\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}))=F(\mathcal{C})
and, similarly,
\mathop{\rm pr}_{2}F(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})=F(\mathop{\rm pr}_{2}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}))=F(\mathcal{D})
. Consequently,
F(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\subseteq F(\mathcal{C})\times F(\mathcal{D})
. By applying Theorem
3.7
(3) once more, we obtain

F(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D})=F(\varphi(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}))=\varphi(F(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}))\subseteq\varphi(F(\mathcal{C})\times F(\mathcal{D}))=F(\mathcal{C})F(\mathcal{D}),

as was to be shown.
∎

Proposition 3.32
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow and
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
. For
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
set
\delta(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})=F(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}^{-1})
. If
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
then the following statements hold:

(i)

\delta(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})=e
if and only if
\mathcal{C}\simeq\mathcal{D}
,

(ii)

\delta(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})=\delta(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C})
,

(iii)

\delta(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\subseteq\delta(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B})\delta(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{D})
,

(iv)

\delta(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{B},\mathcal{D}\mathcal{B})=\delta(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
.

Remark 3.33
.

The proposition states that
\delta
is a translation-invariant
2^{G}
-valued pseudo-metric on
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, which identifies precisely the pairs of equivalent extensions. It follows immediately from the definition of
\delta
and from conditions (i)–(iv) above that
\delta
gives rise to a translation-invariant
2^{G}
-valued metric on
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)=\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)/\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)=\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)/\simeq
.

Proof of Proposition
3.32
.

Statement (i) follows from statement (a) in Lemma
3.31
. Statement (ii) is a direct consequence of statement (b) from Lemma
3.31
. Statement (iii) follows from statement (c) of Lemma
3.31
:

\begin{split}\delta(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})&=F(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}^{-1})=F\left(\left(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{B}^{-1}\right)\left(\mathcal{B}\mathcal{D}^{-1}\right)\right)\subseteq F\left(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{B}^{-1}\right)F\left(\mathcal{B}\mathcal{D}^{-1}\right)\\
&=\delta(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B})\delta(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{D}).\end{split}

Finally, statement (iv) follows at once from the definition of
\delta
.
∎

Now let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow and
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
. We wish to introduce a topology on
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
by using the metric
\delta
defined above. Given a neighbourhood
V
of
e
in
G
and an extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, let
B(\mathcal{C},V)
be the set of all
\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
such that
\delta(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C})\subseteq V
. As follows from Theorem
3.34
below, the sets
B(\mathcal{C},V)
form a basis for a group topology on
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. We shall call it the
ext-topology
and denote it by
\tau_{ext}
.

Theorem 3.34
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow and
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
. Then the collection of sets
B(\mathcal{C},V)
, where
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and
e\in V\subseteq G
is open, forms a basis for a topology
\tau_{ext}
on
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. Moreover,
\tau_{ext}
is the coarsest of all the topologies on
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, with respect to which
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is a (Hausdorff) topological group and the mapping
F\colon\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\to 2^{G}
is continuous.

Remark 3.35
.

The following facts will follow from our arguments in the proof of the theorem.

•

The topology
\tau_{ext}
is in fact the coarsest of all the (Hausdorff) topologies on
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, with respect to which the mapping
F\colon\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\to 2^{G}
is continuous and
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is a semi-topological semigroup (that is, the product in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is separately continuous).

•

For a fixed
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, the sets
B(\mathcal{C},V)
form a local base at
\mathcal{C}
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
with
\tau_{ext}
, when
V
runs through the neighbourhoods of
e
in
G
.

The following observations will also be useful to us.

•

A net
(\mathcal{C}_{i})
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
converges to
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
with respect to
\tau_{ext}
if and only if the net
(F(\mathcal{C}_{i}\mathcal{C}^{-1}))
converges to
e
in
2^{G}
; this follows from the previous statement.

•

If
G
is a Lie group (that is, if
G_{0}
is a finite-dimensional torus and is open in
G
) then
G
has no small subgroups and the topology
\tau_{ext}
on
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is therefore discrete. Thus, in particular, the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is always discrete.

Proof of Theorem
3.34
.

First, we verify that the sets
B(\mathcal{C},V)
form a basis for a Hausdorff topology on
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. Their union is clearly the whole set
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, since
B(\mathcal{C},V)
contains
\mathcal{C}
for all
\mathcal{C}
and
V
.

Now let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and
V,W
be neighbourhoods of
e
in
G
with
\mathcal{E}\in B(\mathcal{C},V)\cap B(\mathcal{D},W)
; we claim that
B(\mathcal{E},N)\subseteq B(\mathcal{C},V)\cap B(\mathcal{D},W)
for an appropriate neighbourhood
N
of
e
in
G
. (In particular, this will verify the second and hence also the third statement from Remark
3.35
.) By definition,
\delta(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{C})
and
\delta(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})
are closed subgroups of
G
and they are contained in
V
and
W
, respectively. Consequently, there is a neighbourhood
N
of
e
in
G
with
N\delta(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{C})\subseteq V
and
N\delta(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})\subseteq W
. We claim that
B(\mathcal{E},N)\subseteq B(\mathcal{C},V)\cap B(\mathcal{D},W)
. Indeed, if
\mathcal{B}\in B(\mathcal{E},N)
then, by Proposition
3.32
(iii),
\delta(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})\subseteq\delta(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{E})\delta(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{C})\subseteq N\delta(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{C})\subseteq V
and hence
\mathcal{B}\in B(\mathcal{C},V)
. By a similar argument,
B(\mathcal{E},N)\subseteq B(\mathcal{D},W)
.

We show that
\tau_{ext}
satisfies the Hausdorff condition. If
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
are distinct then
\delta(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\neq e
by Proposition
3.32
(i). It follows that
\delta(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\not\subseteq V^{2}
for an appropriate neighbourhood
V
of
e
in
G
. We claim that
B(\mathcal{C},V)\cap B(\mathcal{D},V)=\emptyset
. Indeed, if
\mathcal{E}\in B(\mathcal{C},V)\cap B(\mathcal{D},V)
then, by Proposition
3.32
(iii, ii),

\delta(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\subseteq\delta(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})\delta(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})=\delta(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{C})\delta(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})\subseteq VV=V^{2},

a contradiction.

Now we verify the second statement of the theorem. We start by showing that
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is a topological group when equipped with the topology
\tau_{ext}
and that the mapping
F
is continuous. First, we verify the continuity of the product in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. To this end, fix
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and a neighbourhood
V
of
e
in
G
; we shall find a neighbourhood
W
of
e
in
G
with
B(\mathcal{C},W)B(\mathcal{D},W)\subseteq B(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D},V)
. Choose a neighbourhood
W
of
e
in
G
with
W^{2}\subseteq V
. Then for all
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\in B(\mathcal{C},W)
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in B(\mathcal{D},W)
,

\delta(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\mathcal{D}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D})\subseteq\delta(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\mathcal{D}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\mathcal{D})\delta(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D})=\delta(\mathcal{D}^{\prime},\mathcal{D})\delta(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{C})\subseteq WW\subseteq V

by virtue of Proposition
3.32
. Consequently,
B(\mathcal{C},W)B(\mathcal{D},W)\subseteq B(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D},V)
, which verifies the continuity of the product in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. The continuity of the inversion in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
follows from the identity
B(\mathcal{C},V)^{-1}=B(\mathcal{C}^{-1},V)
, which holds by Lemma
3.31
(b).

To show that
F
is continuous, fix
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and a neighbourhood
V
of
e
in
G
. By virtue of Lemma
2.2
, it suffices to show that
F(\mathcal{D})\in\mathcal{U}_{V}(F(\mathcal{C}))
for every
\mathcal{D}\in B(\mathcal{C},V)
. If
\mathcal{D}\in B(\mathcal{C},V)
then

F(\mathcal{D})=F((\mathcal{D}\mathcal{C}^{-1})\mathcal{C})\subseteq F(\mathcal{D}\mathcal{C}^{-1})F(\mathcal{C})=\delta(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{C})F(\mathcal{C})\subseteq VF(\mathcal{C})

by Lemma
3.31
(c), and
F(\mathcal{C})\subseteq VF(\mathcal{D})
by a similar argument. Thus,
F(\mathcal{D})\in\mathcal{U}_{V}(F(\mathcal{C}))
for every
\mathcal{D}\in B(\mathcal{C},V)
, as was to be shown.

Finally, let
\tau
be a group topology on
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, with respect to which the map
F
is continuous; we show that
\tau_{ext}
is coarser than
\tau
. To this end, fix a net
(\mathcal{C}_{i})
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and assume that
\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
with respect to
\tau
. Since
\tau
is a group topology on
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
, it follows that
\mathcal{C}_{i}\mathcal{C}^{-1}\to e
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
with respect to
\tau
. Consequently, by continuity of
F
,
F(\mathcal{C}_{i}\mathcal{C}^{-1})\to F(e)=e
in
2^{G}
. By the third part of Remark
3.35
, it follows that
\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
with respect to
\tau_{ext}
. This shows that
\tau_{ext}
is coarser than
\tau
, as was to be shown.
∎

3.5
Lifts of extensions

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow. In this section we study the problem of lifting extensions of
\mathcal{F}
across covering morphisms of abelian topological groups. In Theorem
3.38
we show that this is always possible if the acting group
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{F}
is simply connected. (On the other hand, it is no longer possible if the assumption of simple connectedness is moved from
\Gamma
to the phase space
X
of
\mathcal{F}
; this is shown in Example
3.40
.) Then we use this result to find the structure of a real separable Fréchet or Banach space in the groups
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
for connected second countable groups
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
, see Corollary
3.41
. Finally, in Theorem
3.43
we show that for flows
\mathcal{F}
with simply connected acting groups
\Gamma\in\mathsf{CLAC}
and with compact second countable phase spaces
X
satisfying
\pi^{1}(X)\neq 0
, the groupoid
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinH}_{\mathcal{F}}}
contains an isomorphic copy of the group
\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 3.36
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X
be a minimal flow with a connected acting group
\Gamma
,
G,G^{\prime}
be abelian topological groups and
p\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{\prime},G)
be a morphism with a totally disconnected kernel. Given
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and a continuous map
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\colon\Gamma\times X\to G^{\prime}
with
p\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}
, the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G^{\prime})
,

(b)

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(1,x)=e
for every
x\in X
,

(c)

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(1,z)=e
for some
z\in X
.

Remark 3.37
.

Our assumptions on
p
are satisfied if
p
is a covering morphism. Indeed, recall that an epimorphism
p\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{\prime},G)
between topological groups
G^{\prime},G
is a covering map if and only if it is a quotient (equivalently, open) map with a discrete kernel
\ker(p)\subseteq G^{\prime}
.

Proof of Lemma
3.36
.

Implication (a)
\Rightarrow
(b) follows from the cocycle identity and implication (b)
\Rightarrow
(c) is clear. We show that (c)
\Rightarrow
(b). To this end, consider the map
\varphi\colon\Gamma\to G^{\prime}
, given by
\varphi(\gamma)=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(1,T_{\gamma}z)
. Then
p\varphi(\gamma)=p\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(1,T_{\gamma}z)=\mathcal{C}(1,T_{\gamma}z)=e
for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
and so
\varphi
takes its values in
\ker(p)
. Since
\varphi
is continuous,
\Gamma
is connected and
\ker(p)
is totally disconnected, it follows that
\varphi
is constant with the value
\varphi(1)=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(1,z)=e
. That is to say,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(1,x)=e
for every
x
from the
\mathcal{F}
-orbit of
z
. Condition (b) now follows by minimality of
\mathcal{F}
.

We finish the proof by showing that (b)
\Rightarrow
(a). To this end, fix
x\in X
and consider the map
\psi\colon\Gamma\times\Gamma\to G^{\prime}
, given by
\psi(\alpha,\beta)=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\alpha,T_{\beta}x)\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\beta,x)\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\alpha\beta,x)^{-1}
. Then
p\psi(\alpha,\beta)=\mathcal{C}(\alpha,T_{\beta}x)\mathcal{C}(\beta,x)\mathcal{C}(\alpha\beta,x)^{-1}=e
for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma
and so
\psi
takes its values in
\ker(p)
. As above, it follows that
\psi
is constant with the value
\psi(1,1)=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(1,x)=e
and so
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
satisfies the cocycle identity at
x
. Since
x\in X
was arbitrary, this verifies condition (a).
∎

Theorem 3.38
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X
be a minimal flow with
\Gamma\in\mathsf{CLAC}
simply connected,
G,G^{\prime}
be abelian topological groups and
p\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{\prime},G)
be a covering morphism. Then for every
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
there is a unique
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G^{\prime})
with
p\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}
.

Remark 3.39
.

We wish to add the following observations.

•

The theorem applies to the following particular situations:

(a)

G=G^{\prime}=\mathbb{T}^{1}
and
p=\kappa_{d}
(
d\in\mathbb{N}
); this shows that under the assumptions of the theorem, the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is divisible,

(b)

G=\mathbb{T}^{1}
,
G^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}
and
p\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
is the usual covering morphism.

•

If the groups
G,G^{\prime}
are compact then
p\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{\prime},G)
is a covering morphism if and only if it is an epimorphism with a finite kernel. In Theorem
4.14
we shall see that for compact groups
G,G^{\prime}
, the extensions
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
lift uniquely across
p
also when
p\colon G^{\prime}\to G
is an epimorphism with a totally disconnected kernel.

•

It is a frequent assumption in texts dealing with covering maps that the domain of a lifted map is an element of
\mathsf{CLAC}
. A special form of our lifted map
\mathcal{C}
will allow us to abandon this assumption. That is, we will not have to assume that
X\in\mathsf{CLAC}
. As a matter of fact, we shall need to apply Theorem
3.38
several times to a situation when
X
is a general, not necessarily locally connected continuum.

Proof of Theorem
3.38
.

By virtue of Lemma
3.36
, we need to lift
\mathcal{C}
across
p
to a continuous map
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\colon\Gamma\times X\to G^{\prime}
with
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(1,x)=e
for every
x\in X
. We proceed in four steps.

1st step.
We define the required lift
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{C}
.

Fix
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
x\in X
and choose a path
f
in
\Gamma
from
1
to
\gamma
. Then
\widetilde{f}\colon[0,1]\ni t\mapsto\mathcal{C}(f(t),x)\in G
is a path in
G
from
e
to
\mathcal{C}(\gamma,x)
. Since
p
is a covering morphism,
\widetilde{f}
lifts across
p
to a unique path
\widehat{f}
in
G^{\prime}
starting at
e
. Since
\Gamma
is simply connected, the endpoint of
\widehat{f}
does not depend on
f
, only on
\gamma
and
x
; we shall denote this endpoint by
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\gamma,x)
. Clearly, the map
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\colon\Gamma\times X\to G^{\prime}
thus defined projects to
\mathcal{C}
via
p
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(1,x)=e
for every
x\in X
. We thus need to show that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is continuous.

2nd step.
We show that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is continuous in the second variable.

Fix
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
x\in X
; we show that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is continuous at
(\gamma,x)
in the second variable. So let
V^{\prime}
be a neighbourhood of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\gamma,x)
in
G^{\prime}
. Fix a path
f
in
\Gamma
from
1
to
\gamma
. From our construction of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
it follows that
\widehat{f}(t)=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(f(t),x)
for every
t\in[0,1]
. Thus, by continuity of
\widehat{f}
, there exist

•

a partition
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{n}=1
of
[0,1]
,

•

open subsets
V_{1},\dots,V_{n}
of
G
, each of them evenly covered by
p

•

open subsets
V_{1}^{\prime},\dots,V_{n}^{\prime}
of
G^{\prime}
,
V_{i}^{\prime}
being a
p
-slice over
V_{i}
for every
i=1,\dots,n
, and
V_{n}^{\prime}\subseteq V^{\prime}
,

such that

(
*
)

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(f([t_{i-1},t_{i}])\times x)\subseteq V_{i}^{\prime}
for
i=1,\dots,n
.

By virtue of (
*
),

\mathcal{C}(f([t_{i-1},t_{i}])\times x)=p\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(f([t_{i-1},t_{i}])\times x)\subseteq p(V_{i}^{\prime})=V_{i}

for
i=1,\dots,n
and
\mathcal{C}(f(t_{i}),x)=p\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(f(t_{i}),x)\in p(V_{i}^{\prime}\cap V_{i+1}^{\prime})
for
i=1,\dots,n-1
. Consequently, by continuity of
\mathcal{C}
, there is a neighbourhood
U
of
x
in
X
such that

(a)

\mathcal{C}(f([t_{i-1},t_{i}])\times U)\subseteq V_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
, and

(b)

\mathcal{C}(f(t_{i})\times U)\subseteq p(V_{i}^{\prime}\cap V_{i+1}^{\prime})
for
i=1,\dots,n-1
.

Now let
y\in U
; we show that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\gamma,y)\in V^{\prime}
. Consider the path
\widetilde{g}\colon[0,1]\ni t\mapsto\mathcal{C}(f(t),y)\in G
in
G
from
e
to
\mathcal{C}(\gamma,y)
. For
i=1,\dots,n
, let
\widetilde{g}_{i}
be the restriction of
\widetilde{g}
onto
[t_{i-1},t_{i}]
,
p_{i}\colon V_{i}^{\prime}\to V_{i}
be the homeomorphism obtained by restricting
p
and
\widehat{g}_{i}=p_{i}^{-1}\widetilde{g}_{i}
. By virtue of (a), all the maps
\widehat{g}_{i}
are well defined and continuous. By virtue of (b),

\widehat{g}_{i}(t_{i})=p_{i}^{-1}\widetilde{g}_{i}(t_{i})=p_{i}^{-1}\mathcal{C}(f(t_{i}),y)=p_{i+1}^{-1}\mathcal{C}(f(t_{i}),y)=p_{i+1}^{-1}\widetilde{g}_{i+1}(t_{i})=\widehat{g}_{i+1}(t_{i})

for every
i=1,\dots,n-1
. Consequently, the maps
\widehat{g}_{i}
are restrictions of a single path
\widehat{g}\colon[0,1]\to G^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
, which projects to
\widetilde{g}
via
p
. By our definition of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, it follows that

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\gamma,y)=\widehat{g}(1)=\widehat{g}_{n}(1)=p_{n}^{-1}\widetilde{g}_{n}(1)\in V_{n}^{\prime}\subseteq V^{\prime},

as was to be shown. This shows that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is continuous in the second variable.

3rd step.
We show that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is (jointly) continuous.

Let
\gamma
,
x
and
V^{\prime}
be as in the second step of the proof and set
V=p(V^{\prime})
; we shall find a neighbourhood
W
of
\gamma
in
\Gamma
and a neighbourhood
U
of
x
in
X
with
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(W\times U)\subseteq V^{\prime}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
V
is evenly covered by
p
and
V^{\prime}
is a
p
-slice over
V
. Since
p
is an open map by the assumptions,
V
is a neighbourhood of
\mathcal{C}(\gamma,x)
in
G
. Fix an arc-wise connected neighbourhood
W
of
\gamma
in
\Gamma
and a neighbourhood
U
of
x
in
X
with
\mathcal{C}(W\times U)\subseteq V
. Since
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is continuous in the second variable by the second step of the proof, we may assume that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\gamma\times U)\subseteq V^{\prime}
.

Fix
\delta\in W
and
y\in U
. Choose a path
h
in
\Gamma
with

•

h(0)=1
,
h(1/2)=\gamma
,
h(1)=\delta
and

•

h(t)\in W
for every
t\in[1/2,1]
.

Write
\widetilde{h}(t)=\mathcal{C}(h(t),y)
for
t\in[0,1]
and let
\widehat{h}
be the lift of
\widetilde{h}
across
p
starting at
e
. Recall from the second step of the proof that
\widehat{h}(t)=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(h(t),y)
for every
t\in[0,1]
. Denote by
p_{0}
the homeomorphism
V^{\prime}\to V
, obtained by restricting
p
. Since
\widehat{h}(1/2)=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(h(1/2),y)=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\gamma,y)\in V^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{h}(t)\in V
for every
t\in[1/2,1]
, it follows that
\widehat{h}|_{[1/2,1]}
and
p_{0}^{-1}\widetilde{h}|_{[1/2,1]}
are both lifts of
\widetilde{h}|_{[1/2,1]}
across
p
, starting at the same point. Thus, by uniqueness of lifts of paths,
\widehat{h}(t)=p_{0}^{-1}\widetilde{h}(t)
for every
t\in[1/2,1]
. In particular,

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\delta,y)=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(h(1),y)=\widehat{h}(1)=p_{0}^{-1}\widetilde{h}(1)\in p_{0}^{-1}(V)=V^{\prime}.

Thus,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(W\times U)\subseteq V^{\prime}
, as was to be shown.

4th step.
We finish the proof by verifying the uniqueness part of the theorem.

So assume that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G^{\prime})
satisfy
p\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}=p\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
. Then
p(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\left(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}\right)^{-1})=e
and hence
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\left(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}\right)^{-1}
takes its values in the kernel
\ker(p)
of
p
. Since the domain
\Gamma\times X
of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime})^{-1}
is connected and
\ker(p)
is totally disconnected, it follows that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime})^{-1}
is constant with the value
e
. This shows that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

By the first part of Remark
3.39
, if the acting group
\Gamma
of a minimal flow
\mathcal{F}
is simply connected then the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is divisible. In the following example we show that this is no longer the case if the assumption of simple connectedness is moved from
\Gamma
to
X
. (This is contrary to the well known situation of
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}
and
X
compact simply connected, in which case the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}\cong C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})\cong C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R})
is divisible.)

Example 3.40
.

Consider the usual transitive action
\mathcal{F}\colon\text{U}(2)\curvearrowright\mathbb{S}^{3}
(see Subsection
2.5.3
). The acting group
\text{U}(2)
of
\mathcal{F}
is not simply connected, having an infinite cyclic fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\text{U}(2))
, but the phase space
\mathbb{S}^{3}
of
\mathcal{F}
is simply connected; we show that the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is not divisible. To this end, let
h=\det
be the determinant function on
\text{U}(2)
. Clearly,
h
is a topological morphism
\text{U}(2)\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
. Moreover, since

h\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}e^{i2\pi t}&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)=\det\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}e^{i2\pi t}&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)=e^{i2\pi t}

for every
t\in[0,1]
, the morphism
h^{\sharp}\colon\pi_{1}(\text{U}(2))\to\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
induced by
h
is an epimorphism (in fact,
h^{\sharp}
is an isomorphism). Now let
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{Q}_{h}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
be the quasi-coboundary induced by
h
; we claim that
\mathcal{C}
is a non-divisible element of
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. If
z
is a base point for
\mathbb{S}^{3}
then, by virtue of (
2.30
) from Lemma
2.17
,

\mathcal{C}^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\text{U}(2)\times\mathbb{S}^{3})=\mathcal{C}_{z}^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\text{U}(2))=h^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\text{U}(2))=\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}).

It follows that
\mathcal{C}
does not lift across any of the endomorphisms
\kappa_{d}
of
\mathbb{T}^{1}
with
d\geq 2
. (Indeed, recall that
\kappa_{d}^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1})=d(\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}))\subsetneq\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
for
d\geq 2
.) Thus,
\mathcal{C}
is a non-divisible element of
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
, as was to be shown.

Corollary 3.41
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow with
\Gamma\in\mathsf{LieGp}
simply connected and with
X
compact second countable. Let
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
be a connected second countable group. Then
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is the additive topological group of a real separable Fréchet space. If the group
G
is finite-dimensional then
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is the additive topological group of a real separable Banach space.

Remark 3.42
.

In the proof of the corollary we shall observe that
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
is a separable Banach space and find out that it is isomorphic to
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
as a topological group. This will yield the structure of a separable Banach space in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. Now assume that the space
X
is infinite, so that
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R})
is an infinite-dimensional real linear space. Since
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
contains
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})\cong C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R})
as a linear subspace, it follows from the Mackey theorem that
\mathop{\rm dim_{\mathbb{R}LS}}(\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R}))\geq\mathfrak{c}
. On the other hand, by separability of
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
,
\mathop{\rm dim_{\mathbb{R}LS}}(\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R}))\leq\mathop{\rm card}(\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R}))\leq\mathfrak{c}
. Consequently,

\mathop{\rm dim_{\mathbb{R}LS}}(\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})=\mathop{\rm dim_{\mathbb{R}LS}}(\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R}))=\mathfrak{c}.

Proof of Corollary
3.41
.

Throughout the whole proof, all the groups
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
are assumed to carry the topology of u.c.s. convergence. Let
p\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
be the usual covering morphism and
\widehat{p}\colon\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})\ni\mathcal{D}\mapsto p\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
be the induced topological morphism. By virtue of Theorem
3.38
,
\widehat{p}
is an epimorphism. Since
\Gamma\times X
is connected and
\ker(p)\cong\mathbb{Z}
is totally disconnected,
\widehat{p}
is also a monomorphism.

We show that
\widehat{p}
is in fact a topological isomorphism. To this end, let
(\mathcal{D}_{i})_{i\in J}
be a net in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
with
\widehat{p}(\mathcal{D}_{i})=p\mathcal{D}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ucs}}{{\longrightarrow}}1
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
; we show that
\mathcal{D}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ucs}}{{\longrightarrow}}0
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
. So let
F\subseteq\Gamma\times X
be a compact set and
0<\varepsilon<1/2
. Fix compact sets
1\in K\subseteq\Gamma
and
C\subseteq X
with
K
connected and with
F\subseteq K\times C
. Since
p\mathcal{D}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ucs}}{{\longrightarrow}}1
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
, there is
i_{0}\in J
such that
p\mathcal{D}_{i}(\gamma,x)\in p(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
for all
i\geq i_{0}
,
\gamma\in K
and
x\in C
. Since
1\in K
is connected and
\varepsilon<1/2
, it follows that
\mathcal{D}_{i}(K\times x)\subseteq(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
for all
i\geq i_{0}
and
x\in C
. Consequently,
\mathcal{D}_{i}(F)\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{i}(K\times C)\subseteq(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
for every
i\geq i_{0}
. Hence,
\mathcal{D}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ucs}}{{\longrightarrow}}0
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
, as was to be shown.

By our assumptions on
\Gamma
and
X
,
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
is the additive topological group of a real separable Banach space, see Lemma
2.18
and Remark
2.19
in Subsection
2.5.11
. By the first part of the proof, it follows that so is the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. Moreover, the groups
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and
\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
are topologically isomorphic due to Theorem
3.1
for every
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
. Now, if
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
is a connected second countable group then its dual group
G^{*}
is torsion-free countable and so
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\cong\mathop{\rm Hom}(G^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
is the additive topological group of a real separable Fréchet space by the first part of Lemma
2.1
. If, in addition, the group
G
is finite-dimensional then the group
G^{*}
is of a finite rank and so
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is the additive topological group of a real separable Banach space by the second part of Lemma
2.1
.
∎

Theorem 3.43
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow with
\Gamma\in\mathsf{CLAC}
simply connected and with
X
compact. Then there is a short exact sequence of abelian groups

0\longrightarrow\pi^{1}(X)\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}\longrightarrow 0.

(3.17)

Further, there is a divisible torsion-free subgroup
\mathfrak{D}\cong\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}/\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})
of the groupoid
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinH}_{\mathcal{F}}}
with
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}=\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})\oplus\mathfrak{D}
. Finally, if
1\leq\mathop{\rm rank}(\pi^{1}(X))<\mathfrak{c}
then

\mathop{\rm rank}(\mathfrak{D})=\mathop{\rm dim_{\mathbb{Q}LS}}(\mathfrak{D})\geq\mathfrak{c}.

Remark 3.44
.

Notice the following facts.

•

The assumption
\mathop{\rm rank}(\pi^{1}(X))<\mathfrak{c}
is satisfied if the group
\pi^{1}(X)
is countable. This is the case if the space
X
is second countable.

•

It follows from the last two statements of the theorem that the groupoid
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinH}_{\mathcal{F}}}
contains an isomorphic copy of the group
\mathbb{Q}^{(\mathfrak{c})}\cong\mathbb{R}
.

Proof of Theorem
3.43
.

We verify each of the three statements of the theorem in a separate step of the proof.

1st step.
We show that there is an exact sequence of abelian groups (
3.17
).

Let
p\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
be the usual covering morphism and
\widehat{p}\colon\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})\ni\mathcal{D}\mapsto p\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
be the induced morphism. By an argument similar to that from Corollary
3.41
,
\widehat{p}
is an isomorphism of groups. Further, fix a base point
z
for
X
and consider the map
\varphi\colon C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})\ni\xi\mapsto\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. Clearly,
\varphi
is an isomorphism of groups. Finally, since the space
X
is connected and the kernel
\ker(p)=\mathbb{Z}
of
p
is totally disconnected, it follows that
q\colon C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R})\ni\zeta\mapsto p\zeta\in C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
is a monomorphism of groups. Moreover, by definition of
\pi^{1}(X)
, there is a direct sum
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})=\mathop{\rm im}(q)\oplus\pi^{1}(X)
. An elementary argument also shows that
\varphi(\mathop{\rm im}(q))=\widehat{p}(\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R}))
.

Now, the ideas formulated in the preceding paragraph yield a direct sum

\begin{split}\widehat{p}^{-1}(\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}})&=\widehat{p}^{-1}\varphi\left(C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})\right)=\widehat{p}^{-1}\varphi\left(\mathop{\rm im}(q)\oplus\pi^{1}(X)\right)\\
&=\widehat{p}^{-1}\varphi(\mathop{\rm im}(q))\oplus\widehat{p}^{-1}\varphi(\pi^{1}(X))=\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})\oplus\widehat{p}^{-1}\varphi(\pi^{1}(X)).\end{split}

(3.18)

Further, since
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
is a linear subspace of
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
, it follows that
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
is a direct summand in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
and each of its complementary summands in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
is isomorphic to
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})/\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})=\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
. Since
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})\cap\widehat{p}^{-1}\varphi(\pi^{1}(X))=0
by virtue of (
3.18
), there is a complementary summand
H
of
\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
with
\widehat{p}^{-1}\varphi(\pi^{1}(X))\subseteq H
. Consequently, there are isomorphisms of groups

\begin{split}\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}&=\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}/\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}\cong\widehat{p}^{-1}(\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})/\widehat{p}^{-1}(\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}})=\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})/\widehat{p}^{-1}(\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}})\\
&=\left(\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})\oplus H\right)/\left(\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})\oplus\widehat{p}^{-1}\varphi(\pi^{1}(X))\right)\cong H/\widehat{p}^{-1}\varphi(\pi^{1}(X)).\end{split}

Since
H\cong\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
and
\widehat{p}^{-1}\varphi(\pi^{1}(X))\cong\pi^{1}(X)
, this verifies the existence of the desired exact sequence (
3.17
).

2nd step.
We verify the second statement of the theorem.

By the first step of the proof, there is an isomorphism
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}\cong\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
. Since the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
is divisible, it follows that so are the groups
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
,
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and
\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})
. Consequently,
\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})
is a direct summand in
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and each of its complementary summands is divisible, torsion-free and isomorphic to
\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}/\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})
. Fix such a complementary summand
\mathfrak{D}
; we need to show that
\mathfrak{D}\subseteq\mathop{\rm{\bf MinH}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. This follows immediately from the identity
\mathop{\rm{\bf MinH}_{\mathcal{F}}}\setminus 1=\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}}\setminus\mathop{\rm tor}(\mathop{\rm{\bf H}_{\mathcal{F}}})
, see Corollary
3.9
.

3rd step.
We finish the proof of the theorem by verifying its last statement.

So assume that
1\leq\mathop{\rm rank}(\pi^{1}(X))<\mathfrak{c}
and fix a complementary summand
\mathfrak{D}
of
