contains an extension with a prescribed non-trivial totally disconnected section
K\mathrel{\lhd}S_{\bf p}
if and only if
K^{\perp}\cong\mathbb{Z}
. Finally, we show that for a flow
\mathcal{F}
with a free cycle, the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(S_{\bf p})
contains also minimal extensions.

Theorem 5.25
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow with
\Gamma\in\mathsf{LieGp}
connected and with
X
a compact connected manifold, and let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

for every
K\mathrel{\lhd}\mathbb{T}^{k}
there is
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{k})
with
F(\mathcal{C})=K
,

(2)

for every
K\mathrel{\lhd}\mathbb{T}^{k}
finite there is
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{k})
with
F(\mathcal{C})=K
,

(3)

\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(X))-\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F}))\geq k
.

Proof.

Write
n=\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F}))
,
n+m=\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(X))
and let
d_{1},\dots,d_{n}=d
be the elementary divisors of
H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})
in
H_{1}^{w}(X)
written, as usual, in the increasing order. The implication (1)
\Rightarrow
(2) is clear. To verify that (2) implies (3), fix an integer
l\geq 2
co-prime with
d
and set
K=(\mathbb{Z}_{l})^{k}\mathrel{\lhd}\mathbb{T}^{k}
. By virtue of (2), there is
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{k})
with
F(\mathcal{C})=K
. Set
A=\mathbb{Z}_{d_{1}}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{d_{n}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{m}
. By Corollary
5.24
,
K
is a quotient group of
A
. Denote the underlying quotient morphism
A\to K
by
q
. Then, by our choice of
l
,
K=dK=dq(A)=q(dA)=q(d\mathbb{Z}^{m})
. It follows that
m\geq k
, which verifies (3).

We show that (3) implies (2). So assume that
m\geq k
and fix a finite subgroup
K
of
\mathbb{T}^{k}
. The Pontryagin dual to the inclusion morphism
K\to\mathbb{T}^{k}
is (equivalent to) a quotient morphism
\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to K
. Since
m\geq k
, it follows that
K
is a quotient group of
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
and hence also of
\mathbb{Z}_{d_{1}}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{d_{n}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{m}
. Thus, by Corollary
5.24
, there is an extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{k})
with
F(\mathcal{C})=K
. This verifies (2).

We finish the proof by showing that (3) implies (1). So assume that (3) holds and fix a closed subgroup
K
of
\mathbb{T}^{k}
. We distinguish two cases.

Case 1.
The group
K\mathrel{\lhd}\mathbb{T}^{k}
is connected.

In this case
K
is a torus with dimension at most
k
and it is therefore a quotient group of
\mathbb{T}^{k}
. Let
r\colon\mathbb{T}^{k}\to K
be a quotient morphism and assume that
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{k})
is minimal (that is, it satisfies
F(\mathcal{C})=\mathbb{T}^{k}
). Then
r\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(K)\subseteq\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{k})
and
F(r\mathcal{C})=rF(\mathcal{C})=r(\mathbb{T}^{k})=K
. Thus, in order to verify condition (1) in Case 1, it suffices to construct a minimal extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{k})
.

Condition (3) yields an epimorphism
h\colon H_{1}^{w}(X)\to H_{1}^{w}(\mathbb{T}^{k})
with
h(H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F}))=0
. By virtue of Lemma
5.21
,
h=\xi^{\sharp}
for an appropriate continuous base point preserving map
\xi\colon X\to\mathbb{T}^{k}
. Let
p\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{T}^{k}
be the usual covering morphism with kernel
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
. Since
\xi^{\sharp}\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\Gamma)=0
by our choice of
\xi
, statement (
2.30
) from Lemma
2.17
yields
\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\Gamma\times X)=0
. Consequently,
\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)
lifts across
p
to an extension
\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
. For every
l\in\mathbb{N}
set
\mathcal{C}_{l}=p((1/l)\mathcal{D})\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{k})
. Then
\kappa_{l}\mathcal{C}_{l}=\kappa_{l}p((1/l)\mathcal{D})=p\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{C}
, where
\kappa_{l}
stands for the
l
-endomorphism of
\mathbb{T}^{k}
.

We claim that the following statements hold:

(a)

F(\mathcal{C}_{l})=(\mathbb{Z}_{l})^{k}
for every
l\in\mathbb{N}
; indeed, by virtue of Theorem
5.14
(1),

F(\mathcal{C}_{l})=\mathcal{E}_{\ker(\kappa_{l})}(\xi^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(X))=\mathcal{E}_{\ker(\kappa_{l})}(\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{k}))=\ker(\kappa_{l})=(\mathbb{Z}_{l})^{k},

(b)

\mathcal{C}_{l}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ucs}}{{\longrightarrow}}1
as
l\to\infty
; this follows from our definition of the extensions
\mathcal{C}_{l}
(
l\in\mathbb{N}
) and from an obvious convergence
(1/l)\mathcal{D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ucs}}{{\longrightarrow}}0
as
l\to\infty
.

Further, since the groups
F(\mathcal{C}_{l})=(\mathbb{Z}_{l})^{k}
(
l\in\mathbb{N}
) become mutually group-disjoint if we restrict to the prime numbers
l
, we may apply Theorem
3.23
to obtain an increasing sequence of prime numbers
(p_{l})_{l\in\mathbb{N}}
and an extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{k})
with

•

\mathcal{C}=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{p_{l}}
u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{k})
, and

•

F(\mathcal{C})\supseteq\overline{\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}}F(\mathcal{C}_{p_{l}})}
.

Since
\mathbb{T}^{k}\supseteq F(\mathcal{C})\supseteq\overline{\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}}F(\mathcal{C}_{p_{l}})}=\overline{\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p_{l}})^{k}}=\mathbb{T}^{k}
, it follows that
F(\mathcal{C})=\mathbb{T}^{k}
and so the extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{k})
is minimal. This verifies condition (1) in Case 1.

Case 2.
The group
K\mathrel{\lhd}\mathbb{T}^{k}
is arbitrary.

The group
K
can be expressed as a topological direct sum
K=K_{1}\oplus K_{2}
, where
K_{1}\mathrel{\lhd}\mathbb{T}^{k}
is connected and
K_{2}\mathrel{\lhd}\mathbb{T}^{k}
is finite. From Case 1 we know that
K_{1}=F(\mathcal{C}_{1})
for an appropriate
\mathcal{C}_{1}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{k})
. Moreover, since the equivalence of (2) and (3) has already been established in an earlier part of the proof, we may apply (2) to obtain
\mathcal{C}_{2}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{k})
with
F(\mathcal{C}_{2})=K_{2}
. Now, by virtue of Corollary
3.20
,
F(\mathcal{C}_{1}+\mathcal{C}_{2})=F(\mathcal{C}_{1})+F(\mathcal{C}_{2})=K_{1}\oplus K_{2}=K
. This verifies statement (1) also in Case 2.
∎

By applying Theorem
5.25
to minimal equicontinuous flows
\mathcal{F}
, we will be able to enlarge ranges of dense Lie group morphisms in Corollary
5.28
. Before doing so, we prove an auxiliary lemma.

Lemma 5.26
.

Let
G
be a compact connected Lie group. Then the map

\varphi\colon\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,\mathbb{T}^{1})\ni\xi\mapsto\xi^{\sharp}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\pi_{1}(G),\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}))

(5.8)

is an isomorphism of groups. In particular, given
\varrho\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\pi_{1}(G),\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}))
, there is
\xi\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,\mathbb{T}^{1})
with
\varrho=\xi^{\sharp}
.

Remark 5.27
.

We wish to add the following remarks.

•

Recall from Subsection
2.4.9
that there is an epimorphism

C_{e}(G,\mathbb{T}^{1})\ni f\mapsto f^{\sharp}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\pi_{1}(G),\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1})),

which induces an isomorphism between the group
\pi^{1}(G)
of the homotopy classes of continuous base point preserving maps
f\colon G\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
and the group of morphisms
\varrho\colon\pi_{1}(G)\to\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
. By combining this fact with the statement of Lemma
5.26
, we infer that every continuous base point preserving map
f\colon G\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
is homotopic to a unique topological morphism
\xi\colon G\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
.

•

We were not able to find the statement of this lemma explicitly stated in the literature. Therefore, we present its detailed proof based on results from the structure theory of compact (Lie) groups.

Proof of Lemma
5.26
.

A rough idea of the proof is to reduce the problem to the case when
G
is a torus, in which case the statement of the lemma clearly holds (see, for instance, our discussion in Subsections
2.4.9
and
2.4.10
). We divide the proof into three steps.

1st step.
We start by collecting some preliminary observations.

Let
G^{\prime}
be the commutator subgroup of
G
. By virtue of
[
38
, Theorem 6.18(i), p. 206]
,
G^{\prime}
is a compact connected semi-simple Lie group and its fundamental group
\pi_{1}(G^{\prime})
is thus finite by the Weyl’s theorem (see
[
5
, Corollary 4, p. 285]
). Further, by the second structure theorem for connected compact Lie groups (see
[
38
, Theorem 6.41(i), p. 221]
), there is a (finite-dimensional) torus
T
such that
G
is the topological semi-direct product
T\ltimes G^{\prime}
of
T
and
G^{\prime}
; we denote by
p\colon G\to T
the associated projection morphism. Under the usual identification
\pi_{1}(G)=\pi_{1}(T)\oplus\pi_{1}(G^{\prime})
, the induced morphism
p^{\sharp}
takes the form of the projection of
\pi_{1}(G)
onto
\pi_{1}(T)
and so it is an epimorphism with the kernel
\ker(p^{\sharp})=\pi_{1}(G^{\prime})
. Finally, given
\xi\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,\mathbb{T}^{1})
, we have
G^{\prime}\subseteq\ker(\xi)
by commutativity of
\mathbb{T}^{1}
and so
\xi=\xi^{\prime}p
for a unique
\xi^{\prime}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(T,\mathbb{T}^{1})
.

2nd step.
We show that
\varphi
is a monomorphism.

Fix
\xi\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,\mathbb{T}^{1})
and assume that
\varphi(\xi)=\xi^{\sharp}=0
. Then
0=\xi^{\sharp}=(\xi^{\prime}p)^{\sharp}=(\xi^{\prime})^{\sharp}p^{\sharp}
and hence
(\xi^{\prime})^{\sharp}=0
by surjectivity of
p^{\sharp}
. Since the domain of
\xi^{\prime}
is a torus, it follows that
\xi^{\prime}=1
and so
\xi=\xi^{\prime}p=1
. Thus,
\varphi
is indeed a monomorphism.

3rd step.
We finish the proof by showing that
\varphi
is an epimorphism.

So let
\varrho\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\pi_{1}(G),\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}))
; we shall find
\xi\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,\mathbb{T}^{1})
with
\varrho=\xi^{\sharp}
. Since the group
\pi_{1}(G^{\prime})
is finite and the group
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
is torsion-free,
\ker(\varrho)\supseteq\pi_{1}(G^{\prime})=\ker(p^{\sharp})
. Consequently, there is
\vartheta\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\pi_{1}(T),\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}))
with
\varrho=\vartheta p^{\sharp}
. Let
\zeta\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(T,\mathbb{T}^{1})
be such that
\zeta^{\sharp}=\vartheta
and set
\xi=\zeta p
. Then
\xi\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,\mathbb{T}^{1})
and
\varrho=\vartheta p^{\sharp}=\zeta^{\sharp}p^{\sharp}=(\zeta p)^{\sharp}=\xi^{\sharp}
. Thus,
\varrho\in\mathop{\rm im}(\varphi)
, as was to be shown.
∎

Corollary 5.28
.

Let
\Gamma,G
be connected Lie groups,
\Gamma
non-compact,
G
compact, and let
h\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\Gamma,G)
be a monomorphism with a dense image. Then for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
there is
r\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{T}^{k})
such that the morphism
h\oplus r\colon\Gamma\to G\oplus\mathbb{T}^{k}
has a dense image, that is,
\overline{\mathop{\rm im}(h\oplus r)}=G\oplus\mathbb{T}^{k}
.

Remark 5.29
.

We wish to add the following observations.

•

In connection with Remark
2.11
notice the following immediate consequence of the corollary: if for a given connected group
\Gamma\in\mathsf{LieGp}
there exist a compact group
G\in\mathsf{LieGp}
and a monomorphism
h\colon\Gamma\to G
with a dense image (in other words, if
\Gamma
possesses a faithful finite-dimensional unitary representation) then
\Gamma
possesses also a non-trivial one-dimensional unitary representation, that is, a non-trivial character
\Gamma\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
.

•

The statement of the corollary can be reformulated by saying that the minimal equicontinuous flow
\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright G
induced by
h
possesses a minimal pure quasi-coboundary
\mathcal{Q}_{r}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{k})
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof of Corollary
5.28
.

It is clearly sufficient to verify the statement of the corollary in case when
k=1
; the general case then follows by a repeated application of this particular one. Our proof will follow the line of that of Case 1 in the proof of Theorem
5.25
, but we shall present it with all the details.

Let
\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright G
be the minimal equicontinuous flow induced by
h
(see (
2.22
)). We choose the identity
z=e
as the base point of
G
; this leads to
\mathcal{F}_{z}=h
. Since
h\colon\Gamma\to G
is not an isomorphism, the flow
\mathcal{F}
possesses a free cycle by virtue of Proposition
2.12
; that is,
\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F}))<\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(G))
. Consequently, there is an epimorphism
\varrho\colon H_{1}^{w}(G)\to H_{1}^{w}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
with
H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})\subseteq\ker(\varrho)
. By virtue of Lemma
5.26
,
\varrho=\xi^{\sharp}
for some
\xi\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(G,\mathbb{T}^{1})
.

Now, under the usual identification
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1})=H_{1}^{w}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
, Lemma
2.17
yields

\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\Gamma\times G)=\xi^{\sharp}\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\Gamma)=\xi^{\sharp}\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}H_{1}^{w}(\Gamma)=\xi^{\sharp}H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})=\varrho H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})=0.

Thus,
\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)
lifts across the usual covering morphism
p\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
to an extension
\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
. For
l\in\mathbb{N}
set
\mathcal{C}_{l}=p((1/l)\mathcal{D})
; clearly,
\mathcal{C}_{l}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is the lift of
\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)
across the
l
-endomorphism
\kappa_{l}
of
\mathbb{T}^{1}
. Also, since
(1/l)\mathcal{D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ucs}}{{\longrightarrow}}0
as
l\to\infty
, it follows that
\mathcal{C}_{l}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ucs}}{{\longrightarrow}}1
as
l\to\infty
.

Further, by our choice of
\xi
and by Theorem
5.14
(1),

F(\mathcal{C}_{l})=\mathcal{E}_{\ker(\kappa_{l})}\left(\xi^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(G)\right)=\mathcal{E}_{\ker(\kappa_{l})}(\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}))=\ker(\kappa_{l})=\mathbb{Z}_{l}

for every
l\in\mathbb{N}
. Consequently, Theorem
3.23
yields an increasing sequence of prime numbers
(p_{l})_{l\in\mathbb{N}}
and an extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
, such that

•

\mathcal{C}=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{p_{l}}
and

•

F(\mathcal{C})\supseteq\overline{\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}}F(\mathcal{C}_{p_{l}})}
.

Moreover, since
\overline{\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}}F(\mathcal{C}_{p_{l}})}=\overline{\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{Z}_{p_{l}}}=\mathbb{T}^{1}
, it follows that the extension
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{F}
is minimal.

We claim that
\mathcal{C}
is a pure quasi-coboundary over
\mathcal{F}
. First, given
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
g\in G
, we have

p\mathcal{D}(\gamma,g)=\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)(\gamma,g)=\xi(h(\gamma)g)\xi(g)^{-1}=\xi h(\gamma),

since
\xi\colon G\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
is a morphism of groups. Consequently, since
G
is connected and
\ker(p)=\mathbb{Z}
is totally disconnected, it follows that
\mathcal{D}(\gamma,-)
is constant on
G
for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
. This means that
\mathcal{D}
is a pure quasi-coboundary over
\mathcal{F}
and hence so is
\mathcal{C}_{l}
for every
l\in\mathbb{N}
. Since the pure quasi-coboundaries form a closed subgroup of
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
, the extension
\mathcal{C}=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{p_{l}}
is also a pure quasi-coboundary over
\mathcal{F}
, which verifies the claim.

Let
r\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{T}^{1})
be the morphism with
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{Q}_{r}
; we show that
r
satisfies the conclusion of the corollary. Indeed, by minimality of
\mathcal{C}
,

G\oplus\mathbb{T}^{1}=\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}(e,1)=\overline{\{(h(\gamma),r(\gamma)):\gamma\in\Gamma\}}=\overline{\mathop{\rm im}(h\oplus r)},

as was to be shown.
∎

Theorem 5.30
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow with
\Gamma\in\mathsf{LieGp}
connected and with
X
a compact connected manifold, and let
d_{1},\dots,d_{n}
be the elementary divisors of
H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})
in
H_{1}^{w}(X)
. Fix a solenoid
S_{\bf{p}}
and a closed non-trivial proper subgroup
K
of
S_{\bf{p}}
. Then the following statements hold:

(A)

If the flow
\mathcal{F}
does not possess a free cycle then there is no extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(S_{{\bf p}})
with
F(\mathcal{C})=K
.

(B)

If the flow
\mathcal{F}
does possess a free cycle then the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

F(\mathcal{C})=K
for an appropriate extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(S_{\bf{p}})
,

(ii)

K^{\perp}
is an infinite cyclic group, that is,
K^{\perp}\cong\mathbb{Z}
,

(iii)

K=\mathop{\rm pr}_{m}^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}_{k})
for an appropriate pair of integers
m\geq 1
and
k\geq 2
.

(C)

If the flow
\mathcal{F}
possesses a free cycle then there is
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(S_{{\bf p}})
with
F(\mathcal{C})=S_{{\bf p}}
, that is,
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(S_{{\bf p}})
contains a minimal extension.

Remark 5.31
.

We wish to add the following remarks.

•

In connection with statements (A) and (C) observe that if
\mathcal{F}
does not possess a free cycle then there may or may not exist
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(S_{{\bf p}})
with
F(\mathcal{C})=S_{{\bf p}}
. Firstly, let
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathbb{R}\curvearrowright X
be a minimal continuous flow on a simply connected manifold
X
, say, on
X=\mathbb{S}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
. (The existence of a minimal continuous flow on
\mathbb{S}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
follows from
[
18
]
.) Then
H_{1}^{w}(X)=0
(hence
\mathcal{F}
does not possess a free cycle) and the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
contains a minimal extension for every connected group
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
with
\mathop{\rm card}(G^{*})\leq\mathfrak{c}
. (This follows from our Theorem
6.3
in Section
6.1
.) Secondly, let
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathbb{T}^{1}\curvearrowright X
be the natural action of
\mathbb{T}^{1}
on
X=\mathbb{T}^{1}
by rotations. Then
H_{1}^{w}(\mathcal{F})=H_{1}^{w}(X)\cong\mathbb{Z}
(hence
\mathcal{F}
does not possess a free cycle) and for every non-trivial group
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
, the group
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
contains no minimal extensions whatsoever. (Indeed, recall that, up to a homeomorphism, there is a unique non-degenerate space - namely
\mathbb{T}^{1}
, on which the group
\mathbb{T}^{1}
acts in a minimal way.)

•

Condition (ii) from statement (B) is clearly equivalent to the isomorphism
S_{{\bf p}}/K\cong\mathbb{T}^{1}
. This occurs if and only if
p_{K}
is equivalent to a non-trivial character of
S_{{\bf p}}
.

•

From statement (A) and from condition (ii) in statement (B) it follows that a closed non-trivial proper subgroup
K
of
S_{{\bf p}}
must be infinite in order to be the section
F(\mathcal{C})
of an extension
\mathcal{C}
from
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(S_{{\bf p}})
. Consequently, finite non-trivial sections for extensions in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(S_{{\bf p}})
are not allowed. This observation follows also from Theorem
5.14
(3), for every finite subgroup of a solenoid
S_{{\bf p}}
intersects the identity arc-component
(S_{{\bf p}})_{a}
of
S_{\bf p}
only at the identity. Alternatively, one could also use Corollary
5.16
and Remark
5.17
.

Proof of Theorem
5.30
.

We divide the proof into four steps.

1st step.
We show that the following conditions are equivalent for a non-trivial proper closed subgroup
K
of
S_{{\bf p}}
:

(a)

K^{\perp}
is an infinite cyclic group, that is,
K^{\perp}\cong\mathbb{Z}
,

(b)

K=\mathop{\rm pr}_{m}^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}_{k})
for an appropriate pair of integers
m,k\geq 1
,

(c)

K=\mathop{\rm pr}_{m}^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}_{k})
for an appropriate pair of integers
m\geq 1
and
k\geq 2
.

Given integers
m,k\geq 1
, the following statements are equivalent:

•

K=\mathop{\rm pr}_{m}^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}_{k})
,

•

K=\ker(\chi_{k,m})
,

•

K^{\perp}=\langle\chi_{k,m}\rangle
,

•

\lambda^{-1}(K^{\perp})=k\mathbb{Z}/p_{0}\dots p_{m-1}
,

where
\lambda
is the isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}/{\bf p}\to(S_{\bf p})^{*}
defined by (
2.19
). Since the infinite cyclic subgroups of
\mathbb{Z}/{\bf p}
are precisely the groups of the form
k\mathbb{Z}/p_{0}\dots p_{m-1}
with
m,k\in\mathbb{N}
, the equivalence of (a) and (b) follows. Implication (c)
\Rightarrow
(b) is clear and the converse follows from the identity
\mathop{\rm pr}_{m}^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}_{k})=\mathop{\rm pr}_{m+1}^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}_{kp_{m}})
, which holds for all
m,k\in\mathbb{N}
.

2nd step.
We verify statement (A).

Assume, contrary to (A), that
K=F(\mathcal{C})
for some extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(S_{\bf p})
. By Theorem
5.22
, there exist
k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\in K\cap(S_{\bf p})_{a}
with
K=\overline{\langle k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\rangle}
and such that
k_{i}^{d_{i}}=e
for
i=1,\dots,n
. It follows that the group
K
is finite and
K\subseteq(S_{\bf p})_{a}
. Consequently,
K\subseteq(S_{\bf p})_{a}\cap\mathop{\rm tor}(S_{{\bf p}})=e
, which contradicts the assumptions of the theorem.

3rd step.
We verify statement (B).

First, the equivalence of (ii) and (iii) has been established in the first part of the proof. Further, implication (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) follows immediately from Remark
5.17
. Finally, to show that (i) follows from (iii), let
K
have the form
K=\mathop{\rm pr}_{m}^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}_{k})
with
m\geq 1
and
k\geq 2
. Consider the morphism
h\in\mathcal{L}(S_{{\bf p}})
defined by
h(t)=(\exp(i2\pi t/p_{0}\dots p_{n-1}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Then
h(p_{0}\dots p_{m-1}/k)
is a topological generator of
K
, which lies on the identity arc-component
(S_{{\bf p}})_{a}
of
S_{{\bf p}}
. Since the flow
\mathcal{F}
possesses a free cycle by the assumptions, we may apply Theorem
5.22
to obtain
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(S_{{\bf p}})
with
F(\mathcal{C})=K
. This verifies statement (i) and finishes the proof of (B).

4th step.
We verify statement (C). The argument will be similar to the one used to verify Case 1 in the proof of Theorem
5.25
.

Since the flow
\mathcal{F}
possesses a free cycle by the assumptions, Lemma
5.21
yields a continuous base point preserving map
\xi\colon X\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
with
\xi^{\sharp}\mathcal{F}_{z}^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\Gamma)=0
and
\xi^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(X)=\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
. Let
p\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
be the standard covering morphism with the kernel
\mathbb{Z}
. By virtue of (
2.30
) from Lemma
2.17
,
\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)
lifts across
p
to an extension
\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{R})
. Consider the morphism
h\colon\mathbb{R}\to S_{{\bf p}}
from the third step of the proof and set
\mathcal{C}_{l}=h((1/l)\mathcal{D})\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(S_{{\bf p}})
for
l\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
(1/l)\mathcal{D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ucs}}{{\longrightarrow}}0
as
l\to\infty
, it follows that
\mathcal{C}_{l}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ucs}}{{\longrightarrow}}e
as
l\to\infty
. Consequently, there exists an increasing sequence of prime numbers
(r_{l})_{l\in\mathbb{N}}
such that the series
\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{q_{l}}
converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(S_{{\bf p}})
for every subsequence
(q_{l})_{l\in\mathbb{N}}
of
(r_{l})_{l\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Given
l\in\mathbb{N}
, we show that
F(\mathcal{C}_{l})=\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}_{l})
. First, from the identity
\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}h=p
it follows that

(\kappa_{l}\text{pr}_{1})\mathcal{C}_{l}=\kappa_{l}\text{pr}_{1}h((1/l)\mathcal{D})=\kappa_{l}p((1/l)\mathcal{D})=p\mathcal{D}=\mathop{\rm co}(\xi).

Moreover, since
K_{l}:=\ker(\kappa_{l}\mathop{\rm pr}_{1})=\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}_{l})
is a (closed) totally disconnected subgroup of
S_{{\bf p}}
, we may apply (
5.1
) from Theorem
5.14
to obtain

K_{l}\supseteq F(\mathcal{C}_{l})=\overline{\mathcal{E}_{K_{l}}}(\xi^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(X))=\overline{\mathcal{E}_{K_{l}}}(\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}))\supseteq\overline{\langle h(1/l)\rangle}=K_{l};

where we have used the following facts:

•

\mathcal{E}_{K_{l}}(\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}))\ni h(1/l)
; this follows from the fact that
h(t/l)
(
0\leq t\leq 1
) is a path in
S_{\bf p}
from
e
to
h(1/l)
, which projects to the element
p(t)
(
0\leq t\leq 1
) of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
via
\kappa_{l}\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}
,

•

h(1/l)
is a topological generator of
K_{l}
; this is clear (see also the third step of the proof).

Thus it follows that
F(\mathcal{C}_{l})=K_{l}=\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}_{l})
, as was to be shown. As an immediate corollary of this fact we get
F(\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}\mathcal{C}_{l})=\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}F(\mathcal{C}_{l})=\mathbb{Z}_{l}
.

From our discussion so far the following facts emerge:

•

\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}\mathcal{C}_{r_{l}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ucs}}{{\longrightarrow}}1
as
l\to\infty
,

•

F(\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}\mathcal{C}_{r_{l}})=\mathbb{Z}_{r_{l}}
(
l\in\mathbb{N}
) are mutually group-disjoint.

Consequently, by Theorem
3.23
, there is a subsequence
(q_{l})_{l\in\mathbb{N}}
of
(r_{l})_{l\in\mathbb{N}}
such that the series
\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}\mathcal{C}_{q_{l}}
converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and

\mathbb{T}^{1}\supseteq F\left(\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\text{pr}_{1}\mathcal{C}_{q_{l}}\right)\supseteq\overline{\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}F(\text{pr}_{1}\mathcal{C}_{q_{l}})}=\overline{\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{Z}_{q_{l}}}=\mathbb{T}^{1}.

(5.9)

Now set
\mathcal{C}=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{q_{l}}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(S_{{\bf p}})
. (Recall that the series converges u.c.s. in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(S_{\bf p})
by our choice of the sequence
(r_{l})_{l\in\mathbb{N}}
and so the extension
\mathcal{C}
is well defined.) Moreover, by virtue of (
5.9
),

\text{pr}_{1}F(\mathcal{C})=F(\text{pr}_{1}\mathcal{C})=F\left(\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\text{pr}_{1}\mathcal{C}_{q_{l}}\right)=\mathbb{T}^{1},

which shows that
F(\mathcal{C})=S_{{\bf p}}
. This verifies statement (C).
∎

5.5
Relation to the first cohomotopy group

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow. In this section we continue our study of the problem of determining the section
F(\mathcal{C})
of an extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
in terms of morphisms induced by transfer functions of certain quotients of
\mathcal{C}
on one-dimensional algebraic-topological invariants. Now we concentrate on the situation when
X
is an arbitrary compact connected space. When dealing with general, not necessarily locally connected continua, the first cohomotopy group
\pi^{1}
is a more appropriate tool than the first homology group
H_{1}
. In Theorems
5.32
and
5.40
we explain how the functors
F
and
\pi^{1}
are related, and show that
\pi^{1}
can be used to obtain the values of
F
in some important situations.

Before formulating and proving our results, let us mention that in the whole present section we shall use notation introduced at the beginning of Section
5.1
.

Theorem 5.32
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow,
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
be connected and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. Fix
z\in X
. Assume that
K\mathrel{\lhd}G
is finite with
p_{K}\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G/K)
(that is, with
F(\mathcal{C})\subseteq K
), and denote by
\xi
the transfer function of
p_{K}\mathcal{C}
with
\xi(z)=e
. If the space
X
is compact connected and the group
\Gamma
has no non-trivial finite abelian quotient groups then the following statements hold under the usual identifications
\pi^{1}(G/K)\cong(G/K)^{*}\cong K^{\perp}
:

(1)

the group
F(\mathcal{C})
is the smallest of all the subgroups
E
of
K
, such that the morphism
\xi^{\flat}\colon(G/K)^{*}\to\pi^{1}(X)
extends through
(q_{E})^{*}\colon(G/K)^{*}\to(G/E)^{*}
to a morphism
\varrho\colon(G/E)^{*}\to\pi^{1}(X)
,

(2)

if
d
stands for the largest torsion coefficient of
K
and
\eta=|K|
denotes the cardinality of
K
then

F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}=\frac{1}{d}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}\left(d\pi^{1}(X)\right)=\frac{1}{\eta}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}\left(\eta\pi^{1}(X)\right),

(5.10)

see also
(
5.11
)
below,

(3)

the following statements are equivalent:

(
\iota
)

F(\mathcal{C})=K
,

(
\iota\iota
)

dG^{*}\cap\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X))=dK^{\perp}
,

(
\iota\iota\iota
)

\xi^{\flat}\left(dG^{*}\setminus dK^{\perp}\right)\cap d\pi^{1}(X)=\emptyset
.

Remark 5.33
.

We wish to mention the following facts.

•

The connectedness assumption of
G
is not at all restrictive in the situation of the theorem, for under our assumptions on
\Gamma
and
X
, every extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
with
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
takes its values in the identity component
G_{0}
of
G
. Indeed, since the group
G/G_{0}
is totally disconnected, its Pontryagin dual
(G/G_{0})^{*}
is a torsion group. Further, by Theorem
4.10
,
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is a torsion-free group. Hence, by Theorem
3.1
,

\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G/G_{0})\cong\mathop{\rm Hom}((G/G_{0})^{*},\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})=0.

Thus, if
p_{0}\colon G\to G/G_{0}
denotes the canonical quotient morphism then
p_{0}\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G/G_{0})
is trivial and hence
\mathcal{C}
takes its values in
G_{0}
.

•

We would like to clarify formula (
5.10
) from statement (2) of the theorem. By definition,
\xi^{\flat}
is a morphism
\pi^{1}(G/K)\to\pi^{1}(X)
. Since the group
G/K\in\mathsf{CAGp}
is connected, there are isomorphisms
\pi^{1}(G/K)\cong(G/K)^{*}\cong K^{\perp}
, see Subsection
2.4.10
. Thus,
(\xi^{\flat})^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X))
can be interpreted as a subgroup of
K^{\perp}\subseteq G^{*}
and the expression
(1/d)(\xi^{\flat})^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X))
then stands for the group of all
\chi\in G^{*}
with
\chi^{d}\in(\xi^{\flat})^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X))
. If one prefers not to use the identification
(G/K)^{*}\cong K^{\perp}
and to regard
\xi^{\flat}
as a morphism
(G/K)^{*}\to\pi^{1}(X)
then the formula (
5.10
) takes the form

F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}=\frac{1}{d}\,p_{K}^{*}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}\left(d\pi^{1}(X)\right)=\frac{1}{\eta}\,p_{K}^{*}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}\left(\eta\pi^{1}(X)\right).

(5.11)

More generally, if the morphism
p_{K}
is replaced by a general quotient morphism
q\colon G\to H
with the kernel
K
(and the map
\xi
thus takes its values in
H
) then

F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}=\frac{1}{d}\,q^{*}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}\left(d\pi^{1}(X)\right)=\frac{1}{\eta}\,q^{*}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}\left(\eta\pi^{1}(X)\right),

(5.12)

where
\xi^{\flat}
is interpreted as a morphism
H^{*}\to\pi^{1}(X)
. This follows from (
5.11
) and from the equivalence of
q
and
p_{K}
.

•

If the group
\pi^{1}(X)
is
d
-divisible (that is, if
d\pi^{1}(X)=\pi^{1}(X)
) then formula (
5.10
) yields
F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}=(1/d)K^{\perp}=G^{*}
, where the last equality holds by definition of
d
. Thus, in this situation,
F(\mathcal{C})=e
and hence
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
.

•

Recall that under the assumptions of Theorem
5.14
,
F(\mathcal{C})\subseteq G_{a}
as soon as
F(\mathcal{C})
is finite. Under the assumptions of Theorem
5.32
this is no longer the case, not even when the acting group
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{F}
is a (simply) connected Lie group. For an example of such a situation we refer to Section
5.6
, where finite subgroups of solenoids
S_{\bf p}
appear as sections
F(\mathcal{C})
of appropriate extensions
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(S_{\bf p})
. (Recall that a finite subgroup of a solenoid
S_{\bf p}
intersects the identity arc-component
(S_{\bf p})_{a}
of
S_{\bf p}
only at the identity
e
.)

Proof of Theorem
5.32
.

We begin the proof by verifying statement (1). By virtue of statement (ii) from Proposition
5.3
, it suffices to show that for every subgroup
E
of
K
, the following conditions are equivalent:

(
\alpha
)

there is
\sigma\in\mathop{\rm Hom}((G/E)^{*},C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1}))
with
\sigma(q_{E})^{*}=\xi^{*}
,

(
\beta
)

there is
\varrho\in\mathop{\rm Hom}((G/E)^{*},\pi^{1}(X))
with
\varrho(q_{E})^{*}=\xi^{\flat}
.

First recall that there is an isomorphism
\varphi\colon C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})\to C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R})\oplus\pi^{1}(X)
with
\mathop{\rm pr}_{2}\varphi=\pi
, where
\pi
stands for the canonical quotient morphism
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})\to\pi^{1}(X)
. Set
\nu=\mathop{\rm pr}_{1}\varphi\xi^{*}
. Then
\nu\in\mathop{\rm Hom}((G/K)^{*},C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R}))
and, since
\pi\xi^{*}=\xi^{\flat}
,
\varphi\xi^{*}=\nu\oplus\xi^{\flat}
. Consequently, we may use identifications
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})\cong C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R})\oplus\pi^{1}(X)
and
\xi^{*}\cong\nu\oplus\xi^{\flat}
.

Now let
E
be a subgroup of
K
. We show that (
\alpha
) and (
\beta
) are equivalent. First, (
\beta
) follows from (
\alpha
) at once, for if
\sigma(q_{E})^{*}=\xi^{*}
then
(\pi\sigma)(q_{E})^{*}=\pi(\sigma(q_{E})^{*})=\pi\xi^{*}=\xi^{\flat}
and so we may take
\varrho=\pi\sigma
to see that (
\beta
) holds. Conversely, assume that a morphism
\varrho\colon(G/E)^{*}\to\pi^{1}(X)
satisfies
\varrho(q_{E})^{*}=\xi^{\flat}
. Since the group
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R})
is divisible,
\nu
extends through
(q_{E})^{*}
to a morphism
\mu\colon(G/E)^{*}\to C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R})
. That is,
\mu(q_{E})^{*}=\nu
. Set
\sigma=\mu\oplus\varrho\colon(G/E)^{*}\to C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
. Then
\sigma(q_{E})^{*}=\mu(q_{E})^{*}\oplus\varrho(q_{E})^{*}=\nu\oplus\xi^{\flat}=\xi^{*}
and this verifies statement (
\alpha
).

We verify formula (
5.10
) from statement (2). In order to simplify notation, write
D=(1/d)\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X))
. Given a subgroup
E
of
K
, we shall use the standard identification of the group
(G/E)^{*}
with the annihilator
E^{\perp}
of
E
in
G^{*}
, the isomorphism between them being the Pontryagin dual
(p_{E})^{*}\colon(G/E)^{*}\to E^{\perp}\subseteq G^{*}
to the quotient morphism
p_{E}\colon G\to G/E
. Under this identification the morphism
(q_{E})^{*}
takes the form of the inclusion morphism
K^{\perp}\to E^{\perp}
. Further, via the annihilator mechanism in
G^{*}
, the subgroups
E
of
K
are in a one-to-one correspondence with the subgroups
A
of
G^{*}
containing
K^{\perp}
. Thus, by virtue of statement (1) of the theorem,
F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}
is the largest (with respect to inclusion) of all the subgroups
A
of
G^{*}
containing
K^{\perp}
, such that
\xi^{\flat}
extends to a morphism
\varrho\colon A\to\pi^{1}(X)
. Consequently, in order to prove the first equality in (
5.10
), we need to show that the following statements hold:

(i)

K^{\perp}\subseteq D
,

(ii)

for every
\chi\in G^{*}
,
\chi
is an element of
D
if and only if
\xi^{\flat}
extends to a morphism
K^{\perp}+\langle\chi\rangle\to\pi^{1}(X)
.

Before proving the two statements let us describe when a given character
\chi\in G^{*}
belongs to
D
. Given
\chi\in G^{*}
, we have
\chi^{d}\in K^{\perp}
by definition of
d
and so
\chi^{d}=\Upsilon p_{K}
for a unique
\Upsilon\in(G/K)^{*}
. Thus, under our identifications
\pi^{1}(G/K)\cong(G/K)^{*}\cong K^{\perp}
,
\chi
belongs to
D
if and only if
\xi^{\flat}(\Upsilon)\in d\pi^{1}(X)
.

Now, given
\chi\in K^{\perp}
, fix
\Upsilon\in(G/K)^{*}
with
\chi=\Upsilon p_{K}
. Then
\chi^{d}=\Upsilon^{d}p_{K}
and, since
\xi^{\flat}(\Upsilon^{d})=d\xi^{\flat}(\Upsilon)\in d\pi^{1}(X)
, statement (i) follows. To verify statement (ii), fix
\chi\in G^{*}
and let
\Upsilon\in(G/K)^{*}
be such that
\chi^{d}=\Upsilon p_{K}
. We need to show that the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

\xi^{\flat}
extends to a morphism
\varrho\colon K^{\perp}+\langle\chi\rangle\to\pi^{1}(X)
,

(b)

there is
f\in\pi^{1}(X)
with
\xi^{\flat}(\Upsilon)=df
.

Assume that condition (a) is satisfied and set
f=\varrho(\chi)
. Then
f\in\pi^{1}(X)
and
df=d\varrho(\chi)=\varrho(\chi^{d})=\xi^{\flat}(\chi^{d})=\xi^{\flat}(\Upsilon)
, which verifies (b). Conversely, let
f\in\pi^{1}(X)
be as in (b) and denote by
p
the smallest positive integer with
\chi^{p}\in K^{\perp}
. Since
\chi^{d}\in K^{\perp}
, it follows that
p
divides
d
and so
d=pq
for an appropriate positive integer
q
. We claim that
\xi^{\flat}(\chi^{p})=pf
. Indeed, by our choice of
f
,
q\xi^{\flat}(\chi^{p})=\xi^{\flat}(\chi^{pq})=\xi^{\flat}(\Upsilon)=df=q(pf)
and hence, since
\pi^{1}(X)
is a torsion-free group,
\xi^{\flat}(\chi^{p})=pf
. Using now the additive notation in
G^{*}
, define a map
\varrho\colon K^{\perp}+\langle\chi\rangle\to\pi^{1}(X)
by the rule
\varrho(\chi^{\prime}+k\chi)=\xi^{\flat}(\chi^{\prime})+kf
, where
\chi^{\prime}\in K^{\perp}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. We show that
\varrho
is well defined and it will immediately follow that
\varrho
is in fact a morphism extending
\xi^{\flat}
. So let
\chi_{1}^{\prime},\chi_{2}^{\prime}\in K^{\perp}
and
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}
, and assume that
\chi_{1}^{\prime}+k\chi=\chi_{2}^{\prime}+l\chi
. Then
(l-k)\chi=\chi_{1}^{\prime}-\chi_{2}^{\prime}\in K^{\perp}
and hence, by our definition of
p
,
l-k=pr
for an appropriate integer
r
. Thus,

\begin{split}\xi^{\flat}(\chi_{1}^{\prime})+kf&=\xi^{\flat}(\chi_{2}^{\prime}+r(p\chi))+kf=\xi^{\flat}(\chi_{2}^{\prime})+r\xi^{\flat}(p\chi)+kf\\
&=\xi^{\flat}(\chi_{2}^{\prime})+rpf+kf=\xi^{\flat}(\chi_{2}^{\prime})+lf,\end{split}

as was to be shown. This finishes the proof of the first equality from (
5.10
).

We verify the second equality from (
5.10
). Write
\eta=ld
with
l\in\mathbb{N}
. Since the group
\pi^{1}(X)
is torsion-free, the equivalence of the following conditions follows immediately for every
\chi\in G^{*}
:

•

\chi\in(1/\eta)\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(\eta\pi^{1}(X))
,

•

\xi^{\flat}(\chi^{\eta})\in\eta\pi^{1}(X)
,

•

l\xi^{\flat}(\chi^{d})\in l(d\pi^{1}(X))
,

•

\xi^{\flat}(\chi^{d})\in d\pi^{1}(X)
,

•

\chi\in(1/d)\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X))
.

This verifies the second equality from (
5.10
).

We finish the proof of the theorem be verifying statement (3). By virtue of statement (2), condition (
\iota
) is equivalent with
K^{\perp}=(1/d)\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X))
. Moreover, the inclusion
K^{\perp}\subseteq(1/d)\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X))
has been verified above (see condition (i)) and the inclusion
dG^{*}\cap\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X))\supseteq dK^{\perp}
from (
\iota\iota
) is clear. Consequently, it remains to verify the equivalence of the following statements:

•

(1/d)\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X))\subseteq K^{\perp}
,

•

dG^{*}\cap\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X))\subseteq dK^{\perp}
,

•

\xi^{\flat}(dG^{*}\setminus dK^{\perp})\cap d\pi^{1}(X)=\emptyset
.

The equivalence of the above statements follows by elementary arguments, with the help of the fact that the group
G^{*}
is torsion-free by the assumptions of the theorem.
∎

Corollary 5.34
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow on a compact connected space
X
. Consider the following statements:

(i)

for every
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
, the group
F(\mathcal{C})
is connected,

(ii)

the group
\pi^{1}(X)
is divisible.

If
\Gamma
has no non-trivial finite abelian quotient groups then
(i)
follows from
(ii)
. If
\Gamma\in\mathsf{CLAC}
is simply connected then
(ii)
follows from
(i)
.

Remark 5.35
.

Recall from Remark
4.40
that the following conditions are equivalent for every non-trivial connected group
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
:

•

G
has no proper non-trivial closed connected subgroup,

•

\mathop{\rm rank}(G^{*})=1
.

These conditions are satisfied by the circle group
G=\mathbb{T}^{1}
and by the solenoids
G=S_{{\bf p}}
. Consequently, if the acting group
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{F}
has no non-trivial finite abelian quotient groups, the space
X
has a divisible first cohomotopy group
\pi^{1}(X)
and
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
is a connected group with
\mathop{\rm rank}(G^{*})=1
, then every extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is either minimal or a coboundary.

Proof of Corollary
5.34
.

We show that (i) follows from (ii). To this end, assume that
\Gamma
has no non-trivial finite abelian quotient groups and that the group
\pi^{1}(X)
is divisible. Fix
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. We need to show that
F(\mathcal{C})
is connected or, equivalently, that
F(\mathcal{C})^{*}
is torsion-free. So let
\chi
be a torsion element of
F(\mathcal{C})^{*}
. Then
\chi F(\mathcal{C})\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{d}
for some positive integer
d
. The morphism
\chi\colon F(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
extends to a morphism of topological groups
G\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
, denote the latter one by the same symbol
\chi
. Since
F(\chi\mathcal{C})=\chi F(\mathcal{C})\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{d}
, it follows that
F(\kappa_{d}(\chi\mathcal{C}))=\kappa_{d}F(\chi\mathcal{C})=1
and hence
\kappa_{d}(\chi\mathcal{C})\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
, where
\kappa_{d}
is the
d
-endomorphism of
\mathbb{T}^{1}
. Let
\xi\in C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
be the transfer function for
\kappa_{d}(\chi\mathcal{C})
. Then by virtue of statement (2) from Theorem
5.32
and the second part of Remark
5.33
, we obtain

\begin{split}F(\chi\mathcal{C})^{\perp}&=\frac{1}{d}(\kappa_{d})^{*}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X))=\frac{1}{d}(\kappa_{d})^{*}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(\pi^{1}(X))\\
&=\frac{1}{d}(\kappa_{d})^{*}(\mathbb{Z})=\frac{1}{d}(d\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\end{split}

with the usual identifications
\pi^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1})\cong(\mathbb{T}^{1})^{*}\cong\mathbb{Z}
. This shows that
\chi F(\mathcal{C})=F(\chi\mathcal{C})=1
and so
\chi=1
as an element of
F(\mathcal{C})^{*}
. Thus, the group
F(\mathcal{C})^{*}
is torsion-free, as was to be shown.

We show that (ii) follows from (i). So assume that
\Gamma\in\mathsf{CLAC}
is simply connected. Since
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})\cong C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R})\oplus\pi^{1}(X)
and the group
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{R})
is divisible, the divisibility of
\pi^{1}(X)
is equivalent to the divisibility of
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
. Fix
f\in C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
. By Theorem
3.38
,
\mathop{\rm co}(f)\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
lifts across
\kappa_{d}\colon\mathbb{T}^{1}\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
to an extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and
F(\mathcal{C})\subseteq\ker(\kappa_{d})=\mathbb{Z}_{d}
. Since the group
F(\mathcal{C})
is connected by the assumptions,
F(\mathcal{C})=1
and hence
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. Let
g\in C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
be the transfer function for
\mathcal{C}
. Then
f
lifts across
\kappa_{d}
to
g
, which means that
f=g^{d}
holds in
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
. Since
f\in C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
were arbitrary, this shows that the group
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
is divisible and hence so is the group
\pi^{1}(X)
.
∎

Corollary 5.36
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow with
\Gamma\in\mathsf{CLAC}
simply connected and with
X
compact. Given
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
connected and
K\mathrel{\lhd}G
finite with the largest torsion coefficient
d
, the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

there is
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
with
F(\mathcal{C})=K
,

(b)

there is
\varrho\in\mathop{\rm Hom}(K^{\perp},\pi^{1}(X))
with
dG^{*}\cap\varrho^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X))=dK^{\perp}
.

Proof.

To see that (b) follows from (a), let
\xi
be the base point preserving transfer function for
p_{K}\mathcal{C}
. Then by Theorem
5.32
(3), condition (b) holds with
\varrho=\xi^{\flat}
. To see that (a) follows from (b), fix
\varrho
as in (b). By the isomorphism (
2.15
) from Subsection
2.4.10
,
\varrho=\xi^{\flat}
for an appropriate continuous base point preserving map
\xi\colon X\to G/K
. By the second part of Remark
3.39
, Theorem
4.10
and Theorem
4.14
(f),
\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)
lifts across
p_{K}
to an extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. Using Theorem
5.32
(3) once more now yields
F(\mathcal{C})=K
, which verifies condition (a).
∎

Example 5.37
.

Suppose that the assumptions of Theorem
5.14
and Theorem
5.32
are satisfied. Then one can use both (
5.1
) and (
5.10
) to determine the section
F(\mathcal{C})
of a given extension
\mathcal{C}
. As a matter of fact, the two formulas follow from each other. To show this, we verify the following statement which does not involve any dynamics on
X
at all:

(
*
)

Let
X
be a compact connected manifold,
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
be connected and
K\mathrel{\lhd}G
be finite with the largest torsion coefficient
d
. Given a continuous base point preserving map
\xi\colon X\to G/K
, the following identity holds:

\mathcal{E}_{K}\left(\xi^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(X)\right)^{\perp}=\frac{1}{d}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X)),

(5.13)

where the annihilator on the left is taken in
G^{*}
.

Proof of statement (
*
).

We shall prove (
*
) by verifying the equivalence of several pairs of auxiliary statements. This might not be the fastest way to arrive at (
5.13
) but we believe it is (one of) the most transparent.

Given
f\in\pi_{1}(G/K)
, let
\widetilde{f}
be the lift of
f
across
p_{K}
starting at
e
. Fix
\chi\in G^{*}
. It follows immediately from the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{K}
that the following statements are equivalent:

(
\alpha
)

\chi\in\mathcal{E}_{K}(\xi^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(X))^{\perp}
,

(
\beta
)

\chi\widetilde{f}
is a loop for every
f\in\xi^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(X)
.

Further, let
\kappa_{d}
be the
d
-endomorphism of
\mathbb{T}^{1}
. By definition of
d
, there is a unique character
\Upsilon\in(G/K)^{*}
of
G/K
with
\kappa_{d}\chi=\Upsilon p_{K}
. We show that (
\beta
) is equivalent with

(
\gamma
)

\Upsilon^{\sharp}\xi^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(X)\subseteq\kappa_{d}^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
.

Assume, first, that (
\beta
) holds and fix
f\in\xi^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(X)
. Then
\Upsilon^{\sharp}(f)=\Upsilon f=\Upsilon p_{K}\widetilde{f}=\kappa_{d}\chi\widetilde{f}\in\kappa_{d}^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
, since
\chi\widetilde{f}
is a loop. Conversely, let condition (
\gamma
) be fulfilled and fix
f\in\xi^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(X)
. By virtue of (
\gamma
),
\Upsilon f
lifts across
\kappa_{d}
to a loop
l
in
\mathbb{T}^{1}
based at
1
. That is,
\kappa_{d}l=\Upsilon f=\Upsilon p_{K}\widetilde{f}=\kappa_{d}\chi\widetilde{f}
and hence
l=\chi\widetilde{f}
. This shows that
\chi\widetilde{f}
is a loop and so statement (
\beta
) holds.

We continue the proof by showing that (
\gamma
) is equivalent with

(
\delta
)

\Upsilon\xi\in dC_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
.

Assume, first, that (
\delta
) holds and let
\eta\in C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
satisfy
\Upsilon\xi=\eta^{d}
. Then
(\Upsilon\xi)^{\sharp}=(\kappa_{d}\eta)^{\sharp}=\kappa_{d}^{\sharp}\eta^{\sharp}
and so
\Upsilon^{\sharp}\xi^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(X)=(\Upsilon\xi)^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(X)=\kappa_{d}^{\sharp}\eta^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(X)\subseteq\kappa_{d}^{\sharp}\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
, which verifies (
\gamma
). Conversely, let condition (
\gamma
) be fulfilled and define a morphism
\varrho\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1})
by the formula
\varrho=(1/d)(\Upsilon\xi)^{\sharp}
. By our assumptions on
X
,
\varrho=\eta^{\sharp}
for an appropriate
\eta\in C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
. Then
((\Upsilon\xi).(\eta^{d})^{-1})^{\sharp}=0
and so there is
\zeta\in C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
with
(\Upsilon\xi).(\eta^{d})^{-1}=\zeta^{d}
. Consequently,
\Upsilon\xi=(\eta.\zeta)^{d}\in dC_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
and statement (
\delta
) follows.

We finish the proof by showing that (
\delta
) is equivalent with

(
\epsilon
)

\chi\in(1/d)(\xi^{\flat})^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X))
;

the equivalence of (
\alpha
) and (
\epsilon
) will then yield (
5.13
). Let
\pi
be the canonical projection
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})\to\pi^{1}(X)
. Since the complementary summand of
\pi^{1}(X)
in
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
is divisible, the following statements are equivalent:

•

\Upsilon\xi\in dC_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
,

•

\xi^{\flat}(\Upsilon)=\pi(\Upsilon\xi)\in d\pi^{1}(X)
,

•

\chi\in(1/d)(\xi^{\flat})^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X))
.

This verifies the equivalence of (
\delta
) and (
\epsilon
).
∎

In order to formulate our next result we fix some notation. Let
K\in\mathsf{CAGp}
be a totally disconnected group and let
(H_{j})_{j\in J}
be a family of open subgroups of
K
indexed by a directed set
J
, such that
H_{j}\subseteq H_{i}
for
i\leq j
and
\bigcap_{j\in J}H_{j}=e
. For
j\in J
set
K_{j}=K/H_{j}
and let
q_{j}\colon K\to K_{j}
be the quotient morphism. Clearly,
K_{j}
is a finite abelian group; denote by
d_{j}
its largest torsion coefficient. Given
i\leq j
, there is a unique (quotient) morphism
q_{ij}\colon K_{j}\to K_{i}
with
q_{ij}q_{j}=q_{i}
and it follows that
d_{i}
divides
d_{j}
. We call
(d_{j})_{j\in J}
a
generating net of torsions for
K
. If there exist
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in J
such that
d_{j}=d
for all
j\geq i
then we say that
(d_{j})_{j\in J}

terminates at
d
. Finally, since
\bigcap_{j\in J}H_{j}=e
, the inverse limit of the inverse system
(K_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q_{ij}}}{{\longleftarrow}}K_{j})_{i\leq j}
in
\mathsf{CAGp}
is the group
K
along with the morphisms
q_{j}
as the limit projections.

Remark 5.38
.

Notice the following facts.

•

It is possible for two non-isomorphic groups to have a common generating net of torsions. For example, if
d\geq 2
is an integer then both
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{d}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{d}
have as their generating net of torsions the singleton consisting of
d
alone.

•

A given totally disconnected group
K\in\mathsf{CAGp}
has in general many distinct generating nets of torsions. Nevertheless, if
(d_{j})_{j\in J}
and
(d_{l}^{\prime})_{l\in L}
are two such nets then they are related by the following (symmetric) rules:

\circ

for every
j\in J
there is
l\in L
such that
d_{j}
divides
d_{l}^{\prime}
,

\circ

for every
l\in L
there is
j\in J
such that
d_{l}^{\prime}
divides
d_{j}
.

In particular, if
K
possesses a generating net of torsions terminating at
d
then each of its generating nets of torsions terminates at
d
.

•

Assume that
(d_{j})_{j\in J}
is a net in
\mathbb{N}
with the property that
d_{i}
divides
d_{j}
for
i\leq j
. Then there exists a totally disconnected group
K\in\mathsf{CAGp}
, of which
(d_{j})_{j\in J}
is a generating net of torsions. Indeed, for
i\leq j
let
q_{ij}
be the natural quotient morphism
\mathbb{Z}_{d_{j}}\to\mathbb{Z}_{d_{i}}
(that is, under the usual identifications
\mathbb{Z}_{d_{i}}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{d_{j}}\subseteq\mathbb{T}^{1}
,
q_{ij}
acts by the rule
q_{ij}(z)=z^{d_{j}/d_{i}}
). Then the inverse limit
K=\lim_{\leftarrow}(\mathbb{Z}_{d_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q_{ij}}}{{\longleftarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{d_{j}})
in
\mathsf{CAGp}
is totally disconnected and it has
(d_{j})_{j\in J}
as its generating net of torsions.

We would like to make now one more remark in order to make some of our later arguments more transparent.

Remark 5.39
.

Let
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and
K\mathrel{\lhd}G
be totally disconnected. Fix a generating net of torsions
(d_{j})_{j\in J}
for
K
. Then

(
*
)

for every
\chi\in G^{*}
there is
j\in J
with
\chi^{d_{j}}\in K^{\perp}
.

To verify statement (
*
), fix
\chi\in G^{*}
. Recall that
V=\{\exp(i2\pi t):-1/3<t<1/3\}
is a neighbourhood of
1
in
\mathbb{T}^{1}
which contains no non-trivial subgroup of
\mathbb{T}^{1}
. Fix a neighbourhood
U
of
e
in
G
with
\chi(U)\subseteq V
. By definition of
(d_{j})_{j\in J}
, there exist
j\in J
and an open subgroup
H_{j}
of
K
such that
H_{j}\subseteq U
and
K/H_{j}
has
d_{j}
as its largest torsion coefficient. Since
\chi(H_{j})\subseteq\chi(U)\subseteq V
is a subgroup of
\mathbb{T}^{1}
, it follows that
\chi\in H_{j}^{\perp}
. Consequently, the restriction
\chi|_{K}
of
\chi
to
K
equals
\chi|_{K}=\chi_{j}p_{j}
for some
\chi_{j}\in(K/H_{j})^{*}
, where
p_{j}
denotes the quotient morphism
K\to K/H_{j}
. Then
\chi_{j}^{d_{j}}=1
and hence
\chi^{d_{j}}\in K^{\perp}
, as was to be shown.

Theorem 5.40
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow and
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
be connected. Let
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
and fix
z\in X
. Assume that
K\mathrel{\lhd}G
is totally disconnected with
p_{K}\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G/K)
and denote by
\xi
the transfer function of
p_{K}\mathcal{C}
with
\xi(z)=e
. If
X
is compact connected,
\Gamma
has no non-trivial finite abelian quotient groups and
(d_{j})_{j\in J}
is a generating net of torsions for
K
then

\begin{split}F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}&=\bigcup_{j\in J}\frac{1}{d_{j}}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}\left(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X)\right)=\sum_{j\in J}\frac{1}{d_{j}}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}\left(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X)\right)\\
&=\lim_{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{d_{j}}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}\left(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X)\right),\end{split}

(5.14)

where
\xi^{\flat}
stands for the morphism
G^{*}\supseteq K^{\perp}\to\pi^{1}(X)
under the usual identifications
\pi^{1}(G/K)\cong(G/K)^{*}\cong K^{\perp}
.

Remark 5.41
.

We wish to add the following remarks.

•

In the third step of the proof of Theorem
5.40
we shall observe that the inclusion
(1/d_{i})(\xi^{\flat})^{-1}(d_{i}\pi^{1}(X))\subseteq(1/d_{j})(\xi^{\flat})^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))
holds for all
i,j\in J
with
i\leq j
. Consequently, if the group
K
possesses a generating net of torsions terminating at
d\in\mathbb{N}
then the formula (
5.14
) takes the form

F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}=\frac{1}{d}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X)),

just as in Theorem
5.32
(2), where the group
K
was assumed to be finite with the largest torsion coefficient
d
.

•

It follows from the identity (
5.14
) that the inclusion

K^{\perp}\subseteq\bigcup_{j\in J}\frac{1}{d_{j}}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))

holds under the assumptions of Theorem
5.40
. Consequently, the equality
F(\mathcal{C})=K
holds if and only if
(1/d_{j})(\xi^{\flat})^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))\subseteq K^{\perp}
for every
j\in J
.

Proof of Theorem
5.40
.

We divide the proof of the theorem into three steps. A rough idea of the proof is to use Theorem
5.32
together with the continuity of the functor
F\colon\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}\to\mathsf{CAGp}
from Theorem
3.7
.

1st step.
We begin by fixing some notation and by collecting some useful observations.

Let
H_{j},K_{j},q_{j}
(
j\in J
) and
q_{ij}
(
i\leq j
) be as in the paragraph preceding Remark
5.38
. Further, for
j\in J
let
p_{j}\colon G\to G/H_{j}
be the quotient morphism and for
i\leq j
let
p_{ij}
be the uniqe quotient morphism
G/H_{j}\to G/H_{i}
with
p_{ij}p_{j}=p_{i}
. Since
\bigcap_{j\in J}H_{j}=e
, the inverse system
(G/H_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ij}}}{{\longleftarrow}}G/H_{j})_{i\leq j}
in
\mathsf{CAGp}
has as its inverse limit the group
G
along with the morphisms
p_{j}
as the limit projections. For
j\in J
let
r_{j}
be the (unique) morphism
G/H_{j}\to G/K
with
r_{j}p_{j}=p_{K}
. Clearly,
r_{j}
is a quotient morphism with a finite kernel
\ker(r_{j})=p_{j}(K)\cong K/H_{j}=K_{j}
. Given
j\in J
, write
\mathcal{C}_{j}=p_{j}\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G/H_{j})
. Then
r_{j}\mathcal{C}_{j}=r_{j}p_{j}\mathcal{C}=p_{K}\mathcal{C}=\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)
. Finally, since
G=\lim_{\leftarrow}(G/H_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ij}}}{{\longleftarrow}}G/H_{j})
in
\mathsf{CAGp}
, it follows from Lemma
2.21
that
\mathcal{C}=\lim_{\leftarrow}(\mathcal{C}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ij}}}{{\longleftarrow}}\mathcal{C}_{j})
in
\mathop{\rm\mathsf{CAGpZ}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and hence, by Theorem
3.7
(5),
F(\mathcal{C})=\lim_{\leftarrow}(F(\mathcal{C}_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ij}}}{{\longleftarrow}}F(\mathcal{C}_{j}))
in
\mathsf{CAGp}
.

2nd step.
The map
\xi\colon X\to G/K
induces a morphism between the first cohomotopy groups
\xi^{\flat}\colon\pi^{1}(G/K)\to\pi^{1}(X)
, acting by the rule
\xi^{\flat}(f)=f\xi
. Under the identifications
\pi^{1}(G/K)\cong(G/K)^{*}\cong[K^{\perp},G^{*}]
, the morphism
\xi^{\flat}
has the form

\xi^{\flat}=\kappa\colon[K^{\perp},G^{*}]\ni\chi\mapsto\widetilde{\chi}\xi\in\pi^{1}(X),

where, for a given
\chi\in[K^{\perp},G^{*}]
,
\widetilde{\chi}
is the unique character of
G/K
with
\widetilde{\chi}p_{K}=\chi
. Now fix
j\in J
. Then there is a topological isomorphism
G/K\cong(G/H_{j})/(K/H_{j})
, which induces isomorphisms of groups

\pi^{1}(G/K)\cong((G/H_{j})/(K/H_{j}))^{*}\cong[(K/H_{j})^{\perp},(G/H_{j})^{*}].

By using these isomorphisms the morphism
\xi^{\flat}
takes the form

\xi^{\flat}=\lambda\colon[(K/H_{j})^{\perp},(G/H_{j})^{*}]\ni\Upsilon\mapsto\bar{\Upsilon}\xi\in\pi^{1}(X),

where, for a given
\Upsilon\in[(K/H_{j})^{\perp},(G/H_{j})^{*}]
,
\bar{\Upsilon}
is the unique character of
G/K
with
\bar{\Upsilon}r_{j}=\Upsilon
. Our aim in this step of the proof is to verify the formula

\left[\left(p_{j}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{j})\right)^{\perp},G^{*}\right]=\left(\frac{1}{d_{j}}\kappa^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))\right)\cap\left[H_{j}^{\perp},G^{*}\right].

(5.15)

First observe that the identity
\lambda r_{j}^{*}=\kappa p_{K}^{*}
holds directly by definitions of
\lambda
and
\kappa
.

Let
k\colon(K/H_{j})^{\perp}\to K^{\perp}
be the restriction of the morphism
p_{j}^{*}\colon(G/H_{j})^{*}\to G^{*}
dual to
p_{j}
(notice that
p_{j}
maps
K
onto
K/H_{j}
and hence
k
is well defined). We claim that
\lambda=\kappa k
. To verify the claim, fix
\Upsilon\in(K/H_{j})^{\perp}
. Then, by definition of the morphisms
\lambda
and
\kappa
,

•

\lambda(\Upsilon)=\bar{\Upsilon}\xi
, where
\bar{\Upsilon}\in(G/K)^{*}
satisfies
\bar{\Upsilon}r_{j}=\Upsilon
,

•

\kappa k(\Upsilon)=\kappa(\Upsilon p_{j})=\widetilde{\chi}\xi
, where
\widetilde{\chi}\in(G/K)^{*}
satisfies
\widetilde{\chi}p_{K}=\Upsilon p_{j}
.

Consequently, it suffices to show that
\bar{\Upsilon}=\widetilde{\chi}
or, equivalently, that
\bar{\Upsilon}p_{K}=\Upsilon p_{j}
. The latter identity follows by a simple computation

\bar{\Upsilon}p_{K}=\bar{\Upsilon}(r_{j}p_{j})=(\bar{\Upsilon}r_{j})p_{j}=\Upsilon p_{j}.

This show that, indeed,
\lambda=\kappa k
and hence, by definition of
k
,

\lambda^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))=(p_{j}^{*})^{-1}\Big(\kappa^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))\Big)\cap\left[(K/H_{j})^{\perp},(G/H_{j})^{*}\right].

(5.16)

Further, the morphism
r_{j}\colon G/H_{j}\to G/K
has a finite kernel
\ker(r_{j})\cong K/H_{j}=K_{j}
with the largest torsion coefficient
d_{j}
. We may therefore use (
5.10
) from Theorem
5.32
(see also the second part of Remark
5.33
) to obtain

\left[F(\mathcal{C}_{j})^{\perp},(G/H_{j})^{*}\right]=\frac{1}{d_{j}}r_{j}^{*}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))=\frac{1}{d_{j}}\lambda^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X)),

(5.17)

where the symbol
\xi^{\flat}=\kappa p_{K}^{*}=\lambda r_{j}^{*}
now stands for the morphism
(G/K)^{*}\to\pi^{1}(X)
under the usual identification
\pi^{1}(G/K)\cong(G/K)^{*}
. Now, by using (
5.16
) and (
5.17
), we obtain

\begin{split}\left[\left(p_{j}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{j})\right)^{\perp},G^{*}\right]&=p_{j}^{*}\left[F(\mathcal{C}_{j})^{\perp},(G/H_{j})^{*}\right]=p_{j}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{d_{j}}\lambda^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))\right)\\
&=p_{j}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{d_{j}}\Bigg((p_{j}^{*})^{-1}\Big(\kappa^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))\Big)\cap\left[(K/H_{j})^{\perp},(G/H_{j})^{*}\right]\Bigg)\right)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\text{i})}}{{=}}p_{j}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{d_{j}}\Bigg((p_{j}^{*})^{-1}\Big(\kappa^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))\Big)\Bigg)\right)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\text{ii})}}{{=}}p_{j}^{*}\left((p_{j}^{*})^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{d_{j}}\kappa^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))\right)\right)\\
&=\left(\frac{1}{d_{j}}\kappa^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))\right)\cap\mathop{\rm im}(p_{j}^{*})\\
&=\left(\frac{1}{d_{j}}\kappa^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))\right)\cap\left[H_{j}^{\perp},G^{*}\right],\end{split}

where the equalities (i) and (ii) follow, respectively, from the following facts:

(i)

\Upsilon^{d_{j}}\in[(K/H_{j})^{\perp},(G/H_{j})^{*}]
for every
\Upsilon\in(G/H_{j})^{*}
; this follows from the definition of
d_{j}
as the largest torsion coefficient of
K_{j}=K/H_{j}
,

(ii)

p_{j}^{*}(\Upsilon^{d_{j}})=(p_{j}^{*}(\Upsilon))^{d_{j}}
for every
\Upsilon\in(G/H_{j})^{*}
; this is clear.

This verifies the desired identity (
5.15
) and finishes the second step of the proof.

3rd step.
We finish the proof of the theorem. First, let us mention that from now on, all the annihilators are taken in
G^{*}
and
\xi^{\flat}
stands for the morphism
\kappa
as described at the beginning of the second step of the proof.

By Lemma
3.15
and Remark
3.16
, the identity
F(\mathcal{C})=\lim_{\leftarrow}(F(\mathcal{C}_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{ij}}}{{\longleftarrow}}F(\mathcal{C}_{j}))
from the first step of the proof can be reformulated by writing
F(\mathcal{C})=\bigcap_{j\in J}p_{j}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{j})
, where
p_{i}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{i})\supseteq p_{j}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{j})
for all
i\leq j
. Consequently,

F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}=\Bigg(\bigcap_{j\in J}p_{j}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{j})\Bigg)^{\perp}=\sum_{j\in J}\left(p_{j}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{j})\right)^{\perp}=\bigcup_{j\in J}\left(p_{j}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{j})\right)^{\perp}.

(5.18)

By definitions of the objects
H_{j}
,
\mathcal{C}_{j}
and
d_{j}
(
j\in J
), the following inclusions hold for all
j\geq i
:

(
\alpha
)

H_{i}^{\perp}\subseteq H_{j}^{\perp}
,

(
\beta
)

(p_{i}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{i}))^{\perp}\subseteq(p_{j}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{j}))^{\perp}
,

(
\gamma
)

(1/d_{i})(\xi^{\flat})^{-1}(d_{i}\pi^{1}(X))\subseteq(1/d_{j})(\xi^{\flat})^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))
.

By using these inclusions along with (
5.18
) and (
5.15
), we obtain for every
i\in J
,

\begin{split}F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}\cap H_{i}^{\perp}&=\Bigg(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in J\\
j\geq i\end{subarray}}\left(p_{j}^{-1}F(\mathcal{C}_{j})\right)^{\perp}\Bigg)\cap H_{i}^{\perp}\\
&=\Bigg(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in J\\
j\geq i\end{subarray}}\left(\left(\frac{1}{d_{j}}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))\right)\cap H_{j}^{\perp}\right)\Bigg)\cap H_{i}^{\perp}\\
&=\Bigg(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in J\\
j\geq i\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{d_{j}}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))\Bigg)\cap H_{i}^{\perp}\\
&=\Bigg(\bigcup_{j\in J}\frac{1}{d_{j}}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))\Bigg)\cap H_{i}^{\perp}.\end{split}

Finally, since
\bigcup_{i\in J}H_{i}^{\perp}=G^{*}
, it follows that

\begin{split}F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}&=\bigcup_{i\in J}\left(F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}\cap H_{i}^{\perp}\right)=\bigcup_{i\in J}\Bigg(\Bigg(\bigcup_{j\in J}\frac{1}{d_{j}}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))\Bigg)\cap H_{i}^{\perp}\Bigg)\\
&=\bigcup_{j\in J}\frac{1}{d_{j}}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X)),\end{split}

which verifies the first equality from the theorem. The second and the third equality follow from the first one by virtue of (
\gamma
).
∎

5.6
Existence of prescribed sections, part 2

In this section we continue our discussion from Sections
5.3
and
5.4
. Given a minimal flow
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
, a group
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and its subgroup
H\mathrel{\lhd}G
, we ask whether there is an extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
with
F(\mathcal{C})=H
. Similarly to Section
5.4
, we concentrate mainly on the tori
G=\mathbb{T}^{n}
and the solenoids
G=S_{\bf p}
. Our interest now is in extensions of flows with simply connected acting groups
\Gamma
and arbitrary continua
X
as phase spaces. For such flows
\mathcal{F}
, the results of Section
5.5
will serve us as the main tool.

We begin this section by considering first the situation when
X
is a compact manifold,
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
is an arbitrary connected group and
H
is totally disconnected; this is done in Theorem
5.42
. (This case has already been covered by our Theorem
5.22
in Section
5.3
, but our results from Section
5.5
will enable us to see this case from a different point of view.) Then we turn to the case of a general continuum
X
in the base and the fibre groups
G=\mathbb{T}^{n}
and
G=S_{\bf p}
; in Theorems
5.44
and
5.48
we show how the existence of prescribed totally disconnected sections
F(\mathcal{C})
for extensions
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
is related to the algebraic properties of
\pi^{1}(X)
. At the end of this section we also give sufficient conditions for the existence of minimal extensions in the groups
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
.

Theorem 5.42
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow with
\Gamma\in\mathsf{CLAC}
simply connected and with
X
a compact (connected) manifold, and let
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
be connected. Assume that
K
is a closed non-trivial totally disconnected subgroup of
G
and
(d_{j})_{j\in J}
is a generating net of torsions for
K
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

there exists
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
with
F(\mathcal{C})=K
,

(2)

the group
K^{\perp}
can be expressed as a direct sum
K^{\perp}=F\oplus Q
, where
F
is a free abelian group with
\mathop{\rm rank}(F)\leq\mathop{\rm rank}(\pi^{1}(X))
and
Q
is a
d_{j}
-pure subgroup of
G^{*}
for every
j\in J
,

(3)

the group
G/K
contains a topological direct summand
T
, which is a torus with
\dim(T)\leq\mathop{\rm rank}(\pi^{1}(X))
and whose pre-image
p_{K}^{-1}(T)
is connected.

Remark 5.43
.

We wish to add the following remarks.

•

From our proof of Lemma
5.20
it follows that
\pi^{1}(X)
is isomorphic to
H_{1}^{w}(X)
. Since the latter group is finitely generated by our assumptions on
X
, it follows that the rank
\mathop{\rm rank}(\pi^{1}(X))=\mathop{\rm rank}(H_{1}^{w}(X))
is finite.

•

In connection with condition (3) observe that for every closed subgroup
T
of
G/K
, the group
p_{K}^{-1}(T)
is connected if and only if
K\subseteq p_{K}^{-1}(T)_{0}
. Indeed, the implication from left to right follows from an obvious inclusion
K\subseteq p_{K}^{-1}(T)
and the converse implication follows from the equality
p_{K}(p_{K}^{-1}(T)_{0})=T
. We shall use this observation also in our proof of the theorem.

•

To clarify the nature of condition (3), let us give an example of a morphism
p=p_{K}\colon G\to G/K
which the condition excludes. Let
T
be an arbitrary torus,
r\colon S_{\bf p}\to S_{\bf q}
be an epimorphism between two solenoids with a finite kernel
F
and set
p=\mathop{\rm id}_{T}\oplus r\colon T\oplus S_{\bf p}\to T\oplus S_{\bf q}
. Then
p^{-1}(T)=T\oplus F
is not connected and so condition (3) fails. (Also, in connection with the previous remark observe that the kernel
0\oplus F
of
p
is not contained in
p^{-1}(T)_{0}=T\oplus 0
.)

Proof of Theorem
5.42
.

We divide the proof into three steps.

1st step.
We show that (2) follows from (1).

So let
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
be an extension with
F(\mathcal{C})=K
. Denote by
\xi
the base point preserving transfer function for
p_{K}\mathcal{C}
. Let
Q
be the kernel of the induced morphism
\xi^{\flat}\colon K^{\perp}\to\pi^{1}(X)
with the usual identifications
\pi^{1}(G/K)\cong(G/K)^{*}\cong K^{\perp}
. By our assumptions on
X
,
\pi^{1}(X)
is a free abelian group with a finite rank, and so the group
Q
is a direct summand in
K^{\perp}
. Let
F
be a complementary summand to
Q
in
K^{\perp}
. Since the restriction
\xi^{\flat}\colon F\to\pi^{1}(X)
is a monomorphism, the group
F
is free abelian with
\mathop{\rm rank}(F)\leq\mathop{\rm rank}(\pi^{1}(X))
. Now, in order to show that the group
Q
is
d_{j}
-pure in
G^{*}
for every
j\in J
, notice that, by virtue of Theorem
5.40
,

K^{\perp}=F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}=\bigcup_{j\in J}\frac{1}{d_{j}}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X)),

which yields the inclusion

\frac{1}{d_{j}}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))\subseteq K^{\perp}

(5.19)

for every
j\in J
. Fix
j\in J
and
\chi\in G^{*}
with
d_{j}\chi\in Q
; we show that
\chi\in Q
. By definition of
Q
,
\xi^{\flat}(d_{j}\chi)=0\in d_{j}\pi^{1}(X)
, which means that
\chi\in(1/d_{j})(\xi^{\flat})^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))
. Thus, by virtue of (
5.19
),
\chi\in K^{\perp}
. Write
\chi=f+q
with
f\in F
and
q\in Q
. Then
d_{j}f+d_{j}q=d_{j}\chi\in Q
and hence
d_{j}f=0
. Since the group
F
is torsion-free, it follows that
f=0
. Consequently,
\chi=q\in Q
, as was to be shown.

2nd step.
We show that (1) follows from (2).

So let
F
and
Q
be as in (2). Fix a morphism
\varrho\colon K^{\perp}\to\pi^{1}(X)
with the following properties:

•

\varrho
restricted to
F
is a monomorphism and
\varrho(F)
is a direct summand in
\pi^{1}(X)
,

•

Q
equals the kernel
\ker(\varrho)
of
\varrho
.

By virtue of the isomorphism (
2.15
) from Subsection
2.4.10
, there is a continuous base point preserving map
\xi\colon X\to G/K
with
\xi^{\flat}=\varrho
. Further, by the second part of Remark
3.39
, Theorem
4.10
and Theorem
4.14
(f),
\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)
lifts across
p_{K}
to an extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)
. We show that
F(\mathcal{C})=K
. By Theorem
5.40
and Remark
5.41
, it suffices to show that
(1/d_{j})\varrho^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))\subseteq K^{\perp}
for every
j\in J
. So fix
j\in J
and let
\chi\in(1/d_{j})\varrho^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))
. Then, in particular,
d_{j}\chi\in K^{\perp}
. Write
d_{j}\chi=f+q
with
f\in F
and
q\in Q
. Then
d_{j}\pi^{1}(X)\ni\varrho(d_{j}\chi)=\varrho(f)
and hence, by definition of
\varrho
,
f=d_{j}f^{\prime}
for some
f^{\prime}\in F
. Consequently,
d_{j}\chi=d_{j}f^{\prime}+q
and hence
d_{j}(\chi-f^{\prime})=q\in Q
. Since the group
Q
is
d_{j}
-pure in
G^{*}
by the assumptions, it follows that
\chi-f^{\prime}\in Q
. Thus,
\chi\in f^{\prime}+Q\subseteq F\oplus Q=K^{\perp}
, which verifies the desired inclusion
(1/d_{j})\varrho^{-1}(d_{j}\pi^{1}(X))\subseteq K^{\perp}
.

3rd step.
We show that (3) follows from (2).

So fix
F,Q
as in (2). There exist
T,R\mathrel{\lhd}G/K
such that
F=R^{\perp}
and
Q=T^{\perp}
. We claim that there is a topological splitting
G/K=T\oplus R
. First, we have
e=F\cap Q=R^{\perp}\cap T^{\perp}=(R+T)^{\perp}
, which shows that
R+T=G/K
. Second,
(G/K)^{*}=F+Q=R^{\perp}+T^{\perp}=(R\cap T)^{\perp}
and so
R\cap T=e
. It follows that the splitting
G/K=T\oplus R
is algebraic and hence, by compactness of
T
and
R
, it is also topological. Moreover,
T^{*}\cong(G/K)^{*}/T^{\perp}=(F\oplus Q)/Q\cong F
, which shows that
T
is a torus with dimension
\dim(T)=\mathop{\rm rank}(F)\leq\mathop{\rm rank}(\pi^{1}(X))
.

Write
\widetilde{T}=p_{K}^{-1}(T)
. We need to show that the group
\widetilde{T}
is connected. Given
j\in J
, the
d_{j}
-purity of
Q
in
G^{*}
translates into the following, mutually equivalent statements:

•

for every
\chi\in G^{*}
,
\chi^{d_{j}}\in\widetilde{T}^{\perp}
implies
\chi\in\widetilde{T}^{\perp}
,

•

\kappa_{d_{j}}(\widetilde{T})^{\perp}=\widetilde{T}^{\perp}
,

•

\kappa_{d_{j}}\colon\widetilde{T}\to\widetilde{T}
is an epimorphism.

It follows that
\kappa_{d_{j}}\colon\widetilde{T}/\widetilde{T}_{0}\to\widetilde{T}/\widetilde{T}_{0}
is an epimorphism for every
j\in J
.

Set
L=K\cap\widetilde{T}_{0}
. Then
L
is clearly a closed subgroup of
K
. The inclusion morphism
K\to\widetilde{T}
induces a morphism
\varphi\colon K/L\to\widetilde{T}/\widetilde{T}_{0}
, acting by the rule
\varphi(k+L)=k+\widetilde{T}_{0}
for every
k\in K
. We claim that
\varphi
is an isomorphism. First,
\varphi
is a monomorphism by definition of
L
. Second, since the group
T
is connected and
p_{K}\colon\widetilde{T}\to T
is an epimorphism, the restriction
p_{K}\colon\widetilde{T}_{0}\to T
is also an epimorphism and so every element of
\widetilde{T}/\widetilde{T}_{0}
intersects
K
. This shows that
\varphi
is an epimorphism. Thus, the groups
\widetilde{T}/\widetilde{T}_{0}
and
K/L
are (topologically) isomorphic. Since the morphisms
\kappa_{d_{j}}\colon\widetilde{T}/\widetilde{T}_{0}\to\widetilde{T}/\widetilde{T}_{0}
are all epic by the previous paragraph, it follows that so are the morphisms
\kappa_{d_{j}}\colon K/L\to K/L
for all
j\in J
.

Let
(c_{i})_{i\in I}
be a generating net of torsions for
K/L
. Given
i\in I
, the (topological) group
K/L
factors onto
\mathbb{Z}_{c_{i}}
and hence so does the group
K
. Consequently, for every
i\in I
there is
j\in J
such that
c_{i}
divides
d_{j}
. On the other hand, since
\kappa_{d_{j}}\colon K/L\to K/L
is an epimorphism for every
j\in J
, it follows that all
\kappa_{d_{j}}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{c_{i}}\to\mathbb{Z}_{c_{i}}
are epimorphisms. Hence,
c_{i}
is co-prime with
d_{j}
for all
i\in I
and
j\in J
. Summarizing, we have
c_{i}=1
for every
i\in I
and so
L=K
. This shows that
K\subseteq\widetilde{T}_{0}
and hence, by part one of Remark
5.43
, the group
\widetilde{T}=p_{K}^{-1}(T)
is connected.

4th step.
We show that (2) follows from (3).

So fix
T,R
as in (3) and write
Q=T^{\perp}
,
F=R^{\perp}
. As above, one verifies that
(G/K)^{*}=F\oplus Q
and
F^{*}\cong T
, which shows that
F
is a free abelian group with
\mathop{\rm rank}(F)=\dim(T)\leq\mathop{\rm rank}(\pi^{1}(X))
. It remains to show that the group
Q
is
d_{j}
-pure in
G^{*}
for every
j\in J
. That is, we must verify that the following condition holds for every
j\in J
:

(
*
)

given
\chi\in G^{*}
and
\Upsilon\in Q
with
\chi^{d_{j}}=\Upsilon p_{K}
, there is
\Lambda\in Q
with
\chi=\Lambda p_{K}
.

So let
\chi
and
\Upsilon
be as in (
*
). Since
Q=T^{\perp}
and
\kappa_{d_{j}}(\widetilde{T})=\widetilde{T}
by connectedness of
\widetilde{T}
, it follows that

\chi(\widetilde{T})=\chi(\kappa_{d_{j}}(\widetilde{T}))=\chi^{d_{j}}(\widetilde{T})=\Upsilon p_{K}(\widetilde{T})=\Upsilon(T)=1.

This means, in particular, that
\chi\in K^{\perp}
, and hence
\chi=\Lambda p_{K}
for an appropriate character
\Lambda\in(G/K)^{*}
of
G/K
. Moreover,
\Lambda(T)=\Lambda p_{K}(\widetilde{T})=\chi(\widetilde{T})=1
, which shows that
\Lambda\in T^{\perp}=Q
. This verifies (
*
).
∎

Theorem 5.44
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow with
\Gamma\in\mathsf{CLAC}
simply connected and with
X
compact. Fix positive integers
n
and
d\geq 2
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

for every finite subgroup
K
of
\mathbb{T}^{n}
, whose largest torsion coefficient divides
d
, there exists
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{n})
with
F(\mathcal{C})=K
,

(2)

there are
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in\pi^{1}(X)
such that if
k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfy
\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}f_{i}\in d\pi^{1}(X)
then
d
divides
k_{i}
for every
i=1,\dots,n
,

(3)

\pi^{1}(X)/d\pi^{1}(X)
has rank at least
n
, when considered as a
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
-module.

Proof.

Condition (3) is only a reformulation of condition (2) and so it suffices to verify the equivalence of (1) and (2). We do this in two steps.

1st step.
We show that (2) follows from (1).

Set
K=(\mathbb{Z}_{d})^{n}\mathrel{\lhd}\mathbb{T}^{n}
and use (1) to find
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{n})
with
F(\mathcal{C})=K
. Consider the morphism
\kappa_{d}\colon\mathbb{T}^{n}\to\mathbb{T}^{n}
. Since
\ker(\kappa_{d})=K
,
\kappa_{d}\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{n})
. Let
\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})\in C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{n})
be the base point preserving transfer function for
\kappa_{d}\mathcal{C}
. Denote by
\pi
the canonical projection morphism
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})\to\pi^{1}(X)
and set
f_{i}=\pi(\xi_{i})\in\pi^{1}(X)
for
i=1,\dots,n
. Then the induced morphism
\xi^{\flat}\colon\pi^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{n})\to\pi^{1}(X)
acts by the rule
\xi^{\flat}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}f_{i}
under the usual natural identifications
\pi^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{n})\cong(\mathbb{T}^{n})^{*}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

Now, by virtue of Theorem
5.32
and part two of Remark
5.33
,

K^{\perp}=F(\mathcal{C})^{\perp}=\frac{1}{d}\kappa_{d}^{*}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X)).

Observe also that under the identification
(\mathbb{T}^{n})^{*}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{n}
used above,
\kappa_{d}^{*}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{n}\to\mathbb{Z}^{n}
acts by the rule
\kappa_{d}^{*}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})=(dk_{1},\dots,dk_{n})
and
K^{\perp}=\mathop{\rm im}(\kappa_{d}^{*})=d\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Consequently, the following conditions are equivalent for every
(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
:

•

d
divides
k_{i}
for every
i=1,\dots,n
,

•

(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\in d\mathbb{Z}^{n}=K^{\perp}
,

•

(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\in(1/d)\kappa_{d}^{*}(\xi^{\flat})^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X))
,

•

\xi^{\flat}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\in d\pi^{1}(X)
,

•

\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}f_{i}\in d\pi^{1}(X)
.

This shows that
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in\pi^{1}(X)
satisfy the condition from statement (2).

2nd step.
We show that (1) follows from (2).

Fix
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in\pi^{1}(X)
as in (2) and set
\xi=(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})\in C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{n})
. (Here we regard
\pi^{1}(X)
as a subgroup of
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
in a usual way.) By virtue of Theorem
3.38
,
\mathop{\rm co}(\xi)
lifts across
\kappa_{d}\colon\mathbb{T}^{n}\to\mathbb{T}^{n}
to an extension
\mathcal{D}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{n})
. From Theorem
5.32
and part two of Remark
5.33
it follows that

F(\mathcal{D})^{\perp}=\frac{1}{d}\kappa_{d}^{*}\left(\xi^{\flat}\right)^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X)).

Consequently, by our choice of the maps
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
, the following conditions are equivalent for every
(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
:

•

(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\in d\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,

•

\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}f_{i}\in d\pi^{1}(X)
,

•

\xi^{\flat}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\in d\pi^{1}(X)
,

•

(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\in(1/d)\kappa_{d}^{*}(\xi^{\flat})^{-1}(d\pi^{1}(X))
,

•

(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\in F(\mathcal{D})^{\perp}
.

This shows that
F(\mathcal{D})^{\perp}=d\mathbb{Z}^{n}=((\mathbb{Z}_{d})^{n})^{\perp}
and so
F(\mathcal{D})=(\mathbb{Z}_{d})^{n}
.

Now we show that condition (1) holds. So let
K\mathrel{\lhd}\mathbb{T}^{n}
be a finite group with the largest torsion coefficient dividing
d
. There is an epimorphism
p\colon\mathbb{T}^{n}\to\mathbb{T}^{n}
with
K=p((\mathbb{Z}_{d})^{n})
. Set
\mathcal{C}=p\mathcal{D}
. Then
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{n})
and
F(\mathcal{C})=pF(\mathcal{D})=p((\mathbb{Z}_{d})^{n})=K
, as was to be shown.
∎

Proposition 5.45
.

Let
\mathop{\rm\mathcal{F}\colon\Gamma\curvearrowright X}
be a minimal flow with
\Gamma\in\mathsf{LieGp}
simply connected and with
X
compact second countable. Assume that
\pi^{1}(X)\setminus p\pi^{1}(X)\neq\emptyset
(that is, the group
\pi^{1}(X)
is not
p
-divisible) for infinitely many prime numbers
p
. Then for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
there is a sequence
(\mathcal{C}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}^{td}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{m})
, which converges u.c.s. to a minimal extension
\mathcal{C}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathbb{T}^{m})
.

Remark 5.46
.

Notice the following facts.

•

In Theorem
4.39
(2) in Chapter
4
we obtained a direct sum

\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)=\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}^{td}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)\oplus\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}^{cn}_{\mathcal{F}}}(G)

for every connected group
G\in\mathsf{CAGp}
and in Remark
4.40
we claimed that the splitting is not topological in general. Proposition
5.45
justifies this claim for many flows
\mathcal{F}
in the base and for the fibre groups
G=\mathbb{T}^{m}
(
m\in\mathbb{N}
).

•

In the proof of Proposition
5.45
we shall use some of our earlier results from Section
3.3
. In order for these results to be applicable, we shall need a sequence
(\mathcal{D}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of extensions in
\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
with the groups
F(\mathcal{D}_{n})
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
) non-trivial and mutually group-disjoint. Such a sequence does not exist if only finitely many prime numbers
p
satisfy
\pi^{1}(X)\setminus p\pi^{1}(X)\neq\emptyset
; this follows from Proposition
5.47
below.

Proof of Proposition
5.45
.

In the whole proof we shall regard
\pi^{1}(X)
as a subgroup of
C_{z}(X,\mathbb{T}^{1})
. Let
p
be a prime number and
f\in\pi^{1}(X)\setminus p\pi^{1}(X)
. By virtue of Theorem
3.38
,
\mathop{\rm co}(f)\in\mathop{\rm{\bf B}_{\mathcal{F}}}
lifts across
\kappa_{p^{l}}\colon\mathbb{T}^{1}\to\mathbb{T}^{1}
to an extension
\mathcal{D}^{(p)}_{l}\in\mathop{\rm{\bf Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
for every
l\in\mathbb{N}
.

1st step.
We show that
F(\mathcal{D}^{(p)}_{l})=\mathbb{Z}_{p^{l}}
for all
p
and
l
as above.

Under the usual identifications
\pi^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1})\cong(\mathbb{T}^{1})^{*}\cong\mathbb{Z}
, the morphism
f^{\flat}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\pi^{1}(X)
acts by the rule
f^{\flat}(k)=kf
and the morphism
\kappa_{p^{l}}^{*}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
acts by the rule
\kappa_{p^{l}}^{*}(k)=p^{l}k
. We claim that
